Discarding Lavrentiev’s Gap in Non-autonomous and Non-Convex Variational Problems
Michał Borowski, Pierre Bousquet, Iwona Chlebicka, Benjamin Lledos, Błażej Miasojedow

Abstract
We establish that the Lavrentiev gap between Sobolev and Lipschitz maps does not occur for a scalar variational problem of the form: to minimize u ↦ ∫ Ω f ⁡ ( x , u , ∇ u ) ​ 𝑑 x , \textrm{to minimize}\qquad u\mapsto\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,, under a Dirichlet boundary condition. Here, Ω \Omega is a bounded Lipschitz open set in ℝ N {\mathbb{R}^{N}} , N ≥ 1 N\geq 1 and the function f f is required to be measurable with respect to the spatial variable, continuous with respect to the second one, and convex with respect to the last variable. Under these assumptions alone, Lavrentiev gaps may occur, as illustrated by classical examples in the literature. We identify an additional natural condition on f f to discard such phenomena, that can be interpreted as a balance between the variations with respect to the first variable and the growth with respect to the last one. This unifies most of the structural assumptions that have been introduced so far to prevent the occurence of Lavrentiev gaps. Remarkably, typical assumptions that are usually imposed on f f in this setting are dropped here: we do not require f f to be bounded or convex with respect to the second variable, nor impose any condition of Δ 2 \Delta_{2} -kind with respect to the last variable.



1
Introduction

We are interested in excluding the Lavrentiev phenomenon, which occurs when the infimum of a variational problem over a family of regular functions is strictly greater than the infimum taken over all functions satisfying the same boundary conditions. Understanding the phenomenon in the multidimensional calculus of variations is of significant importance due to its implications not only in mathematical analysis but also in various applied fields, such as physics, engineering, and materials science, where optimization problems frequently arise, cf.
[
6
,
8
,
22
,
20
,
28
,
19
,
47
]
and references therein. By its very nature, excluding the Lavrentiev phenomenon is closely related to the approximation theory
[
3
,
11
,
23
,
27
,
36
]
. Moreover, it is intrinsically connected to the regularity theory in the Calculus of Variations. Indeed, for a Sobolev minimizer to be Lipschitz continuous, the absence of the Lavrentiev phenomenon is clearly a necessary condition. It turns out that this is also a sufficient condition for the regularity of minimizers for a large family of functionals
[
4
,
32
,
25
,
24
,
40
,
39
,
10
]
.

Lavrentiev
[
44
]
presented the first example of a one-dimensional variational problem for which the infimum on the set of absolutely continuous functions differs from the infimum on the set of Lipschitz functions. This example was later simplified by Manià
[
46
]
, who considered the following problem:

I_{M}:u\in W^{1,1}(0,1)\mapsto\int_{0}^{1}(u(x)^{3}-x)^{2}u^{\prime}(x)^{6}\,{\,{\rm d}}x,

where the admissible functions
u
are subject to the boundary conditions
u(0)=0
and
u(1)=1
. Then, one has

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in W^{1,1}(0,1),\\
u(0)=0,u(1)=1\end{subarray}}I_{M}(u)<\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in W^{1,\infty}(0,1),\\
u(0)=0,u(1)=1\end{subarray}}I_{M}(u)\,.

(1.1)

This example demonstrates that the Lavrentiev phenomenon is not related to the smoothness of the integrand, as it can occur even when the latter is a polynomial. The example also shows that convexity with respect to the last variable does not discard the Lavrentiev phenomenon. In fact, it persists for modified versions of the above problem where the integrand is uniformly convex in the last variable
[
9
]
.
One may wonder whether an even more elementary example exists, where the Lagrangian would depend only on
(x,u^{\prime})
or
(u,u^{\prime})
. In the one-dimensional case, the answer is negative, see
[
44
,
1
,
21
]
. The three variables
(x,u,u^{\prime})
have to be summoned for the Lavrentiev phenomenon to occur.

In the higher-dimensional case, we study the Lavrentiev gap between Sobolev and Lipschitz maps for a general scalar functional of the form

E_{\Omega}:u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)\mapsto\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,,

(1.2)

where
\Omega
is a bounded Lipschitz open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
,
N\geq 1
.
Our standing assumptions require that
f
be a nonnegative Carathéodory map which is convex with respect to the last variable. For integrands
f
depending just on
(x,\nabla u)
or just on
(u,\nabla u)
, one may be tempted to conjecture for the case
N\geq 2
the non-occurrence of the Lavrentiev phenomenon established when
N=1
. It turns out that the situation is much more complex in the higher-dimensional setting. For autonomous Lagrangians, namely those which do not depend on the spatial variable
x
, this conjecture is correct. There is no Lavrentiev phenomenon in that case, as in the one-dimensional situation
[
16
]
. On the contrary, when
f
depends just on
(x,\nabla u)
, Lavrentiev phenomena have been detected for many functionals
[
49
,
32
,
7
,
13
]
.

Let us conjure the functional which has attracted notable attention in the past few years
[
12
,
10
,
25
,
24
,
13
,
18
]
, the double phase functional:

u\mapsto\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}+a(x)|\nabla u|^{q}\,{\,{\rm d}}x\,,

(1.3)

where
1<p<q
and
a
is a nonnegative and bounded function. A considerable amount of work has been achieved to obtain sharp conditions on
p,q
and the behaviour of
a
that prevent the Lavrentiev phenomenon
[
32
,
7
,
13
,
12
,
18
]
. Significant effort has been devoted to extending these conditions to more general Lagrangians
f
, which still depend only on
x
and
\nabla u
. As many examples suggest, the harsh changes of growth of the Lagrangian
f(x,\nabla u)
with respect to
\nabla u
when
x
varies are responsible for the Lavrentiev phenomenon.
In the case of the double phase functional (
1.3
), the Lagrangian has a
p
growth (in terms of
\nabla u
) at the points
x
where
a
vanishes, whereas it has a
q
growth where
a
is strictly positive. If the variations of
a
are not suitably small, in terms of the difference
q-p
or the ratio
q/p
, then the Lavrentiev phenomenon may occur.
The competition of several regimes of
\nabla u
-growth associated with several subregions of
\Omega
is acknowledged as the only source of Lavrentiev phenomena. From a technical point of view, however, most of the articles discarding the Lavrentiev phenomenon have found it convenient to introduce additional growth restrictions in the
\nabla u
variable, such as the
\Delta_{2}
condition. For later use, let us define this condition for a general function
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
: There exists
C>0
such that for every
(x,t,\xi)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, one has

f(x,t,2\xi)\leq C(f(x,t,\xi)+1).

(1.4)

We then write
f(x,t,\cdot)\in\Delta_{2}
.
Polynomials do satisfy this assumption, which, on the contrary, fail for integrands involving the exponential map. In this paper, our main result does not require such growth conditions.

For more general Lagrangians where the
u
dependence comes into play, it is
a priori
unclear whether this competition between different
\nabla u
-growth regimes is solely responsible for the Lavrentiev phenomenon. Let us recall that in the one-dimensional case, the Lavrentiev phenomenon can occur only when
f
depends on the three variables, emphasizing the role of
u
in producing the gaps between the two infima, compare (
1.1
). This suggests that in the higher dimensional case, new phenomena could arise when passing from Lagrangians depending just on
(x,\nabla u)
to Lagrangians fully depending on the three variables
(x,u,\nabla u)
.

The main goal of this article is to address this possibility. Roughly speaking, our main results state that the spatial variations of the growth with respect to
u

and
with respect to
\nabla u
should now be taken into account. This does not mean, however, that those variables
u
and
\nabla u
have to be considered on an equal footing, for otherwise unnecessary restrictions would be artificially introduced on the
u
-dependence. Without entering into too many details at this stage, let us mention two important aspects:

1.

The summability of
W^{1,p}
functions is (much) higher than the summability of their gradients, as a consequence of the Sobolev embeddings. It is thus expectable that the conditions involving the summability exponents on
u
and
\nabla u
should not be the same.

2.

More importantly,
f
is not assumed to be convex with respect to
(u,\nabla u)
but just with respect to
\nabla u
.

Regarding the second point, we stress the fact that the convexity assumption just with respect to
\nabla u
is the natural condition to impose in the general framework of the direct method of existence in the Calculus of Variations. Indeed, this assumption is essentially equivalent to the weak lower semicontinuity of the functional. In contrast, requiring the convexity of
f
jointly with respect to
u
and
\nabla u
is unnecessarily restrictive in this regard.

In fact, the study of the Lavrentiev phenomenon can be placed within a broader framework.
The weak sequential lower semicontinuity of the functional
E_{\Omega}
is equivalent to the identity

E_{\Omega}(u)=\inf\left\{\liminf_{n\to+\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n}):(u_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset W^{1,1}(\Omega),u_{n}\rightharpoonup_{W^{1,1}}u\right\}.

The corresponding relaxed functional on
W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
is instead defined as

E^{\textrm{rel}}_{\Omega}(u):=\inf\left\{\liminf_{n\to+\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n}):(u_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset W^{1,\infty}(\Omega),u_{n}\rightharpoonup_{W^{1,1}}u\right\}.

The problem of the integral representation of
E^{\textrm{rel}}_{\Omega}(u)
amounts to the existence of some function
g:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

E^{\textrm{rel}}_{\Omega}(u)=\int_{\Omega}g(x,u(x),\nabla u(x))\,dx.

(1.5)

One may expect that
g=f
under suitable conditions on
f
. As a consequence of our main results, we can assert that the assumptions on
f
which discard the Lavrentiev phenomenon are sufficient to establish (
1.5
) with
g=f
. Actually, we have an even stronger conclusion: there is no
Lavrentiev gap
, in the sense that
for every
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
which strongly converges to
u
in
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
and such that
\lim_{n\to+\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)
. Moreover, we can ensure that each
u_{n}
agrees with
u
on
\partial\Omega
(provided of course that the trace of
u
on
\partial\Omega
is Lipschitz continuous). It is expected, as the absence of the Lavrentiev phenomenon is typically inferred by excluding the Lavrentiev gap, see
[
3
,
23
,
18
,
11
,
14
,
38
,
43
]
.

Main results.
In order to present our framework, let us settle some more notation. We consider the space
W^{1,1}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
of those functions
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
which coincide with
{\varphi}
on
\partial\Omega
and the
subset
W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
of those Lipschitz continuous functions
u
on
\Omega
which agree with
{\varphi}
on
\partial\Omega
.
Let us define

{{E}}(\Omega)=\left\{u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)\colon\ E_{\Omega}(u)<\infty\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)=\left\{u\in W^{1,1}_{{\varphi}}(\Omega)\colon\ E_{\Omega}(u)<\infty\right\}\,.

(1.6)

Our main results state the absence of the Lavrentiev gap between Lipschitz functions and the energy space of the functional
E_{\Omega}
, defined in (
1.2
), under a Dirichlet boundary condition formulated in terms of a Lipschitz function
{\varphi}
.
As already mentioned, this requires a condition on
f
which controls the growth in the last two variables
u
and
\nabla u
when the spatial variable
x
varies. Since this condition, in its more general unifying form (see (

H
conv

) below), looks quite uninterpretable at first glance, we prefer to begin with some specific situations where our main result can be formulated in more friendly-looking terms. Let us emphasize, however, that the following statements are already interesting in their own right: they extend and simplify many related theorems on the non-occurrence of the Lavrentiev gap.

In the first simplified situation that we address, we assume that
f
is isotropic, i.e.,
f(x,t,\xi)=f(x,t,|\xi|)
, where
|\xi|
is the Euclidean norm of
\xi
. In that case, the condition connecting the
(t,\xi)
-growth of
f
with the spatial variable
x
requires that:

For every
L=(L_{1},L_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
, there exists a constant
C_{L}>0
such that for a.e.
x,y\in\Omega
and every
(t,\xi)\in[-L_{1},L_{1}]\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
, it holds

|x-y|<{\varepsilon}\ \text{ and }\ |\xi|\leq L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-\min\big(1,\frac{N}{p}\big)}\Longrightarrow f(x,t,|\xi|)\leq C_{L}\Big(f(y,t,|\xi|)+1\Big)\,.

(
H^{\rm iso}_{0}
)

Hence, if
x
and
y
are not too far apart and
\xi
is not too large, then
f(x,t,\xi)
and
f(y,t,\xi)
are comparable. Observe that the distance between
x
and
y
on the one hand, and the Euclidean norm of
\xi
on the other hand, are controlled with the same parameter
{\varepsilon}
. In particular, we do not require that the right-hand side holds for any
\xi
, but just for those which are suitably small with respect to
|x-y|
. To the best of our knowledge, this type of condition was first introduced by Zhikov in his seminal paper
[
49
]
on the double phase problem, compare (
1.3
).
It should be emphasized that the second variable
t
and the third variable
\xi
are not on equal terms in (

H
iso

0

). More specifically, once
L
is fixed, the variable
t
is confined to a bounded interval, and the large values of
t
are thus ignored, whatever the choice of
{\varepsilon}
may be. For isotropic Lagrangians, (

H
iso

0

) is enough to discard any Lavrentiev gap. This is the content of the following statement, which can be deduced from our main result Theorem
3
:

Theorem 1
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded Lipschitz open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
,
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz continuous,
p\geq 1
, and
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{[0,\infty)}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function, which is convex with respect to the last variable and satisfies
f(x,t,0)=0
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Suppose that (

H
iso

0

) holds.
Consider the functional
E_{\Omega}
defined in (
1.2
). Then, for every
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
as
n\to\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)\,.

We stress that the above result directly generalises
[
18
, Theorem 2.3]
, since it includes a non-trivial
u
-dependence of the integrand, which is possibly unbounded and non-convex. Moreover, there is no need here for the integrand to satisfy the
\Delta_{2}
-condition nor to have super-linear growth in the last variable (nor to grow faster than a fixed power function). Consequently, we retrieve classical results for variable exponents and double phase functionals
[
32
,
49
]
, as well as new ones for the latter
[
13
]
. We present novel examples of functionals to which Theorem
1
applies in Section
2
. We also refer to Theorem
5
below for the extended isotropic and orthotropic version of this result.

The formulation of (

H
iso

0

) can be simplified by introducing the following notation:
For a measurable function
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a given ball
B\subset{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
intersecting
\Omega
, we denote

f_{B}^{-}(t,\xi)\coloneqq\essinf_{x\in B\cap\Omega}f(x,t,\xi)\,.

(1.7)

Then, (

H
iso

0

) reads

|\xi|\leq L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-\min\big(1,\frac{N}{p}\big)}\Longrightarrow f(x,t,|\xi|)\leq C_{L}\Big(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)+1\Big)\,.

The isotropic structure of the functionals considered in Theorem
1
can be relaxed to the fully anisotropic one, i.e., when
f
depends on the last variable not necessarily via its length. We present here a simple, fully anisotropic consequence of our main result, Theorem
3
. The new condition on
f
balancing the
(t,\xi)
-growth in terms of the spatial variations now reads:

For every
L=(L_{1},L_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
, there exists a constant
C_{L}>0
such that for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
(t,\xi)\in[-L_{1},L_{1}]\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
, it holds

f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)\leq{L_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}\Longrightarrow f(x,t,\xi)\leq C_{L}\Big(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)+1\Big)\,.

(
H_{\Delta_{2}}
)

Observe that (

H

Δ
2

) differs from (

H
iso

0

) only through the way that the length of
t
and
\xi
are measured. While in (

H
iso

0

), the quantities
f(x,t,\xi)
and
f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)
are comparable when the norm of
\xi
alone is not too large, in (

H

Δ
2

) instead, this holds under a suitable control on
f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)
. As illustrated by many examples in Section
2
, the latter condition often yields sharper results, even for isotropic functionals, provided that the
\Delta_{2}
condition (
1.4
) is satisfied.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose
\Omega
is a bounded Lipschitz open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
and
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is Lipschitz continuous. Let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function which is convex with respect to the last variable, which satisfies
f(x,t,0)=0
and
f(x,t,\cdot)\in\Delta_{2}
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Suppose that (

H

Δ
2

) holds.
Consider the functional
E_{\Omega}
defined in (
1.2
). Then, for every
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
as
n\to\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)\,.

We shall stress that already this special case is new, as it allows for an essentially non-convex structure of
f
. Indeed, the dependence of
f
on the second variable is just continuous, but not bounded nor convex as e.g. in
[
11
,
14
,
17
]
. This makes the proof a non-trivial task, which cannot be solved by merely varying previous reasonings. Moreover, let us emphasize the following facts.

1.

The above statements are interesting even for
N=1
as the continuity of
f
with respect to
x
is not required.

2.

The assumption
f(x,t,0)=0
is given here for the clarity of the statement and might be relaxed, see the growth condition (
1.8
) below, which controls even possibly unbounded behaviour of
f
in the origin.

3.

A bunch of examples to the theorem mentioned above, covering classical functionals as well as new ones, are provided in Section
2.2
. For a quick summary see Table
1
and Table
2
.

In our main result, Theorem
3
, we introduce a fully anisotropic condition (

H
conv

) related to (

H
iso

0

) and (

H

Δ
2

). Still, unlike them, it involves a balance between
f
and the greatest convex minorant of
f_{B}^{-}
in the
\xi
-variable. More specifically, given an open ball
B
intersecting
\Omega
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote by
\left(f^{-}_{B}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}
the supremum of all those convex functions
g:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
which lie below
f^{-}_{B}(t,\cdot)
on
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
(since
f
and thus also
f^{-}_{B}(t,\cdot)
are nonnegative, this supremum is well-defined). We are now in a position to define the main assumption involved in the forthcoming Theorem
3
:

Let
p\geq 1
. We assume that for every
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
, there exists a constant

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}>0
such that for a.e.
x\in{\Omega}
and all
(t,\xi)\in(-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1})\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
, it holds

{\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}\left(\xi\right)+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}\leq{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad f\left(x,t,\xi\right)\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}\left[\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}\left(\xi\right)+1\right]\,.

(
H^{\textrm{conv}}
)

The following remarks are in order.

1.

The condition (

H
conv

) is reminiscent of many articles in the field, compare
[
37
,
41
,
23
]
, and more recently
[
14
,
17
,
43
]
. Although not necessarily intuitive at first, it is quite handy and allows us to handle many situations. Observe in particular that the length of
\xi
is now quantified both in terms of the Euclidean norm of
\xi
and the function
\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}\left(\xi\right)
. As a consequence, we will prove that (

H
conv

) implies (

H
iso

0

) (for isotropic Lagrangians) and (

H

Δ
2

) (for
\Delta_{2}
Lagrangians).

2.

For every
{\varepsilon}\geq\diam\Omega
, for every
(x,t,\xi)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, one has
f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)=f_{B(x,\diam\Omega)}^{-}(t,\xi)
and thus

\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)=\left(f_{B(x,\diam\Omega)}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)\,.

Consequently, in the assumption (

H
conv

), the parameter
\varepsilon>0
can be restricted to the interval
(0,\diam\Omega]
.

3.

The parameter
p
plays a role when
p>N
, in which case (

H
conv

) allows excluding the Lavrentiev gap on the entire energy space of
E_{\Omega}
if the latter is included in
W^{1,p}{(\Omega)}
, see Theorem
3
.

4.

A function
f
of the form
f(x,t,\xi)=g(x,t,\xi)+h(x,t,\xi)
satisfies (

H
conv

) if both
g
and
h
do. This follows from the fact that for every ball
B
, one has
f_{B}^{-}\geq g_{B}^{-}+h_{B}^{-}
and thus for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the function
(g_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}+(h_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}
is a convex minorant of
f_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot)
, so we can infer that
(g_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}+(h_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}\leq(f_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}
and the conclusion easily follows. In particular, this observation can be useful for multi-phase functions of the form
f(x,t,\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\tfrac{1}{k}\psi_{0}(t,\xi)+a_{i}(x,t)\psi_{i}(t,\xi)\right)
. In this case, when
k>2
, the function
f_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot)
is not convex in general and the explicit expression of its greatest convex minorant is out of reach. It is much easier to check whether each term of the sum
{\vartheta}_{i}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq\frac{1}{k}\psi_{0}(t,\xi)+a_{i}(x,t)\psi_{i}(t,\xi)
satisfies (

H
conv

), since each
({\vartheta}_{i})_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot)
is convex.

5.

Section
2.2
presents many illustrative special cases of
f
satisfying (

H
conv

). A representative choice of examples is summarized in Tables
1
and
2
.

Our main result asserts that under (

H
conv

), no Lavrentiev gap may occur:

Theorem 3
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded Lipschitz open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
,
N\geq 1
,
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz continuous,
p\geq 1
, and
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function which is convex with respect to the last variable and satisfies (

H
conv

). When
N>1
, we further assume that the behaviour of
f
in the origin is constrained, namely that there exists
{\vartheta}\in[1,\infty]
,
a\in L^{{\vartheta}}(\Omega)
and
t_{0}>0
which satisfy

0\leq f(x,t,0)\leq a(x)|t|^{p^{*}/{\vartheta}^{\prime}}\qquad\text{for }\ |t|\geq t_{0}\textrm{ and for a.e. }x\in\Omega\,.

(1.8)

Consider the functional
E_{\Omega}
defined in (
1.2
). Then, for every
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
as
n\to\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)\,.

Here,
p*
is defined as
Np/(N-p)
if
p<N
while
p^{*}
is any number larger or equal to
N
otherwise.

Remark 1.1
.

The above theorem includes a condition on the behaviour of
f
when
\xi=0
, namely (
1.8
). It is only used to approximate any Sobolev map
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)
by a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset L^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
in norm and energy.
When we assume a priori that
u
is bounded, we do not need this assumption to conclude.
In particular, when
N=1
,
u
is automatically bounded and condition (
1.8
) can be dropped.

Roughly speaking, the conclusion of Theorem
3
is stable with respect to certain variations of the assumptions:

Remark 1.2
.

If
f
is not convex with respect to the last variable, but comparable to a convex function, one can still infer the absence of the Lavrentiev gap. More precisely – if for a Carathéodory function
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
, there exist a function
g:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
3
, a constant
C>0
, and a non-negative function
\vartheta\in L^{1}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}})
such that

{\tfrac{1}{C}g(x,t,\xi)-\vartheta(x)\leq f(x,t,\xi)\leq Cg(x,t,\xi)+\vartheta(x)\,,}

(1.9)

then by Proposition
A.1
, the conclusion of Theorem
3
holds for
f
.

It is fair to say that the direct verification of the conditions in Theorem
3
may be difficult in practice, due to the presence of the greatest convex minorant. One may wonder whether the conclusion of Theorem
3
remains true when

\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}
in (

H
conv

) is replaced by
f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)
. Let us restrict for an instant to the isotropic situation when
f
depends on
\xi
only through its Euclidean norm and consider the following condition:

For every
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
, there exists a constant

{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}>0
such that for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and all
(t,\xi)\in(-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1})\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
, it holds

{f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,|\xi|)+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}\leq{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad f\left(x,t,|\xi|\right)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}\left[f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,|\xi|)+1\right]\,.

(
H^{\textrm{iso}}
)

This condition is significantly more intuitive and easier to verify than (

H
conv

), while it substantially generalizes (

H
iso

0

) in Theorem
1
.
Since
f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,|\xi|)\geq\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(|\xi|)
, the assumption (

H
conv

) implies (

H
iso

).
Generally speaking, however, the converse implication is not true, compare Example
B.1
below. But under an isotropic or orthotropic regime, those two conditions are equivalent. This is the content of our next result.

Theorem 4
.

Let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,\infty)
be a Carathéodory function, which is convex with respect to the last variable. Then, the following assertions are true.

1.

If
f
is isotropic and satisfies (

H
iso

), then it satisfies (

H
conv

).

2.

If
f
admits an orthotropic decomposition, i.e.
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
is such that

f(x,t,\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(x,t,|\xi_{i}|)\,,\quad\text{where $\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N})$}\,,

(1.10)

and for every
i=1,\dots,N\,,
the function
f_{i}:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{[0,\infty)}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a Carathéodory function which is convex with respect to the last variable, and satisfies (

H
iso

), then
f
satisfies (

H
conv

).

The above statement
can be seen as a counterpart of
[
41
, Theorem 1.2]
for functions having isotropic or orthotropic structure. In both cases, it is shown that a balance between
f
and
f^{-}_{B}
on sub-level sets of
f^{-}_{B}
given by (

H
iso

) implies a similar balance between
f
and
(f^{-}_{B})^{**}
as in (

H
conv

).
The main difference between Theorem
4
and
[
41
, Theorem 1.2]
is the position of the constant (we have it outside
f_{B}^{-}
and
(f_{B}^{-})^{**}
, respectively). Our proof is based on essentially different geometrical observations. We point out that the result above also allows reformulating
[
17
, Hypothesis (H)]
or
[
43
, Assumption 1 (a4)]
in the isotropic or orthotropic setting by eliminating the
**
operator.

As a simple consequence of Theorem
3
and Theorem
4
, we derive the non-occurence of Lavrentiev gaps for isotropic and orthotropic functionals:

Theorem 5
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded Lipschitz open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
,
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz continuous,
p\geq 1
,
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function which is convex with respect to the last variable and which satisfies (
1.8
). Consider the functional
E_{\Omega}
defined in (
1.2
).

We assume either that
f
is isotropic and satisfies (

H
iso

), or that
f
admits an orthotropic decomposition in the sense of (
1.10
), with each
f_{i}
satisfying (

H
iso

).
Then, for every
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
as
n\to\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)\,.

Methods.
The Lagrangian
f
that we consider in (
1.2
) depends on three variables: the spatial variable
x\in\Omega
, the second variable
t
corresponding to the values of
u(x)
, and the gradient variable
\xi
representing the values of
\nabla u(x)
. It is known that the Lavrentiev gap does not arise when
f
is autonomous; that is, when
f
depends only on
t
and
\xi
, see
[
16
]
.
In contrast, the
x
-dependence usually produces Lavrentiev gaps, except when
f
depends just on
x
and
\xi
and satisfies a balance (or anti-jump) condition involving the spatial oscillations and the growth in the gradient variable. In that case, one approximates any given map
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
by a family
(u_{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
obtained by convolution of
u
with a smooth kernel
{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}
(here we ignore the boundary condition for the moment). In order to prove the approximation in energy, namely that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}E_{\Omega}(u_{\varepsilon})=E_{\Omega}(u)
, one first observes that
the liminf inequality
:
\liminf_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}E_{\Omega}(u*{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\geq E_{\Omega}(u)
easily follows from the Fatou lemma.
The heart of the matter is
the limsup inequality
:
\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}E_{\Omega}(u*{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\leq E_{\Omega}(u)
. At this stage, one needs to exploit an anti-jump condition ((

H
conv

) for instance) which implies that:
f(x,\nabla u_{\varepsilon})\lesssim\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}\right)^{**}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})
, on the range where
\nabla u_{\varepsilon}
is controlled, up to innocuous additive terms. Then, one applies the Jensen inequality to
\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}\right)^{**}
to get

f(x,\nabla u_{\varepsilon})\lesssim\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}\right)^{**}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}\right)^{**}(\nabla u(x-y)){\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y){\,{\rm d}y}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}f(x-y,\nabla u(x-y)){\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y){\,{\rm d}y}\,,

where the last inequality follows from the definition of
\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}\right)^{**}
. We thus obtain that the functions
f(x,\nabla u_{\varepsilon})
are dominated (still up to multiplicative constants and harmless additive terms) by

f(x,\nabla u)*{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
. Since
\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}f(x,\nabla u)*{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}=f(x,\nabla u)
in
L^{1}
, the generalized dominated convergence theorem allows to conclude that

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}E_{\Omega}(u_{\varepsilon})=E_{\Omega}(u)
.
The above calculation illustrates that the anti-jump condition is perfectly suited for approximation by convolution with a smooth kernel.
As a matter of fact, one could even have the impression that it has been formulated precisely to make this interplay with the Jensen inequality work (for Lagrangians depending just on
x
and
\xi
). But even so, the anti-jump condition is simultaneously very efficient to discard the Lavrentiev gaps under (almost) sharp conditions on the parameters involved in the definition of
f
(see
[
32
]
, that exploits this fact for the exponents
p
and
q
and the Hölder exponent of the coefficient
a
in the double phase functional (
1.3
), and
[
7
,
13
,
12
]
for generalized versions).

The above approximation by mere convolution does not take into account the boundary condition, namely the fact that the approximating sequence should agree with
u
on
\partial\Omega
. We will revisit this issue subsequently. We note here that for Lagrangians depending only on
x
and
\nabla u
, this may lead to very intricate difficulties and subtle arguments to overcome them; see, e.g.,
[
23
,
42
]
.

We emphasize that this classical approximation of a Sobolev map
u
by convolution with a smooth kernel is doomed to fail for a Lagrangian having an essential and non-convex dependence with respect to
t
. The obstacle is basically that the Jensen inequality, which plays so important a role in
the limsup inequality
, requires a convexity assumption. However, in our situation, the Lagrangian
f
is assumed to be convex just with respect to
\xi
, and not jointly convex with respect to
(t,\xi)
. Hence, even when
f
does not depend on
x
, it is not possible to justify that
f(u_{\varepsilon},\nabla u_{\varepsilon})\lesssim f(u,\nabla u)*{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
.
To overcome this difficulty, as in
[
16
]
, we use the classical formulation of non-parametric variational problems in terms of parametric ones. The most prominent example of such a formulation is the area functional
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}
which can be expressed as the perimeter of the subgraphs of the competing functions
u
; that is,

\int_{\Omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}|D1_{u}|(t,x)\,,

(1.11)

where
|D1_{u}|
is the total variation of the (finite) measure
D1_{u}
and
1_{u}
is the indicator function of the hypograph of
u
, namely
1_{u}(x,t)=1
when
t\leq u(x)
and
1_{u}(x,t)=0
otherwise, see (
5.1
). The right-hand side of (
1.11
) is the perimeter of the subgraph of
u
in the sense of Cacciopoli sets.
More generally, starting from an integrand
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
, we can associate to the corresponding energy
E_{\Omega}
its convex counterpart
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
which is another energy that acts on the subgraphs of the functions
u
.
To be more specific, while the original energy
E_{\Omega}
is defined on
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, the new energy
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
is defined on the set of those maps
v\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
such that their distributional derivatives
Dv
are finite
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measures. The two energies are related by the formula

\forall u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)\qquad E_{\Omega}(u)=\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{u})\,.

This generalizes to any function
f
the classical formulation (
1.11
) of the non-parametric minimum area problem via the minimization of the perimeter. The new energy
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
involves a Lagrangian
\widehat{f}:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}\to[0,\infty]
:

\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v)=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)\,.

This Lagrangian
\widehat{f}
is homogeneous of degree
1
and convex in the last variable, which entitles one to define
{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)
as a measure whenever
Dv
is a measure, see (
5.9
). In the case of (
1.11
), the Lagrangian
\widehat{f}
is simply the Euclidean norm in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
.

In this new formulation, the
t
variable from the original functional becomes an additional spatial variable in the new functional. The fact that the parametric formulation is a way to convexify the original one has already been exploited in the setting of
\Gamma
-convergence for
BV
-functions, see
[
27
]
, and for the formulation of necessary conditions, see
[
15
]
. The key consequence for us is that the non-convex behaviour of
f
with respect to
t
becomes harmless and the convexity with respect to
\xi
is enough to ensure the convexity of
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
.

Hence, the approximate problem is transferred to this new functional
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
, except that the function
v
to be approximated is not a Sobolev map anymore. Moreover, for some reasons that will be clarified subsequently, the desired regularity of the approximating maps
v_{n}
is not the Lipschitz continuity but a
cone
condition of the following form: there exists
C_{n}>0
such that for every
x,y\in\Omega
and for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

v_{n}(x,t+C_{n}|x-y|)\leq v_{n}(y,t)\,.

(1.12)

If
v_{n}
were the indicator function of the subgraph of a certain function
u_{n}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
, this condition would mean that the graph of
u_{n}
lies below a family of cones with bounded apertures, which in turn is equivalent to the Lipschitz continuity of
u_{n}
. To construct such a sequence
v_{n}
, we introduce two scales of parameters
{\varepsilon}
and
\delta
and define

v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)\coloneqq v*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)+\delta\alpha(t)\,,

where
*_{x}
refers to
partial
convolution just with respect to
x
, while
\alpha
is a decreasing function depending just on
t
, see the very beginning of Section
5.3
. This second term is crucial to force
t\mapsto v_{n}(x,t)
to decrease uniformly with respect to
x
, which enables us to obtain a bound
C_{n}
as in (
1.12
).

For every sequence
({\varepsilon}_{n})_{n}
and
(\delta_{n})_{n}
decreasing to
0
, the resulting maps
v_{n}\coloneqq v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}
converge a.e. to
v
. Moreover,
the liminf inequality
, namely
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v_{n})\geq\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v)
, is an easy consequence of the Reshetnyak semicontinuity theorem for functionals of measures, see Proposition
6.2
.
Up to this point, all our arguments closely follow those developed in
[
16
]
.
The key challenge in reaching the conclusion lies in establishing
the limsup inequality
of Proposition
7.1
reading

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v_{n})\leq\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v)\,.

(1.13)

When
f
depends only on
(u,\nabla u)
, and assuming further that
f(u,0)=0
(which is not really restrictive),
the limsup inequality
can be easily derived from a Jensen-type inequality for measures:

\widehat{f}(t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\widehat{f}(t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\,,

(1.14)

see Remark
7.4
below.
One derives therefrom the integral inequality

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\,,

(1.15)

from which
the limsup inequality
(
1.13
) easily follows.

In our situation where
f
depends in an essential way on the three variables, the anti-jump condition on
f
should play a role in the proof of
the limsup inequality
(
1.13
), since otherwise the Lavrentiev phenomenon would never occur. As already explained, the anti-jump condition is perfectly adapted to the Jensen inequality when
f
only depends on
x
and
\xi
and when
u
is approximated by convolution with a smooth kernel. In contrast, it is not obvious how such an anti-jump condition should be exploited for the new Lagrangian
\widehat{f}
and the new approximating sequence
v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}
, which is now defined from
u
in a more intricate way. In particular, the inequality (
1.14
) is markedly false when
f
depends on the spatial variable
x
(since again no Lavrentiev gap would occur otherwise).

In order to take into account the spatial dependence of the Lagrangian
f
, we need to introduce a new strategy to prove
the limsup inequality
.
The first task to establish (
1.13
) under the condition (

H
conv

) is an effective transfer of the anti-jump behaviour exhibited by the integrand
f
into pertinent information for the integrand
\widehat{f}
of the convexified energy
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
. In particular, we present in Section
7.1
estimates for the functions
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
which make it possible to exploit the anti-jump condition satisfied by
\widehat{f}
.
This entitles us to get a suitable bound on the measure
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})
, see Lemma
7.7
. Up to an additive term (depending on
\delta
) that is not difficult to handle, the corresponding estimate reads: for every Borel subset
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq C\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\left(\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\right)\,.

(1.16)

In contrast to (
1.15
), there is a multiplicative constant
C
in the right-hand side (that is not necessarily equal to
1
). So even if we were able to pass to the limit
({\varepsilon},\delta)\to(0,0)
, we would get (with
A^{\prime}=\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
):

\limsup_{({\varepsilon},\delta)\to(0,0)}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq C\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\,.

(1.17)

One would then fail to obtain the expected
limsup inequality
.

A possible way to overcome this obstacle is to rely on another naive attempt to prove (
1.13
), namely applying the
continuity Reshetnyak theorem
(Lemma
6.1
) to the functional
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
, so as to get the identity:

\lim_{({\varepsilon},\delta)\to(0,0)}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)\,,

which is exactly the desired conclusion.
However, this theorem requires among other assumptions that
\widehat{f}(x,t,\cdot)
is continuous on the unit sphere of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
. This certainly holds if
f
satisfies a linear growth condition, but fails to be true for a superlinear
f
, since in that case
\widehat{f}(x,t,(q^{x},q^{t}))=+\infty
for every
(q^{x},q^{t})\in{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{[0,\infty)}
. Introducing the Lebesgue-Radon-Nikodym derivative
(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{x},q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t})
of
Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
with respect to its total variation
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
, the measure
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})
is simply defined as:

\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\widehat{f}(x,t,(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{x},q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}))|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\,.

It follows from the definition of
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
that
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}<0
but
\widehat{f}(x,t,(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{x},q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}))
tends to
+\infty
when
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}
approaches
0
, which happens, roughly speaking, when
\nabla u
is very large. As a consequence, the Reschetnyak continuity theorem seems to be of no use in our framework.

So far, we have presented two possible ways to establish
the limsup inequality
. They both fail for different reasons: the estimate (
1.17
) is an unsatisfactory limsup inequality, due to the nasty multiplicative constant
C
, while the Reshetnyak continuity theorem only applies on subdomains of
\Omega
where the function
q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\vcentcolon=\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}/|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
is bounded from above by a negative constant. It turns out that by suitably intertwining these two failing strategies, one can produce a successful one.

Roughly speaking, one introduces a new parameter
k\geq 1
, and one splits
\Omega
into a good region and a bad region as follows:

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\int_{\{q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\leq-\frac{1}{k}\}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})+\int_{\{q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}>-\frac{1}{k}\}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\,.

For every fixed
k
, one can apply the Reshetnyak theorem to the first term, since
\widehat{f}
is bounded in the good region where
q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\leq-1/k
. The second term is more delicate to handle because the parameter
{\varepsilon}
arises in two different positions, namely through the two functions
q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
and
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
. In a nutshell, one first estimates the integrand by relying on the anti-jump condition obtained for
\widehat{f}
as in (
1.16
). Up to multiplicative constants and additive terms, this leads to

\int_{\{q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}>-\frac{1}{k}\}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\lesssim\int_{\{q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}>-\frac{1}{k}\}}{\,{\rm d}}\left(\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,.

In order to pass to the limit when
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, we have to understand how
q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
behaves when
{\varepsilon}
varies. This requires a careful use of some classical ingredients from geometric measure theory, such as the coarea and the area formula, as well as the approximate tangent spaces to the level sets of
u
. The fact that the bad region
\{q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}>-\frac{1}{k}\}
has a small measure is crucial to conclude. At a technical level, this entitles one to get rid of all the undesirable multiplicative constants when
k\to+\infty
.

Once
the limsup inequality
(
1.13
) is proved, we reach a safe and familiar shore. We
can conclude with the equality
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v_{n})=\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v)
(Corollary
8.1
). It remains to derive from this approximating sequence for
v
an approximating sequence for
u
. This step of the proof is similar to the corresponding one in
[
16
]
: one can select some
s\in(0,1)
and define functions
u_{n}
on
\Omega
such that
1_{u_{n}}
coincides with the indicator function of the super level set
\{(x,t):\ v_{n}(x,t)>s\}
. The convergence of
v_{n}
to
1_{u}
easily implies the convergence of
u_{n}
to
u
. That each
u_{n}
is Lipschitz continuous is a consequence of the condition (
1.12
) satisfied by the maps
v_{n}
.
All those arguments are provided in the proof of Proposition
8.6
.

However, this is not the end of the story, since the maps
u_{n}
that we have just obtained do not necessarily agree with
\varphi
on
\partial\Omega
. A non-trivial task is to modify
u_{n}
to get a new map
u_{n}^{{\varphi}}
that satisfies this boundary condition. A usual construction to achieve this goal is based on a partition of unity argument. Due to the absence of any growth condition of
f
with respect to
\xi
, however, this approach does not work directly in our situation. Instead, we rely again on the convexified energy
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
to localize the problem and reduce to the case when
\Omega
is the epigraph of a Lipschitz function. However, even in this setting, the strategy employed in
[
16
]
, based on local translations, cannot be repeated due to the
x
-dependence. We rely on a different approach and modify the construction of the maps
(u_{n})_{n}
described above by using the trick of decentered convolution, that is, the smooth kernel
{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
is not taken radially symmetric but decentered with respect to the origin. This entitles us to exploit the regularity of
\varphi
and get first an approximating sequence which is uniformly close (but not necessarily equal) to the map
\varphi
on a neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
. A final truncation argument with a cut-off function then yields an approximating sequence that agrees with
\varphi
on the boundary.

The above strategy works well under the anti-jump condition (

H
conv

), which is the most general when compared to (

H

Δ
2

) or (

H
iso

).
The equivalence between (

H
conv

) and (

H

Δ
2

) when
f
satisfies a further
\Delta_{2}
condition follows from a remarkable result by Hästö
[
41
]
. Our Theorem
4
provides a similar conclusion for the equivalence between (

H
conv

) and (

H
iso

). This may seem counterintuitive, in view of the fact that the functions
f_{B}^{-}(t,\xi)
and
\left(f_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)
are not comparable, even in the isotropic setting where
f
, and thus also
f_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot)
and
\left(f_{B}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\cdot)
only depend on the Euclidean norm of
\xi
.
Theorem
4
is also reminiscent of a similar result
[
18
, Lemma 6.2]
which is stated and proved for superlinear functions
f
satisfying a suitable growth condition for large
|\xi|
’s and not depending on the variable
t
. The major difference is the way we measure the length of
\xi
, which in Theorem
4

involves both
|\xi|
and the function
f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,|\xi|)
(and not just
|\xi|
).
Our proof is also substantially different, even if it relies as in
[
18
]
on several properties of the convex envelopes of one-variable functions, see Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
4.2
.

Organization of the paper.
In Section
2
we illustrate by numerous examples the above results on the absence of the Lavrentiev phenomenon. Preliminary information is presented in Section
3
. Section
4
translates the consequences of the main structural and anti-jump conditions to conditions satisfied on sub-level sets of the greatest convex minorant of the infimum of
f
over small ball, namely
(f^{-}_{B})^{**}
, see Theorem
4
. Section
5
introduces the construction of the initial approximation
(v_{{\varepsilon},\delta})
. The next two sections contain the main ingredients for the convergence of convexified energies of the initial approximate sequence, i.e., ‘the liminf estimate’ for initial approximation is given in Section
6
and ‘the limsup estimate’ can be found in Section
7
. In Section
8
, under reduced assumptions on
f
, we establish the inner approximation (ignoring the boundary condition) in Proposition
8.6
and next we consider the boundary approximation, first for special Lipschitz domains in Section
8.2
and then for any Lipschitz sets in Section
8.3
. The final proof of Theorem
3
is presented in Section
9.1
while the proofs of Theorems
2
,
5
are presented in Section
9.2
.

2
On classical and new functionals

2.1
One-dimensional examples of functionals exhibiting the Lavrentiev gap.

We aim at showing why certain integrands known to be associated with some functionals with the Lavrentiev gap, do not fall into our regime.
One-dimensional problems are particularly illustrative due to their simplicity and ease of visualization. Their analysis dates back to classical papers of Lavrentiev
[
44
]
and Mania
[
46
]
, providing the first examples of occurrence and conditions for the non-occurrence of the Lavrentiev gap. Concerning later contributions, we spotlight
[
20
]
, where the relaxation of functionals is considered.
See also the survey
[
19
]
and the recent expository paper
[
21
]
.

Starting with recalling probably the best known example of a functional with the Lavrentiev gap, we point out that the integrand does not satisfy any of our anti-jump conditions.

Example 2.1

(Mania’s example
[
46
]
)
.

If
f:(0,1)\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as
f(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq(t^{3}-x)^{2}\xi^{6}
, then we have, for boundary conditions
u(0)=0
and
u(1)=1
, that
\inf_{AC}{{E}}=0<\inf_{Lip}{{E}}<\infty
.
However,
f_{B(1,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(1,{\varepsilon}^{-1})=0
and
f(1-{\varepsilon},1,{\varepsilon}^{-1})={\varepsilon}^{-4}
, which tends to infinity as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. Hence, the smooth function
f
does not satisfy (

H
conv

) nor (

H
iso

).

The next example illustrates that superlinearity of
f
is not sufficient to exclude the Lavrentiev phenomenon.

Example 2.2

(Ball & Mizel’s example
[
9
]
)
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be given by
f(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq(x^{4}-t^{6})^{2}|\xi|^{27}+\mathcal{v}|\xi|^{2}
with
\mathcal{v}>0
. We have
f^{-}_{B(0,{\varepsilon})}(0,{\varepsilon}^{-1/2})=\mathcal{v}{\varepsilon}^{-1}
, while
f({\varepsilon},0,{\varepsilon}^{-1/2})={\varepsilon}^{-11/2}+\mathcal{v}{\varepsilon}^{-1}
, which divided by
\mathcal{v}{\varepsilon}^{-1}
grows to infinity with
{\varepsilon}
converging to
0
. Therefore,
f
does not satisfy (

H
conv

) nor (

H
iso

).
It is known that for the boundary conditions
u(-1)=k_{1},u(1)=k_{2}
with
-1\leq k_{1}<0<k_{2}\leq 1
, and for
\mathcal{v}
sufficiently small, we have
\inf_{AC}{{E}}=0<\inf_{Lip}{{E}}<\infty
.

One strength of the new results proved in the paper is the fact that we do not require continuity with respect to the first variable. However, we point out that the continuity in the second variable is needed.

Example 2.3

(Cerf & Mariconda’s example
[
21
]
)
.

If
f:(0,1)\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as
f(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq\left(\xi-\tfrac{1}{2t}\right)^{2}
when
t\not=0
and
f(x,0,\xi)=0
, then
\inf_{AC}{{E}}=0
and for every Lipschitz function
u
that is not identically
0
,
{{E}}(u)=+\infty
. Note that
f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)=f(x,t,\xi)
, so
f
does satisfy the balance condition (

H
conv

), but it is not continuous with respect to
t
.

2.2
Multidimensional examples

In this section, we give examples of functionals

E_{\Omega}(u)=\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,,

(2.1)

for which there is no Lavrentiev phenomenon according to Theorems
2
,
3
, and
5
. For a quick summary, see Tables
1
and
2
. We denote the integrand in (
2.1
) as
f=f(x,t,\xi)
, where
x
stands for the spacial variable, the variable
t
corresponds to the
u
-dependence of
f
, and the variable
\xi
to its
\nabla u
-dependence. Let us emphasize that all examples with explicit
u
-dependence are novel, with the only remark that for
f=f(t,\xi)
they follow from
[
16
]
and for
f=f(x,t,\xi)
with
(t,\xi)\mapsto f(x,t,\xi)
being convex they are embraced by
[
17
]
. In this section we will use the following notation: for any real-valued function
w
and a constant
C\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we write
C\ll w
whenever there exists a constant
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
C<c\leq w(\cdot)
.

Examples built upon power models.
The typical examples of functionals exhibiting the Lavrentiev phenomenon are those of the variable exponent and double phase growth, i.e., when the integrand in (
2.1
) is given by
\widetilde{f}_{\text{v}}(x,\xi)\coloneqq|\xi|^{\widetilde{r}(x)}
and
\widetilde{f}_{\text{d}}(x,\xi)\coloneqq|\xi|^{p}+\widetilde{a}(x)|\xi|^{q}
, respectively. To exclude the Lavrentiev phenomenon for the functional involving
\widetilde{f}_{\text{v}}
, one usually requires that
1\ll\widetilde{r}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
is log-Hölder continuous (
\widetilde{r}\in\mathcal{P}^{\text{log}}
), cf.
[
49
]
. For the functional defined by
\widetilde{f}_{\text{d}}
, the typical assumptions are
1<p<q
and
0\leq\widetilde{a}\in C^{0,{\varkappa}}
with
q\leq p+{\varkappa}\max(1,p/N)
, see
[
49
,
32
,
18
]
. The latter was recently improved in
[
13
]
, allowing for
p
and
q
arbitrary far from each other, by considering in the place of
C^{0,{\varkappa}}
a broader class of weights
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\varkappa}}
, where
{\varkappa}\in(0,\infty)
dictates a polynomial rate of vanishing decay of
\widetilde{a}
. We stress that upon these choices of parameters, the functions
\widetilde{f}_{\text{v}}
and
\widetilde{f}_{\text{d}}
satisfy condition (

H
iso

0

) and the corresponding energy functionals are thus covered by Theorem
1
.

Let us prepare some notation before presenting their
u
-dependent counterparts falling into the same realm. With some abuse of typical notation, we say that a continuous function
r:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to[1,\infty)
belongs to
\mathcal{P}^{\text{log}}(\Omega)
, if there exists a locally bounded function
C:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,\infty)
, such that

|r(x,t)-r(y,t)|\leq-\frac{C(t)}{\log(|x-y|)}\qquad\text{for all }\ x,y\in\Omega\,,t\in{\mathbb{R}}\,.

(2.2)

We need also to introduce a class of functions generalizing
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\varkappa}
. Let us take a function
\omega:{[0,\infty)}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\omega(0,t)=0
and
\omega(\cdot,t)
is non-decreasing for every
t
. We say that a function
a:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,\infty)
belongs to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}(\Omega{\times{\mathbb{R}}})
if
t\mapsto a(x,t)
is continuous for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and there exists a locally bounded function
C:{\mathbb{R}}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that

a(x,t)\leq C(t)\big(a(y,t)+\omega(|x-y|,t)\big)\qquad\text{for all }\ x,y\in\Omega\,,t\in{\mathbb{R}}\,.

(2.3)

Due to the function
t\mapsto C(t)
and the lack of concavity assumption on
\omega(\cdot,t)
, the condition (
2.3
) does not describe the modulus of continuity of
a
with respect to
x
. In the special case of
\omega(s,t)=s^{\varkappa}
, the sub-class of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
is
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\varkappa}}
, which is meaningful for all
{\varkappa}\in(0,\infty)
. For more information about the class
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\varkappa}}
in the case when
a
does not depend on
t
we refer to
[
13
]
.

We are now in a position to consider

f_{\text{v}}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq|\xi|^{r(x,t)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad f_{\text{d}}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq|\xi|^{p}+a(x,t)|\xi|^{q}\,.

Let us show that
f_{\text{v}}
verifies (

H
iso

0

) with
1=p\leq r(\cdot,\cdot)
if
r\in\mathcal{P}^{\log}
. Given
L_{2}\geq 1
and
{\varepsilon}<\exp(-1)
, let us take
|\xi|\leq L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}
. For every
x,y\in\Omega
with
|x-y|<{\varepsilon}
, if
|\xi|\geq 1
, then

f_{\text{v}}(x,t,\xi)=|\xi|^{r(y,t)}|\xi|^{r(x,t)-r(y,t)}\leq f_{\text{v}}(y,t,\xi)\cdot(L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1})^{-C(t)/\log({\varepsilon})}\leq L_{2}^{C(t)}\exp({C(t)})f_{\text{v}}(y,t,\xi)\,,

If instead
|\xi|\leq 1
, we simply observe that
f_{\text{v}}(x,t,\xi)\leq 1
.
Hence (

H
iso

0

) is proven. Additionally,
f_{\text{v}}(x,t,0)=0
, so by Theorem
1
, the Lavrentiev gap for the corresponding functional is excluded. We stress that the only property of
t\mapsto r(x,t)
that is needed, besides (
2.2
), is the continuity of this mapping.

Let us show that
f_{\text{d}}
satisfies (

H
iso

0

) if
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\varkappa}}
with
{p\leq q}\leq p+{\varkappa}\max(1,\tfrac{p}{N})
. If
|\xi|\leq L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-\min(1,{N}/{p})}
with
\xi\not=0
, we have for every
x,y\in\Omega
with
|x-y|<{\varepsilon}
,

\frac{f_{\text{d}}(x,t,\xi)}{f_{\text{d}}(y,t,\xi)}=\frac{1+a(x,t)|\xi|^{q-p}}{1+a(y,t)|\xi|^{q-p}}\leq 1+C(t)+C(t)|x-y|^{{\varkappa}}|\xi|^{q-p}\leq 1+C(t)+C(t)L_{2}^{q-p}\,,

which means that (

H
iso

0

) holds true. Given that
f_{\text{d}}(x,t,0)=0
, Theorem
1
implies the absence of the Lavrentiev phenomenon in this case. Again, we stress that the only property of
t\mapsto a(x,t)
that is needed, besides (
2.3
), is the continuity of this mapping. Moreover, with similar computations as for
f_{\text{d}}
, one can check that the function
(x,t,\xi)\mapsto|\xi|^{p(t)}+a(x,t)|\xi|^{q(t)}
satisfies (

H
iso

) if
1\leq p(\cdot),q(\cdot)\in C({\mathbb{R}})
,
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
for
\omega(s,t)=s^{{\varkappa}(t)}
where
{\varkappa}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{[0,\infty)}
, and
q(t)\leq p(t)+{\varkappa}(t)\max(1,\tfrac{p(t)}{N})
for every
t
. Moreover, for every example given above, we can find conditions to exclude the Lavrentiev phenomenon for its orthotropic counterpart also including more phases. For instance, Theorem
5
applies to a multi-phase orthotropic function

f(x,t,\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(|\xi_{i}|^{p_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{i,j}(x,t)|\xi_{i}|^{q_{i,j}}\right)\quad\text{with $a_{i,j}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\varkappa}_{i,j}}$, ${\varkappa}_{i,j}>0$, and $q_{i,j}\leq p_{i}+{\varkappa}_{i,j}\max(1,\tfrac{p_{i}}{N})$}\,.

We observe that already mentioned examples embrace the best known conditions without
u
-dependence of the functional, cf.
[
32
,
14
,
29
,
7
,
13
,
49
,
18
]
. At the same time, introducing this extra dependence does not complicate the conditions in an artificial way.

f(x,t,\xi)

,

for which (

) is satisfied and so for which we provide the absence of any Lavrentiev phenomenon by Theorem

. We write
f=f(x,t,\xi)
, where

stands for the spacial variable, variable

corresponds to

-dependence of

, and variable
\xi
to its
\nabla u
-dependence. In all examples in the table,
b\in C({\mathbb{R}},(0,+\infty))
, which might be relaxed due to Remark
2.1
. The class
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
from (

) embraces (but is not restricted to) Hölder continuity;
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\varkappa}

Generalized Orlicz examples.
In order to include isotropic functionals of essentially non-power growth, we need to study when they verify (

H
iso

). The simplest example under no growth restriction is constructed by the use of an increasing, non-negative convex function
\psi
and reads

f_{\text{e}}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq\psi(|\xi|)+a(x,t)\psi^{\gamma}(|\xi|)\quad\text{with }\ \gamma>1\,.

In this case, to satisfy (

H
iso

) one can require that
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{N(\gamma-1)}
. Indeed, note that whenever
(f_{\text{e}})^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\xi)\leq L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}
with
\xi\not=0
, then
0<|\xi|\leq\psi^{-1}(L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N})
. Therefore, for any
y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{f_{\text{e}}(x,t,\xi)}{f_{\text{e}}(y,t,\xi)}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C(t)+C(t)|x-y|^{N(\gamma-1)}\psi^{\gamma-1}(\psi^{-1}(L_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N})))\leq 1+C(t)+C(t)L_{2}^{\gamma-1}\,.

If
\psi
is such that
\psi(0)=0
, or
a\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
, we can deduce the absence of any Lavrentiev gap from Theorem
5
. This example can be applied to various functions
\psi
, including slowly growing ones, e.g.
\psi(s)=s
or
\psi(s)=s\log(1+\log(\dots(1+\log(1+s))))
, as well as fastly growing ones, e.g. when for all sufficiently large
s
it holds
\psi(s)=\exp(s)
or
\psi(s)=\exp(\exp(\dots\exp(s)))
. We stress that condition
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{N(\gamma-1)}
is meaningful for arbitrary
\gamma>1
and the only property of
t\mapsto a(x,t)
, that is needed, is the continuity of this mapping.

To construct a more general example, we pick two increasing, non-negative, convex functions
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
on
{[0,\infty)}
such that the function
\psi_{1}/\psi_{0}
is non-decreasing. Upon setting

f_{\text{o}}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq\psi_{0}(|\xi|)+a(x,t)\psi_{1}(|\xi|)\,,

one can prove that
f_{\text{o}}
satisfies condition (

H
iso

), if
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
and

\forall\,{L>0}\quad\exists\,{c>0}\quad\forall\,{s>0}\quad\omega(s)\leq c\max\left(\frac{s^{-N}}{\psi_{1}(\psi_{0}^{-1}(Ls^{-N}))},\frac{\psi_{0}(L^{1/N}s^{-1})}{\psi_{1}(L^{1/N}s^{-1})}\right)\,,

(2.4)

Let us point out that the assumption that
a\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
is imposed in order to guarantee (
1.8
), and is satisfied whenever
a
does not depend on
t
.
We refer to Example
B.2
for details. The condition (
2.4
) specializes to the given above conditions for functions
f_{\text{v}}
,
f_{\text{d}}
, and
f_{\text{e}}
. We note that it has also a simpler form under additional compatibility conditions on
\psi_{0},\psi_{1}
. Namely

1.

if
\psi_{1}/\psi_{0}\in\Delta_{2}
, then (

H
iso

) is satisfied with
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
for
\omega(s)\leq\frac{\psi_{0}(s^{-1})}{\psi_{1}(s^{-1})}
;

2.

if
\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{0}^{-1}\in\Delta_{2}
, then (

H
iso

) is satisfied with
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
for
\omega(s)\leq\frac{s^{-N}}{\psi_{1}(\psi_{0}^{-1}(s^{-N}))}
.

Inspired by
[
43
, Example 2.7 (2)]
let us consider

f_{\text{D}}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq|\xi|^{p}+a(x,t)\exp(|\xi|^{q})\,,

where
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
for
\omega(s)=\exp(-s^{-{\varkappa}})
. In
[
43
, Example 2.7 (2)]
the authors allow the parameter
q
to be strictly less than
1
so that
f_{\text{D}}
is not convex in that case.
We demand – via (
2.4
) – that
{\varkappa}>q\min(1,\tfrac{N}{p})
, which means that
p\geq 1
and
q>0
might be arbitrary if the function
a
decays fast enough near the points where it vanishes. Moreover, unlike
[
43
]
, for the absence of the Lavrentiev gap obtained via Theorem
3
, we allow for functionals with explicit
u
-dependence (i.e.
a=a(x,t)
), see Example
B.3
for details.

f(x,t,\xi)

\forall i\quad 1\leq p_{i}\in{\cal P}^{\log}
,

,

or
\big(q_{i}\leq p_{i}

Table 2:
Main examples of anisotropic integrands for functionals
\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}

, where

stands for the spacial variable, variable
t
corresponds to
u
-dependence of
f
, and variable
\xi
to its
\nabla u
-dependence. In all examples in the table,
b\in C({\mathbb{R}},(0,+\infty))
, which might be relaxed due to Remark
2.1
. The class
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
from (
2.3
) embraces (but is not restricted to) Hölder continuity;
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\varkappa}
,
{\varkappa}>0
is its special case. *Assumption that certain functions are
N
-function is given for the simplicity of the exposition.

Fully anisotropic examples.
Inspired by
[
4
,
30
,
5
,
29
]
we present an anisotropic Orlicz multi-phase example. Let us consider radially increasing, convex functions
(\psi_{j})_{j=0}^{k}
, such that each
\psi_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq k
grows essentially faster than
\psi_{0}
at infinity:

f(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq\psi_{0}(\xi)+\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}(x,t)\psi_{j}(\xi)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(\tfrac{1}{k}\psi_{0}(\xi)+a_{j}(x,t)\psi_{j}(\xi)\right)=:\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\vartheta}_{j}(x,t,\xi)\,.

As already observed in item
(iii)
before Theorem
3
, to justify that this function satisfies (

H
conv

), it is enough that this fact holds for each
{\vartheta}_{j}
.
Assuming further that the functions
\psi_{j}
are isotropic, and relying on Theorem
4
below, we only need to require that
condition (

H
iso

) is satisfied for every
{\vartheta}_{j}
which is the case when
a_{j}\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega_{j}(\cdot,\cdot)}
for
\omega_{j}
satisfying (
2.4
) with
\psi_{j}
in the place of
\psi_{1}
. This condition applied to isotropic Lagrangian fully covers the scope of
[
4
, Theorem 3.1]
and extends it in three directions. We allow for explicit
u
-dependence of the considered functional and do not need to assume that
\psi_{j}\in\Delta_{2}
for any
j
. Moreover, in
[
4
]
the imposed compatibility condition forces the closeness of phases expressed as
\limsup_{|\xi|\to\infty}\frac{\psi_{j}(x,|\xi|)}{|\xi|\psi_{0}(x,|\xi|)}<\infty
for every
j
. Upon the condition above, the function
\omega_{j}
does not play a role of the modulus of continuity, because we do not require its concavity, so no closeness of phases is needed.

Let us present another fully anisotropic example using the fact that the function
|\langle x,\xi\rangle|
does not admit an orthotropic decompostion. We can consider Lagrangians of the form

f_{\text{a}}(x,\xi)\coloneqq\psi(|\langle{\upsilon}(x),\xi\rangle|)+|\xi|^{N/\gamma}\,,

where
\psi\in\Delta_{2}
is an increasing, convex function,
{\upsilon}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
is in
C^{0,\gamma}
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
. Then, the integrand
f_{\text{a}}
satisfies assumptions of Theorem
2
, see Example
B.4
for details.

Remark 2.1
.

In every above example,
we can freely multiply the Lagrangian
f
by an extra
t
-dependent continuous function
b:{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,\infty)
still keeping the balance conditions satisfied. In the case when (

H
iso

0

) is satisfied, the function
b
could also vanish. A similar remark holds for a more restrictive variant of (

H
conv

) when the condition
\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}\left(\xi\right)+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}\leq{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}
is replaced by
\ |\xi|^{\max(p,N)}\leq{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}
. Then if
f
satisfies such a modified (

H
conv

), the same condition holds for
b(t)f(x,t,\xi)
and Theorem
3
can be applied to the latter.

3
Preliminaries

3.1
Notation

In the sequel
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
is a fixed bounded open set with Lipschitz boundary. Moreover,
\Lambda
denotes a bounded open set which contains the closure of
\Omega
. We also formulate some arguments with an arbitrary open set
\Lambda^{\prime}\Subset\Lambda
(the latter meaning that
\overline{\Lambda^{\prime}}\subset\Lambda
). Throughout the paper we assume that
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
or
f:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a Carathéodory function, i.e. it is measurable with respect to the first variable and continuous with respect to the second and the third variables. Moreover,
f
is always required to be convex with respect to the last variable. For a function
u
defined on
\Lambda
which shall be clear from the context, we will introduce two Borel subsets
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{0}
of
\Lambda
such that
\Lambda=\Lambda_{+}\cup\Lambda_{0}
and

|\nabla u|>0\textrm{ a.e. on }\Lambda_{+}\textrm{ and }|\nabla u|=0\textrm{ a.e. on }\Lambda_{0}\,.

(3.1)

For a set
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we denote by

C^{0}_{c}(U)
(resp.
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
) the set of continuous (resp. smooth) functions with compact support in
U
, while
W^{1,p}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
,
p\geq 1
stands for the set of weakly differentiable functions in
\Omega
with
p
-integrable weak gradients and trace
{\varphi}
on
\partial\Omega
. Finally,
W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
is the set of Lipschitz functions in
\Omega
agreeing with
{\varphi}
on
\partial\Omega
.

For every
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote by
r_{+}
the positive part of
r
, namely
r_{+}=\max(r,0)
.

The partial derivative of a Sobolev function
w:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with respect to a unit vector
e\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is denoted by
\partial_{e}w
. For a convex function
w:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, we denote by
D^{+}w(s)
,
s\geq 0
, the right-hand side derivative of
w
in point
s
.

For any measurable map
w:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we define its graph

{\mathrm{Graph}}_{w}\coloneqq\{(x,t)\in U\times{\mathbb{R}}:\ t=w(x)\}\,.

For a given Borel function
w:U\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, which is non-increasing and left-continuous with respect to the second variable, we define the
generalized inverse
with respect to the second variable by

w^{-1}(x,s)\coloneqq\inf\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:\ w(x,t)\leq s\}\in[-\infty,+\infty]\qquad\text{for every }\ x\in U\,.

(3.2)

In particular, if for a given
x\in U
, there is no
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
w(x,t)\leq s
, then
w^{-1}(x,s)=+\infty
. If instead,
w(x,t)\leq s
for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then
w(x,t)=-\infty
.

For a function
w:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
bounded from below by an affine function, we define its
greatest convex minorant

w^{**}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
as the supremum of all convex functions which are not larger than
w
in the whole
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. It can also be obtained by applying the Young conjugation operation
*
twice, see e.g.
[
23
, Corollary 2.1.42]
. Note however that this latter fact will not be used in the sequel.
We say that a function
w:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
superlinear
if

\lim_{|\xi|\to\infty}\frac{w(\xi)}{|\xi|}=\infty\,.

We say that
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to[0,\infty)
is superlinear if there exists a function
w:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
as above such that for a.e.
x\in\Omega
, for every
(t,\xi)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,

f(x,t,\xi)\geq w(\xi).

(3.3)

We say that a function
w:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is an
N
–function
if it is convex, continuous, and such that
w(0)=0
,
\lim_{t\to 0}{w(t)}/{t}=0
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}{w(t)}/{t}=\infty
.

We say that a Carathéodory function
w:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
satisfies the
\Delta_{2}
condition (denoted
w(x,t,\cdot)\in\Delta_{2}
) if there exists a constant
c>0
independent of
x
and
t
such that it holds

w(x,t,2\xi)\leq c(w(x,t,\xi)+1)\qquad\text{for a.e. $x\in\Omega$ and all }(t,\xi)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\,.

For a standard regularizing kernel
{\varrho}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B(0,1))
and
{\varepsilon}>0
we denote

{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)\coloneqq{\varepsilon}^{-N}{\varrho}(x/{\varepsilon})\,.

(3.4)

Note that
{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B(0,{\varepsilon}))
for every
{\varepsilon}>0
. Recall that given a Borel function
v:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the convolution with respect to
x
is defined as follows: for every
(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we set

v*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x,t)\coloneqq\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}v(x-y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y){\,{\rm d}y}\,.

For an
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued Borel measure
{\mathsf{v}}
on
\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we define the measure
{\mathsf{v}}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}
by setting for every Borel set
A^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
:

{\mathsf{v}}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(A^{\prime})\coloneqq\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}{\mathsf{v}}(x,t)\,,

(3.5)

where
\chi_{A^{\prime}}
denotes the indicator function of
A^{\prime}
.
Hence, for every bounded Borel function
h:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}h(x,t){\,{\rm d}}({\mathsf{v}}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}})(x,t)=\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}h(y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}{\mathsf{v}}(x,t)\,.

(3.6)

Another important tool is the disintegration of finite measures defined on
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
. To detail this technique, we closely follow
[
2
, Section 2.5]
. Let
\mu
be a positive Radon measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
and
(\nu_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
a family of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measures on
\Omega
such that the function
t\mapsto\nu_{t}(B)
is
\mu
-measurable for any Borel set
B\subset\Omega
.
We further assume that

t\mapsto|\nu_{t}|(\Omega)\textrm{ belongs to }L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)\,.

(3.7)

We denote by
\nu_{t}\otimes\mu
the
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measure on
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
defined for every Borel set
B\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
by

(\nu_{t}\otimes\mu)(B):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\chi_{B}(x,t){\,{\rm d}}\nu_{t}(x)\right){\,{\rm d}}\mu(t)\,.

(3.8)

It then follows that

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}g(x,t){\,{\rm d}}(\nu_{t}\otimes\mu)(x,t)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{\Omega}g(x,t){\,{\rm d}}\nu_{t}(x)\right){\,{\rm d}}\mu(t)\,,

(3.9)

for every bounded Borel map
g:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{[0,\infty)}
.
The above definitions also make sense when
\nu_{t}
is a positive finite measure. Then (
3.9
) holds true for any non-negative Borel function
g
.
Conversely, given a finite
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measure on
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, it can be written as a product of the form
\nu_{t}\otimes\mu
as above, where

\mu
is finite and
|\nu_{t}|(\Omega)=1
for
\mu
a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, see
[
2
, Theorem 2.28]
.

We will make use of a non-decreasing sequence of continuous decreasing functions
\theta_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
for
k\geq 1
such that
\theta_{k}
is supported in
(-\infty,-\frac{1}{k})
and for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
it holds

\lim_{k\to\infty}\theta_{k}(t)=\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(t)\,.

(3.10)

3.2
Precise representatives

In this paragraph, we closely follow
[
45
]
.
Given
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a set
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is said to be
countably
{{H}}^{q}
-rectifiable
if there exist a family
(E_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of subsets in
{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
and for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a Lipschitz map
f_{k}:E_{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that
{{H}}^{q}\big(E\backslash\bigcup\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}f_{k}(E_{k})\big)=0
.

If
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is an open set and
u\in L^{1}_{\textrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, then a representative
\widetilde{u}
of
u
is said to be a
precise representative
if

\widetilde{u}(x)\coloneqq\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{|B_{r}|}\int_{B(x,r)}u(y){\,{\rm d}y}

at all
x
where the limit exists. For every
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, a precise representative is unique up to a
{{H}}^{N-1}
-negligible set.

Proposition 3.1
.

[
45
, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]

Let
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
be precisely represented. Then, the level set
u^{-1}(t)
is countably
{{H}}^{N-1}
-rectifiable for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the graph
{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}=\{(x,u(x)):x\in\Omega\}
of
u
is countably
{{H}}^{N}
-rectifiable and moreover, for every measurable set
E\subset\Omega
, it holds

\int_{E}|\nabla u(x)|{\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{H}}^{N-1}(E\cap u^{-1}(t)){\,{\rm d}t}\,,

(3.11)

\int_{E}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u(x)|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}={{H}}^{N}({\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}\cap(E\times{\mathbb{R}}))\,.

(3.12)

From the
coarea formula
(
3.11
), we deduce by a standard argument that
for every non-negative Borel measurable function
g:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{[0,\infty)}
, we have

\int_{\Omega}g(x,u(x))|\nabla u(x)|{\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{u^{-1}(t)}g(y,t){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(y){\,{\rm d}t}.

(3.13)

Similarly, from the
area formula
(
3.12
), we have

\int_{\Omega}g(x,u(x))\sqrt{1+|\nabla u(x)|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}g(x,t){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}.

(3.14)

For every
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, it follows from the area formula (
3.12
) that if
u
is precisely represented, its graph map satisfies the Lusin condition, i.e., for every Lebesgue measurable set
E\subset\Omega
,

|E|=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{{H}}^{N}\left(\{(x,u(x)):x\in E\}\right)=0\,.

(3.15)

For every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
u^{-1}(t)
is countably
{{H}}^{N-1}
-rectifiable, for
{{H}}^{N-1}
a.e.
x\in u^{-1}(t)
, there exists an approximate tangent space
T_{x}u^{-1}(t)
, which means that

\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{r^{N-1}}\int_{u^{-1}(t)}\phi\left(\frac{y-x}{r}\right){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(y)=\int_{T_{x}u^{-1}(t)}\phi(y){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(y)\,,

(3.16)

for every
\phi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{N})
. For a proof of this result, holding for any countably rectifiable set, see e.g.
[
48
, Theorem 11.6]
.

4
Between balance conditions (

H
iso

) and (

H
conv

)

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4
. The reasoning is based on several geometrical observations of the properties of convex minorants, which we now present.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
{w}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be a continuous and non-decreasing function. For every
t\geq 0
, there exists
a_{t}\in[0,t]
such that
{w}^{**}
is affine on
[a_{t},t]
and
{w}(a_{t})={w}^{**}(a_{t})
.

Proof.

The result is clear if
w^{**}
is a constant function, as then
w^{**}
is constantly equal to
w(0)
. Note also that as
w
is non-decreasing, also
w^{**}
is non-decreasing. Additionally, by monotonicity of the derivative, if
w^{**}
is not constant, then it is unbounded. The result is also true if
w^{**}(t)=w(t)
, as then one can take
a_{t}=t
.

Let us then assume that
w^{**}(t)<w(t)
and that
w^{**}
is unbounded. We take
a_{t}\leq t
to be the minimal number such that
w^{**}
is affine on
[a_{t},t]
. It suffices to show that
w^{**}(a_{t})=w(a_{t})
. The result holds if
a_{t}=0
. Hence, let us assume that
a_{t}>0
.

Suppose by contradiction that
w^{**}(a_{t})<w(a_{t})
. Let us take any
s<a_{t}
such that
w^{**}(a_{t})<w(s)
. As
w^{**}
is unbounded, there exists
s_{2}>a_{t}
such that
w^{**}(s_{2})=w(s)
. Let us define

\widetilde{w}(\tau)=\begin{cases}w^{**}(\tau)&\text{ for $\tau\not\in[s,s_{2}]$,}\\
w^{**}(s)+(\tau-s)\frac{w^{**}(s_{2})-w^{**}(s)}{s_{2}-s}&\text{ for $\tau\in[s,s_{2}]$.}\end{cases}

Note that
w^{**}\leq\widetilde{w}
and
\widetilde{w}
is convex as the maximum of
w^{**}
and an affine function. It is also true that
\widetilde{w}
is a minorant of
w
. Indeed, if
\tau\not\in[s,s_{2}]
, then
\widetilde{w}(\tau)=w^{**}(\tau)\leq w(\tau)
. On the other hand, if
\tau\in[s,s_{2}]
, then

\widetilde{w}(\tau)\leq\widetilde{w}(s_{2})=w^{**}(s_{2})=w(s)\leq w(\tau)\,.

As
w^{**}\leq\widetilde{w}
, and
w^{**}
is the greatest convex minorant of
w
, we have
