w^{**}=\widetilde{w}
. Therefore,
w^{**}
is affine on
[s,s_{2}]
. This however means that
w^{**}
is affine on
[s,t]
, where
s<a_{t}
, which contradicts the definition of
a_{t}
. Therefore, we have that
w^{**}(a_{t})=w(a_{t})
.
∎

The following lemma gives a lower bound on the derivative of
{w}^{**}
whenever
{w}
is the essential infimum of convex functions.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
B
be a non-empty Borel subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and
\big\{{w}_{y}\big\}_{y\in B}
be a family of non-negative and non-decreasing convex functions on
{[0,\infty)}
. Let us denote
{w}\coloneqq\essinf_{y\in B}{w}_{y}
. Then for all
s\geq 0
, it holds that

D^{+}{w}^{**}(s)\geq\essinf_{y\in B}D^{+}{w}_{y}(s).

Proof.

Suppose the contrary, i.e. that
D^{+}{w}^{**}(s)<\essinf_{y\in B}D^{+}{w}_{y}(s)\eqqcolon\mathcal{d}_{s}
for some
s
. Let us define

\widetilde{w}(\tau)\coloneqq\begin{cases}{w}^{**}(\tau)&\text{ for $\tau\in[0,s]\,$,}\\
{w}^{**}(s)+\mathcal{d}_{s}(\tau-s)&\text{ for $\tau>s\,$.}\end{cases}

Observe that
\widetilde{w}
is convex by the monotonicity of its derivative. It is also clear that
\widetilde{w}(\tau)\leq{w}(\tau)
for
\tau\leq s
. Moreover, for
\tau>s
and a.e.
y\in B
, we have

\widetilde{w}(\tau)={w}^{**}(s)+\mathcal{d}_{s}(\tau-s)\leq{w}_{y}(s)+D^{+}{w}_{y}(s)(\tau-s)\leq{w}_{y}(\tau)\,.

As the last inequality is true for a.e.
y\in B
, we get
\widetilde{w}\leq{w}
. Therefore,
\widetilde{w}
is a convex minorant of
{w}
. However, as
\mathcal{d}_{s}>D^{+}{w}^{**}(s)
, we have
{w}^{**}(\tau)<\widetilde{w}(\tau)
for some
\tau>s
, which is a contradiction.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
4
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

We start with proving assertion
(i)
. Observe that we can assume that
f(x,t,0)=0
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Indeed, if it is not true, we can replace
f
by the function
g(x,t,\xi)=(f(x,t,\xi)-f(x,t,0))_{+}
. By Lemma
A.3
, the function
g
satisfies (

H
conv

) if and only
f
does, and the same is true for (

H
iso

). Therefore, proving that
g
satisfies (

H
conv

) yields the desired result.

In the statement of (

H
iso

), there is no loss of generality in replacing
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in{[0,\infty)}^{2}
by
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in({\mathbb{N}}_{*})^{2}
. Using that a countable union of negligible sets is negligible, we infer that there exists a negligible set in
\Omega
such that (

H
iso

) holds for every
x
in its complement and for every
\mathcal{k}\in{[0,\infty)}^{2}
.
Let us fix such an
x\in\Omega
and
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})
,
{\varepsilon}>0
,
t\in[-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1}]
. For a.e.
y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})\cap\Omega
, let us denote

{w}_{y}(s)\coloneqq f(y,t,s)\,,\quad{w}(s)\coloneqq f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,s)\,.

For a.e.
y
, since
{w}_{y}
is non-negative and vanishes at
0
, it achieves its global minimum in
0
. As
{w}_{y}
is convex, this implies that it is non-decreasing. Therefore,
{w}
is also non-decreasing as the essential infimum of non-decreasing functions. Consequently,
{w}^{**}
is non-decreasing as well. Let us also notice that for a.e.
y
and any
s\geq 0
, by convexity of
{w}_{y}
, it holds

D^{+}{w}_{y}(s)\leq{w}_{y}(s+1)-{w}_{y}(s)\leq{w}_{y}(s+1)\,,

which means that the family
\{{w}_{y}\}_{y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})\cap\Omega}
is uniformly locally Lipschitz, as by Lemma
A.2
, the function
f
is bounded on bounded sets. Therefore,
{w}
is continuous.

Let us now take
s\in{[0,\infty)}
such that
{w}^{**}(s)+s^{\max(p,N)}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}
. As
{w}
is non-decreasing and continuous, by Lemma
4.1
, there exists
a_{s}\in[0,s]
such that
{w}^{**}
is affine on
[a_{s},s]
and
w(a_{s})={w}^{**}(a_{s})
.
For every
y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})
, we have
B(x,{\varepsilon})\subset B(y,2{\varepsilon})
, and thus

f^{-}_{B(y,2{\varepsilon})}(t,a_{s})\leq f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,a_{s})=w(a_{s})=w^{**}(a_{s}).

(4.1)

Since
w^{**}
is non-decreasing, this implies that

f^{-}_{B(y,2{\varepsilon})}(t,a_{s})+a_{s}^{\max(p,N)}\leq w^{**}(s)+s^{\max(p,N)}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}=(2^{N}\mathcal{k}_{2})(2{\varepsilon})^{-N}\,.

By (

H
iso

), there exists
{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\geq 1
, with
\widehat{\mathcal{k}}\coloneqq(\mathcal{k}_{1},2^{N}\mathcal{k}_{2})
, such that for a.e.
y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})\cap\Omega
, it holds

{w}_{y}(a_{s})\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\left((f^{-}_{B(y,2{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot))^{**}(a_{s})+1\right)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\left({w}^{**}(a_{s})+1\right)\,,

(4.2)

where the last inequality follows from (
4.1
). In particular, the above inequality holds when
y=x
.
Assume that
a_{s}<s
and let
s^{\prime}\in(a_{s},s)
. Using that
{w}^{**}
is affine on
[a_{s},s]
together with (
4.2
), we get

{w}^{**}(s^{\prime})={w}^{**}(a_{s})+D^{+}{w}^{**}\left(s^{\prime}\right)(s^{\prime}-a_{s}){\geq}\tfrac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}}{w}_{y}(a_{s})-1+D^{+}{w}^{**}\left(s^{\prime}\right)(s^{\prime}-a_{s})\,.

Given
\delta>0
, it follows from Lemma
4.2
that for every
y
in a non-negligible subset of
B(x,{\varepsilon})\cap\Omega
, it holds

D^{+}{w}^{**}\left(s^{\prime}\right)\geq D^{+}{w}_{y}(s^{\prime})-\delta\,.

(4.3)

Using additionally that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\geq 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle{w}^{**}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}}{w}_{y}(a_{s})-1+D^{+}{w}_{y}(s^{\prime})(s^{\prime}-a_{s})-\delta(s^{\prime}-a_{s})

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}}\left({w}_{y}(a_{s})+D^{+}{w}_{y}(s^{\prime})(s^{\prime}-a_{s})\right)-1-\delta(s^{\prime}-a_{s})\,.

Then, by the monotonicity of
D^{+}{w}_{y}
and the fact that
{w}_{y}\geq{w}
for a.e.
y
, we have

\displaystyle{w}^{**}(s^{\prime}){\geq}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}}\left({w}_{y}(a_{s})+\int_{a_{s}}^{s^{\prime}}D^{+}{w}_{y}(\tau){\,{\rm d}}\tau\right)-1-\delta(s^{\prime}-a_{s})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}}{w}_{y}(s^{\prime})-1-\delta(s^{\prime}-a_{s})\overset{}{\geq}\tfrac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}}{w}(s^{\prime})-1-\delta(s^{\prime}-a_{s})\,.

Letting
\delta
to
0
and then
s^{\prime}
to
s
, we get
{w}^{**}(s)\geq\tfrac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}}{w}(s)-1
, or equivalently,

{w}(s)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\left({w}^{**}(s)+1\right)\,.

(4.4)

In particular,
{w}(s)+s^{\max(p,N)}\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}+{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
{\varepsilon}\leq\text{diam}(\Omega)
and that
\mathcal{k}_{2}
is sufficiently large to ensure that
\mathcal{k}_{2}\text{diam}(\Omega)^{-N}\geq 1
. Therefore, we have
{w}(s)+s^{\max(p,N)}\leq 2{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}
. Denoting
\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},2{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\mathcal{k}_{2})
, we get from (

H
iso

) that
{w}_{x}(s)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}\left({w}(s)+1\right)
, which by (
4.4
) yields

{w}_{x}(s)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}\left({\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\left({w}^{**}(s)+1\right)+1\right)\leq\left({\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}+{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}\right)\left({w}^{**}(s)+1\right)\,.

(4.5)

Using (
4.2
) in case of
a_{s}=s
, and (
4.5
) otherwise, we get

{w}^{**}(s)+s^{\max(p,N)}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}\Rightarrow{w}_{x}(s)\leq\left({\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}+{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}+{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\right)\left({w}^{**}(s)+1\right)\,,

which is (

H
conv

) for
f
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}=\left({\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}+{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}+{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{k}}}\right)
.

Let us now prove assertion
(ii)
. Let us fix
i\in\{1,2,\dots,N\}
. As
f_{i}
satisfies (

H
iso

), the function
(x,t,\xi)\mapsto f_{i}(x,t,|\xi|)
satisfies (

H
conv

). It easily follows that the function
(x,t,\xi)\mapsto f_{i}(x,t,|\xi_{i}|)
also satisfies (

H
conv

). Hence,
f
is a sum of functions satisfying (

H
conv

), which implies that
f
satisfies (

H
conv

).

∎

5
Preliminaries to the proof of Theorem
3

5.1
The function
1_{u}

In the following, we systematically choose precise representatives in
L^{1}(\Omega)
. For such a representative
u:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we define the map
v:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
by the following formula

v(x,t)\coloneqq{1}_{u}(x,t)=\begin{cases}1\,,&\textrm{ if }t\leq u(x)\,,\\
0\,,&\text{ otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(5.1)

In order to calculate the distributional derivative of
v
, we first establish the following technical fact.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
,
\Phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
,
\theta\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
, and
e\in\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
. Then

\int_{\Omega}\theta\circ u(x)\Phi(x,u(x))\partial_{e}u(x){\,{\rm d}x}=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left(\int_{[u\geq t]}\partial_{e}\Phi(x,t){\,{\rm d}x}\right){\,{\rm d}t}.

Here,
\partial_{e}u
is the directional derivative of
u
in the direction
e
, namely
\partial_{e}u=\langle\nabla u,e\rangle
.

Proof.

Let us define the Lipschitz continuous function
g:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
via

g(x,s)\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^{s}\Phi(x,t)\theta(t){\,{\rm d}t}\,.

Since
\Phi
is compactly supported in
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, there exists a compact subset
K\Subset\Omega
such that
g(x,s)=0
for every
(x,s)\in(\Omega\setminus K)\times{\mathbb{R}}
. By the chain rule, the function
G:x\mapsto g(x,u(x))
belongs to
W^{1,1}_{0}(\Omega)
and for a.e.
x\in\Omega
,

\partial_{e}G(x)=\partial_{e}g(x,u(x))+\partial_{s}g(x,u(x))\partial_{e}u(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{u(x)}\partial_{e}\Phi(x,t)\theta(t){\,{\rm d}t}+\Phi(x,u(x))\theta(u(x))\partial_{e}u(x)\,.

Integrating the above identity on
\Omega
, one gets

\int_{\Omega}\partial_{e}G(x){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{u(x)}\partial_{e}\Phi(x,t)\theta(t){\,{\rm d}t}\right){\,{\rm d}x}+\int_{\Omega}\Phi(x,u(x))\theta(u(x))\partial_{e}u(x){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

The left-hand side vanishes by the Stokes formula. In the right-hand side, we use the Fubini theorem to write the first integral as

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left(\int_{[u\geq t]}\partial_{e}\Phi(x,t){\,{\rm d}x}\right){\,{\rm d}t}\,.

This completes the proof.
∎

Using the above lemma, we can determine the distributional derivative of the function
v=1_{u}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
and
v=1_{u}
.
The distributional derivative
Dv
of
v
is the
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measure given by

Dv=\frac{(\nabla u,-1)}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}{{H}}^{N}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}\,.

(5.2)

Moreover, for every Borel set
A\subset\Omega
such that
|\nabla u(x)|>0
for a.e.
x\in A
, one has:

Dv\llcorner(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=\left(\chi_{A}\frac{(\nabla u,-1)}{|\nabla u|}{{H}}^{N-1}\llcorner u^{-1}(t)\right)\otimes{{H}}^{1}\,.

(5.3)

It follows from (
5.2
) that the total variation of
D1_{u}
is the measure
|D1_{u}|={{H}}^{N}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}
, the Radon–Nikodým derivative of
D1_{u}
with respect to its total variation is
\frac{(\nabla u,-1)}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}
.
The above statement is well-known, in particular in the setting of
BV
functions, see
[
34
, Theorem 4.1.5.2]
. For the convenience of the reader, we provide an elementary proof which does not rely on the theory of BV functions.

Proof of Lemma 5.2.

We have first to prove that for every
\phi=(\phi^{1},\dots,\phi^{N+1})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1})
it holds

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}1_{u}(x,t)\,\textrm{div}\,\phi(x,t){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=-\int_{{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi^{i}(x,t)\partial_{i}u(x)-\phi^{N+1}(x,t)\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u(x)|^{2}}}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N}(x,t)\,.

By applying the formula (
3.14
) to the right-hand side, this is equivalent to

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}1_{u}(x,t)\,\textrm{div}\,\phi(x,t){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=-\int_{\Omega}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi^{i}(x,u(x))\partial_{i}u(x)-\phi^{N+1}(x,u(x))\right){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(5.4)

For every
1\leq i\leq N
, we apply Lemma
5.1
with
\Phi\coloneqq\phi^{i}
,
e
being the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
vector of the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, and
\theta\equiv 1
. This gives

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{[u\geq t]}\partial_{i}\phi^{i}(x,t){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=-\int_{\Omega}\phi^{i}(x,u(x))\partial_{i}u(x){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

Summing over
i=1,\dots,N
, one gets

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}1_{u}(x,t)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}\phi^{i}(x,t){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=-\int_{\Omega}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi^{i}(x,u(x))\partial_{i}u(x){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(5.5)

For the last partial derivative
\partial_{t}\phi^{N+1}
, we simply use the Fubini theorem to write

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}1_{u}(x,t)\partial_{t}\phi^{N+1}(x,t){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{u(x)}\partial_{t}\phi^{N+1}(x,t){\,{\rm d}t}\right){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}\phi^{N+1}(x,u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(5.6)

Adding (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), we get the identity (
5.4
).

We proceed with the proof of (
5.3
). Let
A\subset\Omega
as in the statement and
g:A\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
a bounded Borel map.
By (
5.2
) and the area formula, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{A\times{\mathbb{R}}}g(x,t){\,{\rm d}}Dv(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}\cap(A\times{\mathbb{R}})}g(x,t)\frac{(\nabla u,-1)}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N}

\displaystyle=\int_{A}g(x,u(x))(\nabla u(x),-1){\,{\rm d}x}.

Applying next the coarea formula, this yields

\displaystyle\int_{A\times{\mathbb{R}}}g(x,t){\,{\rm d}}Dv(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{A\cap u^{-1}(t)}g(z,t)\frac{(\nabla u(z),-1)}{|\nabla u(z)|}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z){\,{\rm d}t}\,,

from which (
5.3
) follows.
∎

5.2
The convex extension
\widehat{E}
of the energy
E

Given a Carathéodory function
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
which is convex with respect to the last variable,
the energy
E_{\Omega}
(see (
1.2
)) does not need to be convex on
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
. Therefore, we associate to
E_{\Omega}
a
new energy
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
defined on the set of those functions
v\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
such that the distributional derivative
Dv
is equal to a finite
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measure.
The functional
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
is constructed in such a way that for every
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
,

E_{\Omega}(u)=\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{u})
.
As we shall see,
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
is convex, in contrast to
E_{\Omega}
.

In order to define
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
, we need to introduce the
\widehat{\ }
-operation, which goes back to
[
27
]
in the context of integral representation of
\Gamma
-limits of variational integrals.
For any non-negative convex function
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
, we define its recession function
h^{\infty}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to[0,\infty]
as

h^{\infty}(\xi)\coloneqq\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\tfrac{h(\lambda\xi)}{\lambda}\,.

Remember that
h^{\infty}
is a convex positively one-homogeneous function.
If one further assumes that
h
is superlinear, then
h^{\infty}(\xi)=\infty
except when
\xi=0
for which
h^{\infty}(0)=0
.

Using the notation
q=(q^{x},q^{t})
for every
q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}={\mathbb{R}}^{N}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote by
\widehat{h}:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,\infty]
the function

\widehat{h}(q)=\widehat{h}(q^{x},q^{t})\coloneqq\begin{cases}-q^{t}h(-\tfrac{q^{x}}{q^{t}})\,,&\text{if $q^{t}<0$}\,,\\
h^{\infty}(q^{x})\,,&\text{if $q^{t}=0$}\,,\\
+\infty\,,&\text{if $q^{t}>0$}\,.\end{cases}

(5.7)

It follows from the convexity of
h
that
\widehat{h}
is convex on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
and positively homogeneous of degree
1
, see e.g.
[
16
, Lemma 8.1]
. In particular, for
h\equiv{1}
, we get for every
q=(q^{x},q^{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\times{\mathbb{R}}
with
q^{t}\leq 0
,

\widehat{1}(q^{x},q^{t})=|q^{t}|\,.

(5.8)

Applying the operation
\ \widehat{}\
to
h=f(x,t,\cdot)
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we obtain a map
q\mapsto\widehat{f(x,t,\cdot)}(q)
. In order to simplify the notation, we write

\widehat{f}(x,t,q)\coloneqq\widehat{f(x,t,\cdot)}(q)\qquad\text{for }\quad(x,t,q)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}\,.

Hence, if
f(x,t,\cdot)
is superlinear for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then the resulting map
\widehat{f}:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,\infty]
is

\widehat{f}(x,t,q^{x},q^{t})=\begin{cases}-q^{t}f(x,t,\frac{q^{x}}{-q^{t}})\,,&\textrm{ if }q^{t}<0\,,\\
\infty\,,\quad&\textrm{ if $q^{t}>0$ or $q^{t}=0$ and $q^{x}\not=0$}\,,\\
0\,,\quad&\textrm{ if $(q^{x},q^{t})=(0,0)$}\,.\end{cases}

(5.9)

For every
v\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
such that the distributional derivative
Dv
is a finite measure on
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we define the measure

\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\coloneqq\widehat{f}\left(x,t,\frac{Dv}{|Dv|}\right)|Dv|\,,

where
|Dv|
is the total variation of
Dv
and
\frac{Dv}{|Dv|}
denotes the Radon–Nikodým derivative of
Dv
with respect to
|Dv|
. For the definition of convex functions of measures, see e.g.
[
2
, Section 2.6]
. We then consider the auxiliary energy

\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v)\coloneqq\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\widehat{f}\left(x,t,\frac{Dv}{|Dv|}(x,t)\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv|(x,t)\,.

(5.10)

Given
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
and setting
v=1_{u}
, it follows from (
5.2
) and the homogeneity of
\widehat{f}
with respect to the last variable that for every Borel function
h:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{[0,\infty)}
,

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}h(x,t){\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}h(x,t)\widehat{f}(x,t,(\nabla u,-1))\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}\,.

The definition of
\widehat{f}
given in (
5.9
) and the area formula (
3.14
) then yield

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}h(x,t){\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)=\int_{\Omega}h(x,u(x))f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(5.11)

In particular , taking
h\equiv 1
, we obtain that
E_{\Omega}(u)=\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{u})
for every
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, where
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
is given by (
5.10
).

5.3
Construction of the inner approximating sequence in a reduced setting

In this section, we work under seemingly more restrictive assumptions than in the main results. By now we consider an open set
\Lambda
in
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
such that
\Omega\Subset\Lambda
, while the Lagrangian satisfies the following, technical version of our main structural assumption.

(
f^{\rm red}
)

Assume that
f:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a Carathéodory function which is convex and superlinear with respect to the last variable and
f(x,t,0)=0
for a.e.
x\in\Lambda
and all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

We explain in Section
9
how the extra assumptions can be removed. Our goal in the reduced setting reads as follows.

Proposition 5.3

(Absence of the Lavrentiev phenomenon in the reduced setting)
.

Let
\Lambda
be an open set on
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, let
\Omega\Subset\Lambda
be a bounded Lipschitz open set,
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz continuous,
p\geq 1
, and
f:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
satisfy Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

). Consider the functional
E_{\Omega}
be defined as in (
1.2
). Then, for every
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
there exists a sequence
(\widetilde{u}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
such that
\widetilde{u}_{n}\to u
as
n\to\infty
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
,
\sup_{n}\|\widetilde{u}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{\Omega}(\widetilde{u}_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)\,.

We emphasize that in the above statement, the assumption (

H
conv

) is required to hold on
\Lambda
and not just on
\Omega
.

Let us present the construction of the initial approximation and establish its basic properties. We start from a map
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap W^{1,p}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
for some
p\geq 1
.
Let

M\coloneqq\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda)}\,.

(5.12)

We introduce an absolutely continuous positive function

\alpha:{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,\infty)

(5.13)

such that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\alpha(t)=0
and for every
\mathcal{k}_{1}>0
, there exists
c_{\mathcal{k}_{1}}>0
such that

\alpha^{\prime}(t)\leq-c_{\mathcal{k}_{1}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t\in(-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1})$}\,.

(5.14)

Ultimately,
\alpha
will be subject to an additional condition that we proceed to formulate. Remember first that
\Lambda_{+}
is a Borel subset of
\Lambda
such that
|\nabla u|>0
a.e. on
\Lambda_{+}
and
|\nabla u|=0
a.e. on
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{+}
. We then observe that for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
{{H}}^{N-1}
a.e.
z\in u^{-1}(t)
, we have
\nabla u(z)\in\Lambda_{+}
. This is a consequence of the coarea formula, which also implies that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{u^{-1}(t)}\frac{f(z,t,\nabla u(z))+|\nabla u(z)|^{p}}{|\nabla u(z)|}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\right){\,{\rm d}t}=\int_{\Lambda_{+}}f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x))+|\nabla u(x)|^{p}{\,{\rm d}x}\,.

We deduce therefrom that the inner integral in the left-hand side is a summable function of
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and we are thus entitled to require that
for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

-\alpha^{\prime}(t)\geq\int_{u^{-1}(t)}\frac{f(z,u(z),\nabla u(z))+|\nabla u(z)|^{p}}{|\nabla u(z)|}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\,.

(5.15)

In order to construct the approximate sequence, we employ the function
v={1}_{u}
defined in (
5.1
) and then, for every
\delta\geq 0
, we set
v_{0,\delta}:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

v_{0,\delta}(x,t)\coloneqq v(x,t)+\delta\alpha(t)\,.

(5.16)

Recall also
{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
from (
3.4
) and note that
since
\Omega\Subset\Lambda
, one can find
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
such that
\Omega+B{(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})}\subset\Lambda
. For every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
, we consider the map
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined as

v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)\coloneqq(v_{0,\delta}*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})(x,t)=(v*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})(x,t)+\delta\alpha(t)\,.

(5.17)

Note that if
\delta\in(0,\infty)
and
{\varepsilon}\in[0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
, then
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
is decreasing with respect to the second variable. We shall denote by
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
the Radon–Nikodým derivative of
Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
with respect to its total variation, i.e.,
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{x},q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t})
and

q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\coloneqq\frac{Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}}{|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}\,.

(5.18)

By standard properties of convolution, the family
(v_{\varepsilon,\delta})_{\varepsilon}
converges to
v_{0,\delta}
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
when
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. Moreover,
Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=Dv_{0,\delta}*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
for every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0}),\delta\in(0,\infty)
, and the family
(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly-
*
to
Dv_{0,\delta}
when
{\varepsilon}\to 0
.
From
[
2
, Corollary 1.60]
, this implies that

\liminf_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})\geq|Dv_{0,\delta}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})\,.

(5.19)

Actually, the converse inequality also holds.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
,
{\varepsilon}\in[0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
, and
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be defined in (
5.17
). For every Borel set
A\subset\Omega
,

|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})\leq\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{A}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{0,\delta}|(x,t)\,,

(5.20)

where the inner integral in the right-hand side reduces to
\chi_{A}(x)
when
{\varepsilon}=0
. Moreover,

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})\leq|Dv_{0,\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=\int_{A}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}+\delta{|A|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|\alpha^{\prime}|{\,{\rm d}t}\,.

(5.21)

Proof.

The measures
{{H}}^{N}\llcorner{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}
and
{{H}}^{N+1}
are mutually singular.
By the definition of
v_{0,\delta}
and Lemma
5.2
, we thus have

|Dv_{0,\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=|Dv|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})+\delta|\alpha^{\prime}|{{{H}}^{N+1}}(A\times{\mathbb{R}})={{H}}^{N}({\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}\cap(A\times{\mathbb{R}}))+\delta\int_{A\times{\mathbb{R}}}|\alpha^{\prime}|{\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}\,.

Using the area formula for the first term and the fact that
|\alpha^{\prime}|
depends only on
t
for the second one, this gives the second equality in (
5.21
); that is,

|Dv_{0,\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=\int_{A}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}+\delta{|A|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|\alpha^{\prime}|{\,{\rm d}t}\,.

(5.22)

We deduce therefrom that

|A|=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|Dv_{0,\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=0.

(5.23)

For the first inequality in (
5.21
), we start from the fact that
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\leq|Dv_{0,\delta}|*_{x}\varrho_{\varepsilon}
for every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
.
Together with (
3.5
), this implies (
5.20
) from which we deduce that

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})\leq\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{A}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{0,\delta}|(x,t)\,.

(5.24)

The inner integral in the right-hand side is equal to
\chi_{A}*\widetilde{{\varrho}}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)
where
\widetilde{{\varrho}}(y)={\varrho}(-y)
, for every
y\in{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
. We observe that
\chi_{A}*\widetilde{{\varrho}}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)
is bounded from above by
1
and converges to
\chi_{A}(x)
for every Lebesgue point
x
of
\chi_{A}
. From (
5.23
), we deduce that for
|Dv_{0,\delta}|
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, the point
x
is a Lebesgue point of
\chi_{A}
and thus

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\chi_{A}*\widetilde{{\varrho}}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)=\chi_{A}(x).

Moreover, applying (
5.22
) with
A=\Omega
, we get that
|Dv_{0,\delta}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})<\infty
. We can thus apply the dominated convergence theorem in the right-hand side of (
5.24
) to obtain
the first inequality in (
5.21
).

∎

From (
5.19
) and (
5.21
) with
A=\Omega
, it follows that

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})=|Dv_{0,\delta}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}).

(5.25)

As a by-product of Lemma
5.4
(see (
5.23
) for
{\varepsilon}=0
and (
5.20
) for
{\varepsilon}>0
), we also deduce what follows.

Remark 5.5
.

For every Borel set
A\subset\Omega
such that
{|A|}=0
, one has
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=0
for
{\varepsilon}\in[0,{\varepsilon}_{0}),\,\delta\in{[0,\infty)}
.

The fact that
f(x,t,0)=0
in
(

f
red

)
entitles one to restrict the measure
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})
to suitable subsets of
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
:

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose
f
satisfies
(

f
red

)
.
Let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda),v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
defined as in (
5.17
),
M
given by (
5.12
) and let us recall the decomposition
\Lambda=\Lambda_{+}\cup\Lambda_{0}
from (
3.1
). Then, for every
\delta\in{[0,\infty)}
,

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv_{0,\delta})=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv_{0,\delta}\llcorner(\Lambda\times[-M,M])).

(5.26)

Moreover,

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)=\left(\frac{1}{|\nabla u|}f(\cdot,t,\nabla u)\left({{H}}^{N-1}\llcorner(u^{-1}(t)\cap\Lambda_{+})\right)\right)\otimes{{{H}}^{1}}.

(5.27)

Finally, for every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
,

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\llcorner(\Omega\times[-M,M]))\,.

(5.28)

Proof.

Since
Dv_{0,\delta}
is the sum of the two mutually singular measures
Dv
and
(0,\delta\alpha^{\prime}){{{H}}^{N+1}}
, one has by
[
2
, Proposition 2.37]
,

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv_{0,\delta})=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)+\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,(0,\delta\alpha^{\prime})){{{H}}^{N+1}}\,.

Using that
f(x,t,0)=0
for a.e.
x\in\Lambda
and every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we get

\forall q^{t}\in(-\infty,0]\qquad\widehat{f}(x,t,(0,q^{t})))=0\,.

(5.29)

In view of the fact that
\alpha^{\prime}(t)<0
for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, this implies that
\widehat{f}(x,t,(0,\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t)))=0
for
{{H}}^{N+1}
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}
and the first equality in (
5.26
) follows. The second one is a consequence of the fact that
Dv=Dv\llcorner(\Lambda\times[-M,M])
, which in turn follows from the fact that
{\mathrm{Graph}_{u}}\subset\Lambda\times[-M,M]
.

We proceed with the proof of (
5.27
). Using the notation introduced in (
5.18
), one has for
|Dv|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

q_{0,0}(x,t)=\frac{Dv}{|Dv|}(x,t)=\frac{(\nabla u(x),-1)}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\,.

Since
\nabla u(x)=0
for a.e.
x\in\Lambda_{0}
, we have
q_{0,0}^{x}(x,t)=0

|Dv|
-a.e. on
\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}
by Remark
5.5
, which also implies by (
5.29
) that
\widehat{f}(x,t,q_{(0,0)}(x,t))=0
for
|Dv|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Hence,

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv\llcorner(\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}))\,.

Inserting (
5.3
) (with
\Lambda
instead of
\Omega
and
A=\Lambda_{+}
) in the above identity, we get

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)=\widehat{f}\left(\cdot,t,\chi_{\Lambda_{+}}\frac{(\nabla u,-1)}{|\nabla u|}\right)\left({{H}}^{N-1}\llcorner u^{-1}(t)\right)\otimes{{{H}}^{1}}\,,

and (
5.27
) then follows from the definition of
\widehat{f}
in terms of
f
.

By definition of partial convolution, for every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
and every Borel
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

Dv_{{\varepsilon},0}(A^{\prime})=\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}Dv(x,t)=\int_{\Lambda\times[-M,M]}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}Dv(x,t)\,.

This implies (
5.28
).

∎

As a consequence of the above lemma, we can deduce that
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-negligible sets are also
\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}
-negligible.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose
f
satisfies
(

f
red

)
.
Let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
and let us recall
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
from (
5.17
). For every
\delta\in(0,\infty),{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
, and every Borel set
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
it holds that

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}{(A^{\prime})}={\int_{A^{\prime}}}\int_{\Lambda_{+}\cap u^{-1}(t)}\frac{1}{|\nabla u(x)|}f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(x){{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N+1}(y,t)}\,.

(5.30)

Moreover, the measure
\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
.

Proof.

From the definition of partial convolution,
for every Borel set
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(A^{\prime})=\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv).

By (
5.27
), this gives

\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(A^{\prime})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{\Lambda_{+}\cap u^{-1}(t)}\frac{1}{|\nabla u(x)|}f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x))\left(\int_{\Omega}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(x){\,{\rm d}t}

and (
5.30
) then follows from the Fubini theorem.

To prove the last assertion, let
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
be a Borel subset such that
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(A^{\prime})=0
. Then, one exploits the fact that
|\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\leq|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
to deduce that
|\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(A^{\prime})=0
. We then observe that
\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
is the sum of two non-positive measures:

\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=\partial_{t}v*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}+\delta\alpha^{\prime}{{{H}}^{N+1}}\,.

This implies that

\int_{A^{\prime}}\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=0\,.

Since
\alpha^{\prime}(t)<0
for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, it follows that
{|A^{\prime}|}=0
, which by (
5.30
) implies
\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(A^{\prime})=0
, as desired.
∎

6
The liminf estimate

The proofs of the liminf and the limsup estimates strongly rely on the Reschetnyak continuity theorem, which requires boundedness and continuity properties of the integrand. Let us notice that by Lemma
A.2
any function satisfying (

H
conv

) is bounded on bounded sets. We exploit this fact in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Suppose
f
satisfies
(

f
red

)
.
Let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
and let us recall
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
and
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
given by (
5.17
) and (
5.18
). Let
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
be a continuous function which vanishes on
[c,\infty)
for some
c<0
. Then, for every
\delta\in(0,\infty)
, it holds

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(q^{t}_{0,\delta}){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{0,\delta}).

Proof.

Given
\tau>0
, there exists
\iota_{\tau}>0
such that for every Borel set
A\subset\Lambda
,

{|A|}\leq\iota_{\tau}\Longrightarrow\int_{A}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}+\delta{|A|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|\alpha^{\prime}|{\,{\rm d}t}\leq\tau.

(6.1)

Let
\eta:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
be a continuous compactly supported function such that
\eta\equiv 1
on
[-M,M]
. Let us define the function
h:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{S}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
via

h(x,t,(q^{x},q^{t}))\coloneqq\eta(t)\theta(q^{t})\widehat{f}(x,t,(q^{x},q^{t}))\,.

Observe that
h(x,t,(q^{x},q^{t}))=0
when
q^{t}\geq c
while for
q^{t}\leq c
,

h(x,t,(q^{x},q^{t}))=-q^{t}\eta(t)\theta(q^{t})f\left(x,t,\frac{q^{x}}{-q^{t}}\right).

We deduce from Lemma
A.2
that
h
is bounded on
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{S}^{N}
. Moreover, for a.e.
x\in\Omega
, the map
(t,q)\mapsto h(x,t,q)
is uniformly continuous. By the Scorza–Dragoni theorem, there exists a compact set
K_{\tau}\subset\Omega
such that
{|\Omega\setminus K_{\tau}|}\leq\iota_{\tau}
and
h|_{K_{\tau}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{S}^{N}}
is continuous. By the Tietze theorem, there exists a continuous extension
\overline{h}:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{S}^{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\|\overline{h}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

Since
Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
weakly-
*
converges to
Dv_{0,\delta}
when
{\varepsilon}\to 0
and using also (
5.25
), we can rely on the Reschetnyak continuity theorem (see e.g.
[
2
, Theorem 2.39]
) to obtain

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\overline{h}\left(x,t,\frac{Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}}{|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\overline{h}\left(x,t,\frac{Dv_{0,\delta}}{|Dv_{0,\delta}|}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{0,\delta}|\,.

Since
h
and
\overline{h}
only differs on
(\Omega\setminus K_{\tau})\times{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{S}^{N}
, we obtain that:

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\left|\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}h\left(x,t,\frac{Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}}{|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|-\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}h\left(x,t,\frac{Dv_{0,\delta}}{|Dv_{0,\delta}|}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{0,\delta}|\right|\\
\leq(\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\overline{h}\|_{L^{\infty}})\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\left(|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|+|Dv_{0,\delta}|\right)\left((\Omega\setminus K_{\tau})\times{\mathbb{R}}\right).

Using that
\|\overline{h}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
and also Lemma
5.4
, this gives

\displaystyle\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\left|\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}h\left(x,t,\frac{Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}}{|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|-\right.

\displaystyle\left.\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}h\left(x,t,\frac{Dv_{0,\delta}}{|Dv_{0,\delta}|}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{0,\delta}|\right|\leq 4\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}|Dv_{0,\delta}|((\Omega\setminus K_{\tau})\times{\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle=4\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\int_{\Omega\setminus K_{\tau}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}+\delta{|\Omega\setminus K_{\tau}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|\alpha^{\prime}|{\,{\rm d}t}\right)\leq 4\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\tau\,,

where the last inequality follows from (
6.1
) applied to
A=\Omega\setminus K_{\tau}
.
In the left-hand side, we rely on the definition of
h
, the fact that
\eta\equiv 1
on
[-M,M]
as well as Lemma
5.6
to obtain:

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\left|\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})-\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(q^{t}_{0,\delta}){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{0,\delta})\right|\leq 4\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\tau.

Since
\tau
is arbitrary, this implies the desired result.
∎

We can easily derive from Lemma
6.1
the liminf estimate for
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}
.

Proposition 6.2

(The liminf estimate)
.

Suppose
f
satisfies
(

f
red

)
. Let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) for
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
.Then for every
\delta>0
, it holds

\liminf_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t))\geq\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv(x,t))\,.

(6.2)

Proof.

By Lemma
6.1
with
\theta=\theta_{k}
(given by (
3.10
)) and the fact that
\theta_{k}\leq 1
, we have:

\liminf_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\geq\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\theta_{k}(q^{t}_{0,\delta}){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{0,\delta})\,.

Using that
q^{t}_{0,\delta}(x,t)<0
for
|Dv_{0,\delta}|
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we deduce from the dominated convergence theorem
that the right-hand side converges, as
k\to\infty
, to
\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{0,\delta})
, which in turn is equal to the right-hand side of (
6.2
) by Lemma
5.6
.
∎

The corresponding limsup estimate is much more delicate to prove and is the object of the next section.

7
The limsup estimate

We rely on the notation introduced in Section
5.3
.
The goal of this section is the following limsup estimate, which will be proven in Section
7.3
. With this aim, Sections
7.1
-
7.2
are focused on translating the anti-jump conditions to the convexified energy.

Proposition 7.1

(The limsup estimate)
.

Suppose
f
satisfies
(

f
red

)
. Let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) for
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
and
\alpha
satisfying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
). Then for every
\delta>0
, it holds

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)\,.

7.1
Consequences for
\widehat{f}
of (

H
conv

) imposed on
f

In this section, we formulate the natural implication of the anti-jump condition (

H
conv

) for functions governing
\widehat{E}
. We denote by
F^{\varepsilon}:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}\to[0,\infty]
the map

F^{\varepsilon}(x,t,q)\coloneqq\widehat{\bigg(\big(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\big)^{**}\bigg)}(q)\,.

(7.1)

Since
f(x,t,0)=0
for a.e.
x\in\Lambda
and every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, one has
f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,0)=0
for every
(x,t)\in\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}
. Hence, using that
0\leq(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}\leq f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}
, we deduce that
(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(0)=0
and thus,

\forall(x,t,q^{t})\in\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times(-\infty,0]\quad\text{it holds}\quad F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,(0,q^{t}))=0\,.

(7.2)

Since
f
is superlinear in the sense of (
3.3
), the map
(x,t,\xi)\mapsto f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)
is superlinear as well, and so is the map
(x,t,\xi)\mapsto(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)
. We deduce therefrom that

\forall(q^{x},q^{t})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{N}\times[0,\infty))\setminus\{(0,0)\}\quad\text{it holds}\quad F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,(q^{x},q^{t}))=+\infty\,.

(7.3)

We will show that if
f
satisfies condition (

H
conv

), then
F^{\varepsilon}
satisfies that for every
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
, for a.e.
x\in\Lambda
, for every
t\in[-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1}]
, for every
q\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
with
q^{t}<0
,

\left(\frac{|q^{x}|}{|q^{t}|}\right)^{\max(p,N)}+\frac{1}{|q^{t}|}F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,q)\leq\frac{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{N}}\Longrightarrow\widehat{f}(x,t,q)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}(F^{\varepsilon}(x,t,q)+|q^{t}|)\,,

(7.4)

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}>0
is the constant in (

H
conv

), which depends only on
\mathcal{k}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

If
f
satisfies Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

) on
\Lambda
, and
F^{\varepsilon}
is given by (
7.1
), then (
7.4
) holds.

Proof.

Due to (

H
conv

) with
\xi=\frac{q^{x}}{|q^{t}|}
, for a.e.
x\in\Lambda
, for every
t\in(-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1})
, for every
q\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
with
q^{t}\not=0
,
it holds

\left(\frac{|q^{x}|}{|q^{t}|}\right)^{\max(p,N)}+{\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}\left(\tfrac{q^{x}}{|q^{t}|}\right)\leq\frac{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{N}}}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad|q^{t}|f\left(x,t,\tfrac{q^{x}}{|q^{t}|}\right)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}|q^{t}|\left[\left(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}\left(\tfrac{q^{x}}{|q^{t}|}\right)+1\right]\,,

which implies the desired result when
q^{t}<0
. For
q^{t}>0
or
q^{t}=0
and
q^{x}\not=0
, the right-hand side of (
7.4
) is
+\infty
. Finally, when
(q^{x},q^{t})=(0,0)
, both sides of (
7.4
) vanish.
∎

A substantial part of the proof of Proposition
7.1
amounts to proving that the map
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{x},q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t})
defined in (
5.18
) satisfies the estimate required in the left-hand side of (
7.4
). A key ingredient is the Jensen inequality, that we apply here to functionals defined on subsets of measures.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
f
satisfy
(

f
red

)
. Let
F^{\varepsilon}
be given by (
7.1
) and let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) with
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)
. Then for every
\delta\in(0,\infty)
, and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
, for every
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-measurable set
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\left(\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\right)\,.

Proof.

Observe that the measure
\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}
, which is absolutely continuous with respect to
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
by Lemma
5.7
, has a natural extension to the
\sigma
-algebra of
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-measurable sets, so the above inequality makes sense.

We first disintegrate the measure
Dv
by writing

Dv={\mathsf{v}}_{t}\otimes{\mathsf{w}}
, where
{\mathsf{w}}
is a positive finite measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathsf{v}}_{t}
is an
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measure on
\Lambda
with
|{\mathsf{v}}_{t}(\Lambda)|=1
. Then,

Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=\left(({\mathsf{v}}_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}){{H}}^{N})\otimes{\mathsf{w}}+(0,\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t)\right){{{H}}^{N+1}}\,.

By subadditivity and homogeneity of
F^{{\varepsilon}}
, we have

F^{{\varepsilon}}(\cdot,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq(F^{{\varepsilon}}\left(\cdot,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}){{H}}^{N}\right)\otimes{\mathsf{w}}+\delta F^{{\varepsilon}}\left(\cdot,t,(0,\alpha^{\prime}(t))\right){{{H}}^{N+1}}=\left(F^{{\varepsilon}}(\cdot,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}){{H}}^{N}\right)\otimes{\mathsf{w}}\,,

(7.5)

where the last equality relies on (
7.2
).
Using the convexity and the homogeneity of
F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,\cdot)
, the Jensen inequality implies that for every
x\in\Omega
,

F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x))\leq\int_{\Lambda}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x-y){\,{\rm d}}F^{{\varepsilon}}\left(x,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t}(y)\right).

For a.e.
y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})
and for every
(t,q)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{S}^{N}
, it holds that
F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,q)\leq\widehat{f}(y,t,q)
. This inequality remains true for
|{\mathsf{v}}_{t}|\otimes{\mathsf{w}}
a.e.
(y,t)\in B(x,{\varepsilon})\times{\mathbb{R}}
and every
q\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, in view of the fact that
|{\mathsf{v}}_{t}|\otimes{\mathsf{w}}(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=|Dv|(A\times{\mathbb{R}})=0
for every Borel set
A\subset\Lambda
such that
{|A|}=0
, see Remark
5.5
. It follows that for
{\mathsf{w}}
a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and every
x\in\Omega
,

F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x))\leq\int_{\Lambda}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x-y){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}\left(y,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t}(y)\right)=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t})*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)\,.

Inserting this estimate in (
7.5
), one gets

\displaystyle F^{{\varepsilon}}(\cdot,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t})*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}{{H}}^{N}\right)\otimes{\mathsf{w}}=\left(\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t})\otimes{\mathsf{w}}\right)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,{\mathsf{v}}_{t}\otimes{\mathsf{w}})*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}=\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\,.

∎

Remark 7.4
.

When
f(x,t,\xi)=g(t,\xi)
for some continuous function
g:{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
which is convex with respect to the second variable and satisfies
g(t,0)=0
for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the corresponding function
F^{\varepsilon}
is simply
\widehat{g}
. The conclusion of Lemma
7.3
then reads

\widehat{g}(t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\widehat{g}(t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}.

We thus recover the Jensen inequality for measures formulated in
[
16
, Proposition 3.11]
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
f
satisfy
(

f
red

)
and let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
. For every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
F^{\varepsilon}
be given by (
7.1
) and let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) with an absolutely continuous bounded function
\alpha:{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,\infty)
satisfying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
). Then there exists
\mathsf{c}>0
which depends only on
\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}
, such that for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
we have

F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\frac{\mathsf{c}}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}(-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\,.

Proof.

Let
A
be a Borel set in
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
By Lemma
5.7
, the estimate
\|{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq{\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}
and (
5.15
), one gets

\int_{A}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi_{A}(y,t)\int_{\Lambda_{+}\cap u^{-1}(t)}\frac{f(z,t,\nabla u(z))}{|\nabla u(z)|}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z){\,{\rm d}t}{\,{\rm d}y}\leq\frac{\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{A}-\alpha^{\prime}(t){\,{\rm d}t}{\,{\rm d}y}\,,

where
\Lambda_{+}
is defined in (
3.1
).
Then we use that

\delta\alpha^{\prime}{{{H}}^{N+1}}=\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}-\partial_{t}v*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\geq\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}.

(7.6)

This gives

\int_{A}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{A}-{\,{\rm d}}\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=\frac{\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{A}(-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\,.

(7.7)

In view of Lemma
7.3
, it holds that

\int_{A}F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|=\int_{A}{\,{\rm d}}F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\frac{\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{A}(-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\,.

Since
A
is arbitrary, this proves the desired result.
∎

Lemma
7.5
provides a suitable estimate for the second term in the left-hand side of (
7.4
). The estimate of the first term is given in the following statement.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
. For every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) with an absolutely continuous bounded function
\alpha:{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,\infty)
satisfying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
). Then there exists
\mathsf{c}^{\prime}>0
which depends only on
\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}
,
\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}}
, and
c_{M}
from (
5.14
) with
M=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda)}
, such that for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\left(\frac{|q^{x}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)|}{|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)|}\right)^{\max(p,N)}\leq\frac{\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}{\delta^{N}{\varepsilon}^{N}}\,.

Proof.

We first prove that for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\left(\frac{|q^{x}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}{|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}\right)^{p}(x,t)\leq\frac{C}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}\qquad\text{for some }\ C>0\,.

(7.8)

Let us introduce the map
g(\xi)\coloneqq|\xi|^{p}
.
Since
q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)<0
for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-a.e.
(x,t)
, this is equivalent to
the fact that for every Borel set
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds

\int_{A^{\prime}}-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}g\left(\frac{q^{x}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}}{-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}}\right){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\leq\frac{C}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{A^{\prime}}-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}{\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\,.

In turn, by the very definition of
\widehat{g}
in (
5.7
), it suffices to show that

\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{g}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\frac{C}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{A^{\prime}}-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}{\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\,.

(7.9)

By Remark
7.4
, the left-hand side above satisfies

\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{g}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{g}(Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}.

Since the function
(x,t,\xi)\mapsto g(\xi)
is a Carathéodory non-negative function which is convex with respect to the last variable and (
5.15
) is satisfied, we obtain similarly to (
7.7
) the following countepart for
g
:

\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{g}(Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{\|{\varrho}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\delta{\varepsilon}^{N}}\int_{A^{\prime}}(-q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\,.

Hence, (
7.9
) is a consequence of the last two displays and (
7.8
) is justified.

Let us now prove that for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\left(\frac{|q^{x}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}{|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}\right)(x,t)\leq\frac{C}{\delta{\varepsilon}}\qquad\text{for some }\ C>0\,.

(7.10)

For any
e\in{\mathbb{S}}^{N-1}
, we introduce the function
h_{e}:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
given by
h_{e}(x,t,\xi)=\langle\xi,e\rangle
. Note that
h
is linear with respect to
\xi
and satisfies
h_{e}(x,t,0)=0
. We then rely on Lemma
5.7
applied to
h_{e}
instead of
f
and Remark
7.4
to get for every Borel set
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
:

\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{h}_{e}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\int_{\Omega}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t)\int_{\Lambda_{+}\cap u^{-1}(t)}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-z)\frac{\partial_{e}u(z)}{|\nabla u(z)|}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z){\,{\rm d}t}{\,{\rm d}y}\,.

(7.11)

Fix
y\in\Omega
. Then, the function
{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-\cdot)
is compactly supported in
\Lambda
.
Applying Lemma
5.1
on
\Lambda
instead of
\Omega
with the functions

\Phi(x,t)\coloneqq{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x)\eta(t)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta(t)\coloneqq\chi_{A^{\prime}\cap(\Lambda\times[-M,M])}(y,t)\,,

where
\eta
is any function in
C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
such that
\eta\equiv 1
on
[-M,M]
(recall that
M=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
),
one gets

\int_{\Lambda}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,u(x)){\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x)\partial_{e}u(x){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{-M}^{M}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t)\left(\int_{[u\geq t]}\partial_{e}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}x}\right){\,{\rm d}t}\,.

In the left-hand side, one can restrict the domain of integration to
\Lambda_{+}
instead of
\Lambda
, and then get by the coarea formula,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t)\left(\int_{u^{-1}(t)\cap\Lambda_{+}}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-z)\frac{\partial_{e}u(z)}{|\nabla u(z)|}{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\right){\,{\rm d}t}=\int_{-M}^{M}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t)\left(\int_{[u\geq t]}\partial_{e}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}x}\right){\,{\rm d}t}\,.

Integrating with respect to
y\in\Omega
and inserting the resulting estimate into (
7.11
), one gets

\displaystyle\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{h}_{e}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\int_{-M}^{M}\chi_{A^{\prime}}(y,t)\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}|\partial_{e}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x)|{\,{\rm d}x}\right){\,{\rm d}t}{\,{\rm d}y}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})}|A^{\prime}\cap(\Omega\times(-M,M))|\,.

(7.12)

By (
5.14
), we know that
\essinf_{t\in[-M,M]}-\alpha^{\prime}(t)\geq c_{M}>0
. Taking into account that
\delta\alpha^{\prime}{{{H}}^{N+1}}\geq\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
in the sense of non-positive measures, we find that

\displaystyle\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{h}_{e}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})}}{c_{M}\delta{\varepsilon}}\int_{A^{\prime}\cap(\Omega\times(-M,M))}-{\,{\rm d}}\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\leq\frac{\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})}}{c_{M}\delta{\varepsilon}}\int_{A^{\prime}}-{\,{\rm d}}\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\,.

This is equivalent to the fact that for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds

\langle q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{x},e\rangle\leq\frac{\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})}}{c_{M}{\delta}{\varepsilon}}|q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}|\,.

Since
e
is arbitrary, this proves (
7.10
). We then have both (
7.8
) and (
7.10
), which completes the proof.
∎

We are now in position to apply (
7.4
) and derive its consequences to estimate the measure
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})
from above.

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose
f
satisfies Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

). For every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) with
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
and
\alpha
satisfying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
).
Let
\mathsf{c}
and
\mathsf{c}^{\prime}
be given by Lemma
7.5
and Lemma
7.6
respectively and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k_{\delta}}}>0
be given by (
7.4
) for
\mathcal{k}_{\delta}=(M,(\mathsf{c}+\mathsf{c}^{\prime})/{\delta^{N}})
with
M=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda)}
. Then for every
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-measurable set
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds that

\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}_{\delta}}\int_{A^{\prime}}{\,{\rm d}}\left(\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}+\widehat{1}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\right)\,.

(7.13)

Proof.

Recall the definition of
F^{\varepsilon}
from (
7.1
) , and the definition of
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
from (
5.18
). By Lemma
7.5
and Lemma
7.6
, for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\left(\frac{|q^{x}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}{|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}\right)^{\max(p,N)}+\frac{1}{|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|}F^{{\varepsilon}}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\frac{\mathsf{c}+\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}{{\delta^{N}}{\varepsilon}^{N}}\,.

In view of (
7.4
) with
\mathcal{k}_{\delta}=(M,(\mathsf{c}+\mathsf{c}^{\prime})/{\delta^{N}})
and also Remark
5.5
, this implies that there exists
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}_{\delta}}>0
such that for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times[-M,M]
,

\widehat{f}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}_{\delta}}\left(F^{\varepsilon}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta})+|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\right)\,.

Since
q^{x}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=0
outside
\Omega\times[-M,M]
and
\widehat{f}(x,t,(0,q^{t}))=0
for every
q^{t}\leq 0
, the above inequality is actually true
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
In terms of measures, this yields

\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}_{\delta}}\left(F^{\varepsilon}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})+|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}||Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\right)\,.

By Lemma
7.3
,

\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}_{\delta}}\left(\widehat{f}(\cdot,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}+|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}||Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\right)\,.

Since
q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\leq 0
, the definition of
\widehat{1}
implies that
|q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}||Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|=\widehat{1}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})
, which completes the proof of (
7.13
).
∎

7.2
A key estimate

To prove Proposition
7.1
, we split the domain
\Omega
into two regions. In the good one, corresponding to the set where
\nabla u
is small, we rely on the Reschetnyak continuity theorem, or more specifically, on its formulation given in Lemma
6.1
. In the bad region where
\nabla u
is large, we use the following estimate, which is based on the consequences of the Jensen inequality that we have derived in the previous section. The decomposition of
\Omega
in two parts is conveyed through a function
\beta
which is subsequently chosen to vanish in the good region (see the proof of Proposition
7.1
in Section
7.3
).

Proposition 7.8
.

Suppose that
f
satisfies Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

).
Let
\beta:{\mathbb{R}}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a continuous, bounded, and non-decreasing function. Let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
and
q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}=(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{x},q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t})
be given by (
5.17
) and (
5.18
), respectively, with
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
and
\alpha
satisfying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
). Then for every
\delta>0
, there exists
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\delta}>0
such that

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t))\widehat{f}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\\
\leq\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\delta}\left[\int_{\Omega}\beta\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{|\nabla u(x)|^{2}+1}}\right)f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}+\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{0,\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}\widehat{1}}(Dv_{0,\delta})\right].

(7.14)

Proof.

We proceed step by step.

Step 1. Decomposition of
Dv
and
Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
.

Let us recall the decomposition
\Lambda=\Lambda_{+}\cup\Lambda_{0}
, with
|\nabla u|>0
a.e. on
\Lambda_{+}
and
\nabla u=0
a.e. on
\Lambda_{0}
.
We define
\ell:\Lambda\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
by the following formula

\ell(x)\coloneqq\chi_{\Lambda_{+}}(x)\frac{(\nabla u(x),-1)}{|\nabla u(x)|}\,.

(7.15)

We will show that there exist non-negative finite measures
\gamma_{t}
on
\Lambda
, a non-negative function
h\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
, and a non-negative finite measure
\mu_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}
such that
{\,{\rm d}t}
and
\mu_{0}
are mutually singular, and

Dv(x,t)=(\ell(x){{H}}^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}u^{-1}(t)){\otimes{{H}}^{1}}-(0,h(t)\gamma_{t}){\otimes{{H}}^{1}}-(0,\gamma_{t})\otimes\mu_{0}\,.

(7.16)

By (
5.3
) applied with
\Lambda
instead of
\Omega
and
A=\Lambda_{+}
, one has:

Dv\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}})=(\ell{{H}}^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}u^{-1}(t)){\otimes{{H}}^{1}}\,.

(7.17)

On the other hand, for every bounded Borel map
g:\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we have by (
5.2
) and the area formula,

\int_{\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}}g(x,t){\,{\rm d}}Dv(x,t)=\int_{\Lambda_{0}}(0,-g(x,u(x))){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

This proves that
(Dv\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}))^{x}=0
and
(Dv\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}))^{t}
is a non-positive finite measure. By disintegration, there exist non-negative probability measures
\gamma_{t}
on
\Lambda
and a non-negative finite measure
\overline{\mu}_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}
such that

Dv\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}})=-(0,\gamma_{t})\otimes\overline{\mu}_{0}\,.

We decompose
\overline{\mu}_{0}=h(t){\,{\rm d}t}+\mu_{0}
where
h\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
is non-negative, while
\mu_{0}
and
{\,{\rm d}t}
are mutually singular.
Hence,

\displaystyle Dv

\displaystyle=Dv\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}})+Dv\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}})=(\ell{{H}}^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}u^{-1}(t)){\otimes{{H}}^{1}}-(0,h(t)\gamma_{t}){\otimes{{H}}^{1}}-(0,\gamma_{t})\otimes\mu_{0}\,.

This completes the proof of (
7.16
).
It follows from the latter that for every
\delta\in(0,1)
,

Dv_{0,\delta}=\Big[\left(\ell{{H}}^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}u^{-1}(t)\right)-(0,h(t)\gamma_{t})+(0,\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t)){{{H}}^{N}}\Big]{\otimes{{H}}^{1}}-(0,\gamma_{t})\otimes\mu_{0}\,.

(7.18)

Then, by partial convolution with
{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
, one obtains that

Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)=\big[\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)-(0,h(t)\gamma_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x))+(0,\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t))\big]{{{H}}^{N+1}}-(0,\gamma_{t}*{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)){{{H}}^{N}}\otimes\mu_{0}\,,

(7.19)

where
\zeta_{\varepsilon}:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
is a Borel function such that for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, it holds

\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\left(\ell{{H}}^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}u^{-1}(t)\right)*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)=\int_{u^{-1}(t)}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x-z)\ell(z){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\,.

(7.20)

Step 2. Continuity of
\ell
given by (
7.15
).

By the coarea formula, the map
\ell
is summable on
u^{-1}(t)
for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We proceed to show that for
|Dv|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}

and for every continuous function
\phi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})
, it holds

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}\int_{u^{-1}(t)}\ell(z)\,\phi\left(\frac{z-x}{{\varepsilon}}\right){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)=\ell(x)\int_{T_{x}u^{-1}(t)}\phi(y){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(y)\,.

(7.21)

Remember that the approximate tangent space
T_{x}u^{-1}(t)
to the level set
u^{-1}(t)
exists at
{{H}}^{N-1}
-a.e.
x\in u^{-1}(t)
, see Section
3.2
. This means that for every
\phi\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})
, it holds

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}\int_{\Lambda}\phi\left(\frac{y-x}{{\varepsilon}}\right){\,{\rm d}}\left({{H}}^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}u^{-1}(t)\right)(y)-\int_{T_{x}u^{-1}(t)}\phi(y){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(y)=0\,.

(7.22)

We fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
u^{-1}(t)
is a countably
{{H}}^{N-1}
rectifiable set,
{{H}}^{N-1}(u^{-1}(t))<\infty
, and
\ell
is summable on
u^{-1}(t)
.

The measure
{{H}}^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}u^{-1}(t)
is finite and Borel regular (see e.g.
[
33
, Theorem 1 of Section 2.1 and Theorem 3 of Section 1.1]
). Since
\ell|_{u^{-1}(t)}
is summable with respect to this measure, then
{{H}}^{N-1}
-a.e.
x\in u^{-1}(t)
is a Lebesgue point of
\ell|_{u^{-1}(t)}
(see e.g.
[
33
, Corollary 1 of Section 1.7]
):

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\frac{1}{{{H}}^{N-1}(u^{-1}(t)\cap B(x,{\varepsilon}))}\int_{u^{-1}(t)\cap B(x,{\varepsilon})}|\ell-\ell(x)|{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}=0\,.

(7.23)

Using that
u^{-1}(t)
is
{{H}}^{N-1}
measurable, one has (see
[
33
, Section 2.3]
),

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\frac{{{H}}^{N-1}(u^{-1}(t)\cap B(x,{\varepsilon}))}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}<\infty\,,

for
{{H}}^{N-1}
-a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
. Together with (
7.23
), this implies

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}\int_{u^{-1}(t)\cap B(x,{\varepsilon})}|\ell-\ell(x)|{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}=0\,.

(7.24)

For every
x\in u^{-1}(t)
such that the above identity holds and
T_{x}u^{-1}(t)
exists, we make the following estimate:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}\right.\int_{u^{-1}(t)}\ell(z)\phi\left(\frac{z-x}{{\varepsilon}}\right)

\displaystyle{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\left.-\ell(x)\int_{T_{x}u^{-1}(t)}\phi(z){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}\int_{u^{-1}(t)}|\ell(z)-\ell(x)|\phi\left(\frac{z-x}{{\varepsilon}}\right){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)

\displaystyle\quad+|\ell(x)|\left|\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}\int_{u^{-1}(t)}\phi\left(\frac{z-x}{{\varepsilon}}\right){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)-\int_{T_{x}u^{-1}(t)}\phi(z){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}\right|\eqqcolon\textrm{I}_{\varepsilon}+\textrm{II}_{\varepsilon}\,.

Note that

\mathrm{I}_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}}{{\varepsilon}^{N-1}}\int_{B(x,{{\varepsilon}R})\cap u^{-1}(t)}|\ell(z)-\ell(x)|{\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\,,

where we choose
R>0
such that
\supp\phi\subset B(0,R)
, so
\mathrm{I}_{\varepsilon}\to 0
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
by using (
7.24
). Moreover,
\textrm{II}_{\varepsilon}\to 0
when
{\varepsilon}\to 0
by (
7.22
). It follows that (
7.21
) holds for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and for
{{{H}}}^{N-1}
-a.e.
x\in u^{-1}(t)
.
Since by (
7.17
) one has
|Dv|=(|\ell|{{H}}^{N-1}\llcorner u^{-1}(t))\otimes{{{H}}^{1}}
on
\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, the identity (
7.21
) also holds for
|Dv|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

Step 3. Completion of the proof of (
7.14
)

Fix
\delta\in(0,\infty)
and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
. The map
(x,t)\in\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\beta(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}(x,t))
is
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
-measurable and bounded.
From Lemma
7.7
, we deduce that

\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}_{\delta}}}\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t))\widehat{f}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\leq\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}+\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{1}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\,.

(7.25)

For the last term,
we claim that

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}\widehat{1}}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{0,\delta}){\,{\rm d}\widehat{1}}(Dv_{0,\delta})\,.

(7.26)

Indeed, since
q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}<0
a.e. for every
{\varepsilon},\delta\geq 0
, one has
\widehat{1}(Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=|\partial_{t}v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
. Moreover, we know by (
5.25
), that
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
converges to
|Dv_{0,\delta}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
. Hence, (
7.26
) follows from the Reshetnyak Continuity Theorem
[
2
, Theorem 2.39]
applied to the continuous bounded map
q\in\mathbb{S}^{N}\mapsto\beta(q^{t})|q^{t}|
.

By the decomposition of
Dv
described in Step 1, the measures
\mu_{0}
and
{{H}}^{1}
are mutually singular on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Hence, there exists a Borel subset
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\mu_{0}(I)=0
and
{|{\mathbb{R}}\setminus I|}=0
.
For
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times I
, the decomposition (
7.19
) implies that

q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)=\frac{Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)}{|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)|}=\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)+(0,-h(t)\gamma_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)+\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t))}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)+(0,-h(t)\gamma_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)+\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t))|}\,.

A simple calculation shows that for every
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
and
a,b<0
, one has

\frac{a+b}{|(\xi,a+b)|}\leq\frac{a}{|(\xi,a)|}\,.

We apply this remark with
(\xi,a)=\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
and
b=-h(t)\gamma_{t}*{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)+\delta\alpha^{\prime}(t)
to get

q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}(x,t)\leq\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(x,t)}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)|}\,,

where the right-hand side has to be understood as
0
when
\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=0
.
Since
\beta
is non-decreasing, this gives for
|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times I
,

\beta\left(q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{t}(x,t)\right)\leq\beta\left(\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(x,t)}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)|}\right)\,.

(7.27)

As already observed,
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
{{H}}^{N+1}
. In particular,
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(\Omega\times({\mathbb{R}}\setminus I))=0
and thus

\displaystyle\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\Omega}\times I}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\Omega}\times I}\beta\left(\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(x,t)}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x,t)|}\right){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,,

where the last inequality follows from (
7.27
). Since the integrand in the right-hand side is non-negative, one can replace
I
by
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Together with (
3.6
), this gives

\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\leq\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\beta\left(\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}|}(y,t)\right){\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\eqqcolon\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{J}_{{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}\,,

(7.28)

with

\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\beta\left(\frac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\right){\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\,,

\mathrm{J}_{{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}\coloneqq\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(\beta\left(\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}|}(y,t)\right)-\beta\left(\frac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\right)\right){\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y-x){\,{\rm d}y}\right){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\,.

We observe that

\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta\left(\frac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\right)\chi_{\Omega}*\widetilde{{\varrho}}_{\varepsilon}(x){\,{\rm d}}\,\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\,,

where
\chi_{\Omega}
is the indicator function of
\Omega
and
\widetilde{{\varrho}}(x)\coloneqq{\varrho}(-x)
for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
. Using that
\chi_{\Omega}*\widetilde{{\varrho}}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)
converges to
\chi_{\Omega}(x)

{{H}}^{N}
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
together with Remark
5.5
, we get that
\beta\left(\frac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\right)\chi_{\Omega}*\widetilde{{\varrho}}_{{\varepsilon}}(x)
converges outside a
|Dv|
-negligible set, and thus for
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)
a.e.
(x,t)\in\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}
. Since the integrand of
{\rm J}_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded by
\|\beta\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}
on
\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}
and the measure
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)
is finite, the dominated convergence theorem implies that

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\mathrm{J}_{{\varepsilon}}=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta\left(\frac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\right){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\,.

By the definition (
7.15
) of
\ell
, the area formula and the fact that
f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x))=0
on
\Lambda_{0}
, this can be written as:

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\Omega\cap\Lambda_{+}}\beta\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u(x)|^{2}}}\right)f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(7.29)

As for
\mathrm{J}_{{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}
, using that
\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)(\Lambda_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}})=0
we have the following estimate:

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{\frac{\Omega-x}{{\varepsilon}}}\left(\beta\left(\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}|}(x+{\varepsilon}y^{\prime},t)\right)-\beta\left(\frac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\right)\right){\varrho}(y^{\prime}){\,{\rm d}y}^{\prime}\right){\,{\rm d}}\,\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\int_{[\varrho>0]}\left|\beta\left(\frac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}|}(x+{\varepsilon}y^{\prime},t)\right)-\beta\left(\frac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\right)\right|{\varrho}(y^{\prime}){\,{\rm d}y}^{\prime}\right){\,{\rm d}}\,\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\,.

(7.30)

We next observe that by (
7.20
), (
7.21
), and (
3.4
), for
|Dv|
-a.e.
(x,t)\in\Lambda_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}
and for every
y^{\prime}\in[{\varrho}>0]
, we have

{\varepsilon}\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x+{\varepsilon}y^{\prime},t)={\varepsilon}^{1-N}\int_{u^{-1}(t)}{\varrho}\left(\frac{x-z}{{\varepsilon}}+y^{\prime}\right)\ell(z){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\xrightarrow{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\ell(x)\int_{T_{x}u^{-1}(t)}{\varrho}(y^{\prime}-z){\,{\rm d}}{{H}}^{N-1}(z)\,.

The last integral is strictly positive since
{\varrho}
is strictly positive on a neighbourhood of
y^{\prime}
.
Thus,

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\tfrac{\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}}{|\zeta_{\varepsilon}|}(x+{\varepsilon}y^{\prime},t)=\tfrac{\ell^{t}}{|\ell|}(x)\,.

(7.31)

By the dominated convergence theorem applied in (
7.30
), this gives

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\mathrm{J}_{{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}=0
. In view of (
7.28
) and (
7.29
), we have thus proved that

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\beta(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)*_{x}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\leq\int_{\Omega\cap\Lambda_{+}}\beta\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u(x)|^{2}}}\right)f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

Inserting this estimate in (
7.25
) and taking into account (
7.26
), the conclusion of Proposition
7.8
follows.
∎

7.3
Completion of the proof of the limsup estimate

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

As in the proof of the liminf estimate, we make use of a sequence of continuous decreasing functions
\theta_{k}
from (
3.10
). We write

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\theta_{k}(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})+\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1-\theta_{k}(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta})){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}).

By Lemma
6.1
with
\theta=\theta_{k}
and the fact that
\theta_{k}\leq 1
and then (
5.26
), we can estimate the first term as follows

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\theta_{k}(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})\leq\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv_{0,\delta})=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,Dv)\,.

(7.32)

For the second term, we rely on Proposition
7.8
with
\beta=1-\theta_{k}
on
\Omega
to get

\limsup_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1-\theta_{k}(q^{t}_{{\varepsilon},\delta}))\widehat{f}(x,t,q_{{\varepsilon},\delta}){\,{\rm d}}|Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta}|\\
\leq\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\delta}\int_{\Omega}\left[1-\theta_{k}\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{|\nabla u(x)|^{2}+1}}\right)\right]f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}+\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\delta}\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1-\theta_{k}(q^{t}_{0,\delta}(x,t))){\,{\rm d}\widehat{1}}(Dv_{0,\delta})\eqqcolon\mathrm{R}_{k}\,.

Let us show that
\mathrm{R}_{k}\to 0
for
k\to\infty
. This can be proven by the dominated convergence theorem since
\theta_{k}
converges pointwise to
\chi_{(-\infty,0)}
,
(1-\theta_{k})\leq 1
,
u\in{{E}}(\Omega)
,

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{1}}(Dv_{0,\delta})=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{1}(Dv)+\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\widehat{1}(0,-\delta\alpha^{\prime}){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}={|\Omega|}-\delta\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}\alpha^{\prime}(t){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=(1+\delta\|\alpha\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}){|\Omega|}<\infty\,,

and also the fact that
q^{t}_{0,\delta}(x,t)<0
for
|Dv_{0,\delta}|
-a.e.
(x,t)
. In turn, by the above observation together with (
7.32
), we get the desired result.

∎

8
Approximation on subdomains

8.1
The inner approximation

In the three previous sections, starting from a map
u
defined on
\Lambda
, we have constructed a family of maps
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
defined on a smaller set
\Omega\Subset\Lambda
. We observe however that the definition of the maps
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
does not depend on the particular choice of
\Omega
(provided that
\Omega+B(0,{\varepsilon})\subset\Lambda
). Hence, we can deduce from Propositions
6.2
and
7.1
the following consequence:

Corollary 8.1
.

Suppose
f
satisfies Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

). Let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) with
\alpha
satisfying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
), for
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
. Then for any open
\Lambda^{\prime}\Subset\Lambda
and any
\delta>0
, it holds

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\int_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,Dv)\,.
