The ultimate goal of this section is to derive from Corollary
8.1
a suitable approximation of
u
, see Proposition
8.6
below.
To this aim, we rely on some technical lemmas from
[
16
]
. Let
\Lambda^{\prime}
be an open subset of
\Lambda
and
w:\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a bounded Borel map such that
Dw
is a finite
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measure and for every
x\in\Lambda^{\prime}
,
t\mapsto w(x,t)
is non-increasing and left-continuous.
We further assume that for every
s\in(0,1)
, there exists
M_{s}>0
such that for all
t\geq M_{s}
it holds

\esssup_{x\in\Lambda^{\prime}}w(x,t)<s<\essinf_{x\in\Lambda^{\prime}}w(x,-t).

(8.1)

Recall the definition of the generalized inverse with respect to the second variable
w^{-1}(\cdot,s)
introduced in (
3.2
). For a.e.
s\in(0,1)
, the function
w^{-1}(\cdot,s)
belongs to
BV(\Lambda^{\prime})\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and satisfies
\|w^{-1}(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda^{\prime})}\leq M_{s}
while the indicator function
\chi_{[w>s]}
of the set
[w>s]=\{(x,t)\in\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}:w(x,t)>s\}
agrees with
1_{w^{-1}(\cdot,s)}
a.e. on
\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, see
[
16
, Lemmas 2.4 and 2.5]
. Let
f:\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
satisfy
(

f
red

)
on
\Lambda^{\prime}
. If
\widehat{E}_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(w)<\infty
, then for a.e.
s\in(0,1)
, the map
w^{-1}(\cdot,s)
belongs to
W^{1,1}(\Lambda^{\prime})
while
s\mapsto\widehat{E}_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\chi_{[w>s]})
is measurable, with

\widehat{E}_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(w)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\widehat{E}_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\chi_{[w>s]}){\,{\rm d}}s\geq\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{E}_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{w^{-1}(\cdot,s)}){\,{\rm d}}s=\int_{0}^{1}E_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(w^{-1}(\cdot,s)){\,{\rm d}}s.

(8.2)

The last assertion follows from
[
16
, Lemmas 2.6 and 8.5]
. In spite of the fact that in the latter reference, those results are stated for a Lagrangian which does not depend on
x
, the extension to the
x
dependent case is straightforward.

The following abstract fact on the convergence follows from
[
16
, Lemmas 2.9 and 2.10]
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda^{\prime})\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda^{\prime})
.
For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we consider a bounded Borel map
v_{n}:\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
which is non-increasing and left-continuous with respect to the second variable and satisfies (
8.1
) on
\Lambda^{\prime}
with constants
M_{s}
which do not depend on
n
. Assume further that

Dv_{n}
is a finite
{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
-valued measure and that

v_{n}\to 1_{u}\qquad\text{a.e. in }\ \Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}\,.

Then, setting for every
s\in(0,1)

{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}(x)\coloneqq v_{n}^{-1}(x,s)\,,

(8.3)

it holds for a.e.
s\in(0,1)

{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}\to u\qquad\text{a.e. in }\ \Lambda^{\prime}\,.

If
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v_{n})=\widehat{E}_{\Lambda^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{u})
, then for almost every
s\in(0,1)
up to a subsequence (which may depend on
s
),
({\overline{u}_{n}^{s}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,1}(\Lambda^{\prime})
converges to
u
in
L^{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and satisfies

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{\Lambda^{\prime}}({\overline{u}_{n}^{s}})=E_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(u)\,.

For the rest of this subsection, we require that
\Lambda^{\prime}\Subset\Lambda
and we fix
{\varepsilon}_{0}
such that
\Lambda^{\prime}+B{(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})}\subset\Lambda
. We consider a map
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
.
Recall that
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
is defined for every
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
by (
5.17
) and denote its generalized inverses on
\Lambda^{\prime}
as

u_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{s}(\cdot)\coloneqq v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{-1}(\cdot,s)\,.

(8.4)

Lemma 8.3
.

For every
0<{\varepsilon}<{\varepsilon}_{0}
and every
0<\delta<1
, the map
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
from (
5.17
) satisfies (
8.1
). Moreover,

\|u_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda^{\prime})}\leq M_{s}\,,

(8.5)

where
M_{s}
does not depend on
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0}),\delta\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

Observe that
\alpha
is bounded as any absolutely continuous function, and remember that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\alpha(t)=0
.
Fix
s\in(0,1)
. There exists
M_{s}\geq M=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda)}
such that for every
t\geq M_{s}
, one has
\alpha(t)<s
. Since
1_{u}(x,t)=1
for every
t\leq-M
and a.e.
x\in\Lambda
, it follows that

v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)=1+\delta\alpha(t)>1
for all
(x,t)\in\Lambda^{\prime}\times(-\infty,-M]
.
Using next that
1_{u}(x,t)=0
for every
t\geq M
, we get
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(x,t)=\delta\alpha(t)\leq\alpha(t)<s
for all
(x,t)\in\Lambda^{\prime}\times[M_{s},\infty)
. This proves that
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
satisfies (
8.1
) with this value of
M_{s}
, from which (
8.5
) follows.
∎

To prove that
u_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{s}
is Lipschitz, we need the following fact which readily follows from
[
16
, Lemma 2.8]
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Suppose that
U
is a bounded open set,
w:U\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a bounded Borel map, which is non-increasing and left-continuous with respect to the second variable and satisfies (
8.1
) for
t\geq M_{s}
. Assume further that there exists
\widetilde{C}>0
such that for all
x,y\in U

and all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds

w(x,t+\widetilde{C}|x-y|)\leq w(y,t)\,.

(8.6)

Then
w^{-1}(\cdot,s)
is Lipschitz continuous on
U
and its Lipschitz rank is not larger than
\widetilde{C}
.

Let us prove that
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
satisfies (
8.6
).

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\alpha
from (
5.13
) satisfy (
5.14
). Let
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) with
u\in W^{1,1}(\Lambda)
. For every
\delta>0
, there exists
\overline{C}_{\delta}>0
such that for every
{\varepsilon}>0
and every
x,y\in\Lambda^{\prime}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds that

v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\left(x,t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)-v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(y,t)\leq 0\,.

(8.7)

Proof.

Remember that
M=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda)}
. Let
c_{M}>0
be given by (
5.14
) with
\mathcal{k}_{1}=M
. Set

\overline{C}_{\delta}\coloneqq\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{N})}(\delta c_{M})^{-1}.

By definition of
v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
we have

v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}\left(x,t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)-v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(y,t)=\left(v_{{\varepsilon},0}\left(x,t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)-v_{{\varepsilon},0}(y,t)\right)+\delta\left(\alpha\left(t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)-\alpha(t)\right)\,.

(8.8)

If
t+\frac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|>M
, then

v_{{\varepsilon},0}\left(x,t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)=\int_{\{u\geq t+\frac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\}}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x-z){\,{\rm d}}z=0\,.

Since
v_{{\varepsilon},0}(y,t)\geq 0
and
\alpha
is decreasing, this implies (
8.7
) in this case. Similarly, if
t<-M
, then

v_{{\varepsilon},0}(y,t)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-z){\,{\rm d}}z=1\geq v_{{\varepsilon},0}\left(x,t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)\,,

and (
8.7
) holds in this case as well. In the rest of the proof, we thus assume that

-M\leq t\leq t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\leq M\,.

(8.9)

Since
v_{{\varepsilon},0}(x,\cdot)
is non-increasing, we infer that

\displaystyle v_{{\varepsilon},0}\left(x,t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)-v_{{\varepsilon},0}(y,t)

\displaystyle\leq v_{{\varepsilon},0}(x,t)-v_{{\varepsilon},0}(y,t)=\int_{\{u\geq t\}}({\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(x-z)-{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(y-z)){\,{\rm d}}z

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\int_{\{u\geq t\}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\nabla{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(sx+(1-s)y-z){\,{\rm d}}s\right){\,{\rm d}}z,x-y\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq|x-y|\int_{0}^{1}\left(\int_{\{u\geq t\}}|\nabla{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(sx+(1-s)y-z)|{\,{\rm d}}z\right){\,{\rm d}}s\,,

(8.10)

where the last line holds due to the Fubini theorem and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. We then estimate the innermost integral as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\{u\geq t\}}|\nabla{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(sx+(1-s)y-z)|{\,{\rm d}}z

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}|\nabla{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(sx+(1-s)y-z)|{\,{\rm d}}z=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{N}}}|\nabla{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}}(z)|{\,{\rm d}}z=\tfrac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})}\,.

Inserting the above inequality into (
8.10
), we get

v_{{\varepsilon},0}\left(x,t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)-v_{{\varepsilon},0}(y,t)\leq\tfrac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})}|x-y|\,.

(8.11)

As for the second term in (
8.8
), taking into account (
8.9
), we simply write

\delta\left(\alpha\left(t+\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}|x-y|\right)-\alpha(t)\right)\leq\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}\delta|x-y|(-c_{M})=-\tfrac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla{\varrho}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{N})}|x-y|\,.

(8.12)

By adding (
8.11
) and (
8.12
), we can conclude that (
8.7
) holds true.
∎

The above lemma, together with Lemma
8.4
, implies that
u_{{\varepsilon},\delta}^{s}
is Lipschitz continuous of rank
\tfrac{\overline{C}_{\delta}}{{\varepsilon}}
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Suppose
f
satisfies Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

) on
\Lambda
. Let
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda),

v_{{\varepsilon},\delta}
be given by (
5.17
) with
\alpha
from (
5.13
) satisfying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
). Then, for every open set
\Lambda^{\prime}\Subset\Lambda
, for almost every
s\in(0,1)
, there exist sequences
({\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converging to
(0,0)
and
(u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}({\Lambda^{\prime}})
as in (
8.4
),
which is bounded in
L^{\infty}({\Lambda^{\prime}})
and such that
u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}\to u
in
L^{1}({\Lambda^{\prime}})
as
n\to\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}}(u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}})=E_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}}(u)\,.

(8.13)

Proof.

By Corollary
8.1
, we have

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\widehat{E}_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\widehat{E}_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{u})
, and thus

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\widehat{E}_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v_{{\varepsilon},\delta})=\widehat{E}_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{u})\,.

Given any
(\delta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
decreasing to
0
, one can find a sequence
({\varepsilon}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
also decreasing to
0
and such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}})=\widehat{E}_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}(1_{u})\,.

We also have
v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}\to 1_{u}
in
L^{1}_{loc}({\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}})
and thus, up to a subsequence, also a.e. in
{\Lambda^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
. By Lemma
8.2
for a.e.
s\in(0,1)
there exists a subsequence of
(({\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, such that
u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}\to u
in
L^{1}({\Lambda^{\prime}})
as
n\to\infty
and (
8.13
) holds true.
It follows from (
8.5
) that the sequence
(u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Lambda^{\prime})
.
By Lemma
8.5
and Lemma
8.4
, each
u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}
is Lipschitz continuous on
{\Lambda^{\prime}}
.
∎

The above proposition provides an
inner
approximation, in the sense that the sequence
(u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
which converges to
u
on
\Lambda^{\prime}
does not necessarily
agree with
u
on
\partial\Omega
. In the next section, we modify this approximating sequence to comply with this additional boundary constraint, but only for special domains
\Omega
and just on one part of the boundary. We completely solve this issue in Section
8.3
.

8.2
The boundary approximation on a special open set

In this paragraph, we assume that
N\geq 2
and we explain in Remark
8.8
below how to adapt the arguments to the (simpler) case
N=1
. Denote by
B^{\prime}(x^{\prime},r)
the ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N-1}
, of centre
x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N-1}
and radius
r>0
.
Let
r,b>0
and
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{N-1}\to(-b/2,b/2)
a Lipschitz function.
We define the set
\Sigma\subset{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
by the following formula

\Sigma\coloneqq\{(x^{\prime},x_{N})\in B^{\prime}(0,r)\times(-b,b):\ \psi(x^{\prime})<x_{N}\}\,.

We also define the lower boundary of
\Sigma
as:

\Gamma\coloneqq\{(x^{\prime},\psi(x^{\prime})):\ x^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}(0,r)\}\,.

Lemma 8.7
.

Given a bounded open set
\Lambda\Supset\Sigma
, let
f:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
satisfy Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

) on
\Lambda
,
{\varphi}:\Lambda\to{\mathbb{R}}
a Lipschitz continuous function and
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
. We assume that
u=\varphi
on
\{(x^{\prime},x_{N})\in\Lambda:x_{N}\leq\psi(x^{\prime})\}
.
Then for almost every
s\in(\frac{1}{4},1)
there exists
({\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma)
which is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, converges to
u
in
L^{1}(\Sigma)
, coincides with
{\varphi}
on
\Gamma
, and such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Sigma}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

Proof.

We start with defining for every
{\varkappa}\in(0,b/2)
, the subset

\Sigma_{\varkappa}:=\{(x^{\prime},x_{N})\in B^{\prime}(0,r)\times(-b,b):\ \psi(x^{\prime})<x_{N}<\psi(x^{\prime})+{\varkappa}\}\,.

Let
\alpha\in W^{1,1}({\mathbb{R}})
be a bounded positive function as in (
5.13
) which satisfies (
5.14
) and (
5.15
).
We apply Proposition
8.6
with
\Sigma
playing the role of
\Lambda^{\prime}
(here, we need that
f
and
u
be defined on the larger set
\Lambda
). For a.e.
s\in(0,1)
, we get sequences
(({\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converging to
(0,0)
which leads to define

{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}=u^{s}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}(\cdot)=v^{-1}_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}(\cdot,s).

Note that each
{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}
is Lipschitz continuous on
\Sigma
,
{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}\to u
in
L^{1}(\Sigma)
as
n\to\infty
,
\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}<\infty
, and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Sigma}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(8.14)

Step 1. De-centered convolution.

Our aim is to show that by using the trick of de-centered convolution, one can ensure the smallness condition:
\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}-{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}})}\leq c{\varkappa}_{n}
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
sufficiently large, where
{\varkappa}_{n}\coloneqq{\varepsilon}_{n}/8
.

Actually, when defining
v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}
, we choose the regularization kernel with the additional requirement that

\supp{\varrho}\Subset B^{\prime}(0,\tfrac{1}{8L_{\psi}})\times(\tfrac{1}{4},\tfrac{3}{4})\subset{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\,,

where
L_{\psi}\geq 1
is any fixed number not lower than the Lipschitz rank of
\psi
. There exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
n\geq n_{0}
, the set
\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}+(B^{\prime}(0,\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{8L_{\psi}})\times(-\frac{3{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4},-\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4}))
is contained in
\Lambda
. Let
n\geq n_{0}
and
x=(x^{\prime},x_{N})\in\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}
.
Then for every
y=(y^{\prime},y_{N})\in B^{\prime}(0,\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{8L_{\psi}})\times(\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4},\frac{3{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4})
, one has
x-y\in\Lambda
and

\psi(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})\geq\psi(x^{\prime})-L_{\psi}\tfrac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{8L_{\psi}}{\geq}\psi(x^{\prime})-\tfrac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{8}\geq x_{N}-y_{N}\,,

where the last inequality relies on the fact that
x_{N}\leq\psi(x^{\prime})+{\varkappa}_{n}
and
y_{N}\geq{\varepsilon}_{n}/4
. This implies that
x-y
belongs to the intersection of
\Lambda
with the hypograph of
\psi
where
u
coincides with
\varphi
. This yields for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

{1}_{u}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(x,t)=\int_{B^{\prime}(0,{\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{8L_{\psi}}})\times(\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4},\frac{3{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4})}{1}_{u}(x-y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(y){\,{\rm d}y}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}{1}_{\varphi}(x-y,t){\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(y){\,{\rm d}y}={1}_{\varphi}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(x,t)\,.

(8.15)

In particular, denoting by
L_{{\varphi}}
the Lipschitz constant of
{\varphi}
on
\Lambda
, one has for every
x,\widetilde{x}\in\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}
,

\displaystyle v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}(x,t+L_{\varphi}|x-\widetilde{x}|)

\displaystyle={1}_{u}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(x,t+L_{\varphi}|x-\widetilde{x}|)+\delta_{n}\alpha(t+L_{\varphi}|x-\widetilde{x}|)

\displaystyle={1}_{\varphi}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(x,t+L_{\varphi}|x-\widetilde{x}|)+\delta_{n}\alpha(t+L_{\varphi}|x-\widetilde{x}|)\,.

Using that
L_{{\varphi}}
is the Lipschitz constant of
{\varphi}
, for every
y\in B^{\prime}(0,\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{8L_{\psi}})\times(\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4},\frac{3{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4})
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
it holds
{1}_{\varphi}(x-y,t+L_{\varphi}|x-\widetilde{x}|)\leq{1}_{\varphi}(x-y,t)
. Together with the fact that
\alpha
is non-increasing, one obtains

v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}(x,t+L_{\varphi}|x-\widetilde{x}|)\leq{1}_{\varphi}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(\widetilde{x},t)+\delta_{n}\alpha(t)=v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}(\widetilde{x},t)\,.

In view of Lemma
8.4
, this ensures that

\|\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}})}\leq L_{\varphi}\,.

(8.16)

Finally, using (
8.15
) again, for every
x\in\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}
, we have

{1}_{u}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(x,{\varphi}(x)+L_{\varphi}{\varepsilon}_{n})={1}_{\varphi}*_{x}{\varrho}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(x,{\varphi}(x)+L_{\varphi}{\varepsilon}_{n})=0\ \,,

where the last equality holds true by the very definition of
1_{\varphi}
and since
{\varphi}(x)+L_{\varphi}{\varepsilon}_{n}>{\varphi}(x-y)
for every
y\in B^{\prime}(0,\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{8L_{\psi}})\times(\frac{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4},\frac{3{\varepsilon}_{n}}{4})
.
It follows that

v_{{\varepsilon}_{n},\delta_{n}}(x,{\varphi}(x)+L_{\varphi}{\varepsilon}_{n})=\delta_{n}\alpha(\varphi(x)+L_{\varphi}\varepsilon_{n}).

In particular, for every
s\in(\frac{1}{4},1)
and every
n
so large that
\delta_{n}\leq\frac{1}{4\|\alpha\|_{L^{\infty}}}
, the right-hand side of the above equality is not larger than
s
and this implies that

{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}(x)\leq{\varphi}(x)+L_{\varphi}{\varepsilon}_{n}\,.
Similarly we can prove that

{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}(x)\geq{\varphi}(x)-L_{\varphi}{\varepsilon}_{n}\,.

We can conclude that

\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}-{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}})}\leq L_{\varphi}{\varepsilon}_{n}=8L_{\varphi}{\varkappa}_{n}\,.

(8.17)

Step 2. Truncation argument.
Let
\phi_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Sigma},[0,1])
be
converging pointwise to
\chi_{\overline{\Sigma}\setminus\overline{\Gamma}}
when
n\to\infty
. We further require that

\phi_{n}\equiv 1\textrm{ on }\overline{\Sigma}\setminus\overline{\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}},\qquad\phi_{n}\equiv 0\textrm{ on }\Gamma\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|\nabla\phi_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\leq\tfrac{C}{{\varkappa}_{n}}\,,

(8.18)

for some universal constant
C>0
.
We then define

{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}\coloneqq\phi_{n}{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}+(1-\phi_{n}){\varphi}\,.

By construction
{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma)
and
{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}={\varphi}
on
\Gamma
.
Observe also that the sequence
({\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
.
Moreover,
{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}-{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}=(1-\phi_{n})({\varphi}-{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}})
, so that

\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}-{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}\|_{L^{1}(\Sigma)}\leq\|{\varphi}-{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}})}|\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}|\,,

and by (
8.17
), the left-hand side converges to
0
. Since
({\overline{u}_{n}^{s}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
u
in
L^{1}(\Sigma)
, this implies that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}-u\|_{L^{1}(\Sigma)}=0.

Let us show that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Sigma}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(8.19)

We notice that

\int_{\Sigma}|f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}})-f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}})|{\,{\rm d}x}\\
=\int_{\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}}\left|f\Big(x,\phi_{n}{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}+(1-\phi_{n}){\varphi},\phi_{n}\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}+(1-\phi_{n})\nabla{\varphi}+({\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}-{\varphi})\nabla\phi_{n}\Big)-f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}})\right|{\,{\rm d}x}\,.

By (
8.16
) we have
\|\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}})}\leq L_{\varphi}
and (
8.17
) together with (
8.18
) ensure that
\|({\overline{u}_{n}^{s}}-{\varphi})\nabla\phi_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}})}\leq c{\varkappa}_{n}\frac{C}{{\varkappa}_{n}}\leq cC
. Since
f
is bounded on bounded sets (see Lemma
A.2
) and
\lim_{n\to+\infty}|\Sigma_{{\varkappa}_{n}}|=0
, one gets

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}|f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s,{\varphi}}})-f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{s}})|{\,{\rm d}x}=0\,,

which, together with (
8.14
), implies (
8.19
).
∎

Remark 8.8

(Boundary approximation in the one dimensional case)
.

In the case
N=1
, a special open set is simply an interval
(a,b)
and a neighbourhood of the boundary can be chosen as a (small) interval near the extremities
a
or
b
. Let us focus for instance on the approximation near
a
. We replace the set
\Sigma
by
(a,b)
. In analogy to the higher dimensional case considered above, this amounts to taking
\psi
equal to the constant
a
and
\Gamma=\{a\}
. We also replace
{\mathbb{R}}^{N-1}\times{\mathbb{R}}
by
{\mathbb{R}}
. Accordingly, in Step 1, we remove all the
N-1
dimensional balls. The rest of the proof is essentially the same, so we omit the details.

8.3
Gluing the local approximations

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

We proceed step by step.

Step 1. Covering and partition of unity.
We consider a finite family of open cubes
\{U_{j}\}_{0\leq j<J}
such that

U_{0}\Subset\Omega\Subset\bigcup_{j=0}^{J}U_{j}\Subset\Lambda,\qquad\partial\Omega\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{J}U_{j}\,,

and for every
1\leq j\leq J
, the set
U_{j}\cap\Omega
is isometric to the epigraph of a Lipschitz function. Namely, we assume that there exist an affine isometry
\sigma_{j}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
,
r_{j},b_{j}>0
and a Lipschitz map
\psi_{j}:\ {\mathbb{R}}^{N-1}\to(-b_{j}/2,b_{j}/2)
such that

\sigma_{j}(U_{j})=B(0,{r_{j}})\times(-b_{j},b_{j}),

and

\sigma_{j}(\Omega)\cap\left(B(0,2{r_{j}})\times(-2b_{j},2b_{j})\right)=\{(y,t):\ t>\psi_{j}(y)\}\cap\left(B(0,2{r_{j}})\times(-2b_{j},2b_{j})\right)\,,

\sigma_{j}(\partial\Omega)\cap\left(B(0,2{r_{j}})\times(-2b_{j},2b_{j})\right)=\{(y,t):t=\psi_{j}(y)\}\cap\left(B(0,2{r_{j}})\times(-2b_{j},2b_{j})\right)\,.

We also introduce a partition of unity

\{\Psi_{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq J}
on
\Lambda
subordinate to the covering
(U_{j})_{0\leq j\leq J}
, that is,

\Psi_{j}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U_{j},[0,1])\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}\equiv 1\ \text{ on }\ \overline{\Omega}\,.

Step 2. Penultimate approximation.

We extend
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
by
{\varphi}
on
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, recall that
M=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{N}})}
, and denote

\Sigma_{j}\coloneqq\Omega\cap U_{j}\,.

Note that
f
satisfies Assumptions
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

) on
\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and that
u\in{{E}}(\Lambda)\cap L^{\infty}(\Lambda)
.
Moreover, for every
1\leq j\leq J
,
u=\varphi
on the set
\Lambda_{j}\setminus\Omega
where

\Lambda_{j}\coloneqq\Lambda\cap\sigma_{j}^{-1}\left(B(0,2{r_{j}})\times(-2b_{j},2b_{j})\right)\,.

Observe that
\Sigma_{j}\Subset\Lambda_{j}
and
\Lambda_{j}\setminus\Omega
is the intersection of
\Lambda_{j}
with the hypograph of
\psi_{j}
.
Hence, we can rely on Proposition
8.6
on
U_{0}
and Lemma
8.7
on each
\Sigma_{j}
, for
1\leq j\leq J
(applied with
\Lambda_{j}
instead of
\Lambda
) to deduce that there exist
M^{\prime}>0
and maps
{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{j})
for
0\leq j\leq J
such that

\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{j})}\leq M^{\prime}\,,\quad{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}u\ \text{ in }\ L^{1}(\Sigma_{j})\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{\Sigma_{j}}({\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}})=E_{\Sigma_{j}}(u)\,,

(8.20)

and for
1\leq j\leq J
, each
{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}
agrees with
{\varphi}
on
U_{j}\cap\partial\Omega
. We are thus entitled to extend each
{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}
by
{\varphi}
on
U_{j}\setminus\Omega
, so that each property in (
8.20
) holds true on
U_{j}
instead of
\Sigma_{j}
.

Define
\overline{\alpha}:{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,\infty)
via

\overline{\alpha}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&\textrm{ if }t\leq-M^{\prime}\,,\\
\frac{M^{\prime}-t}{2M^{\prime}}&\textrm{ if }-M^{\prime}\leq t\leq M^{\prime}\,,\\
0&\textrm{ if }t\geq M^{\prime}\,.\end{cases}

(8.21)

Finally, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}},\ \overline{\delta}>0
, one defines for every
(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,t)\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}(x)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t)+\overline{\delta}\overline{\alpha}(t)\,.

Note that even if
{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}
is not defined on the whole
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, the product
\Psi_{j}(x)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t)
is well-defined on
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, since
\Psi_{j}
is compactly supported in
U_{j}
.
For every
\overline{\delta}>0
, we also define the map

\Phi_{\infty,\overline{\delta}}(x,t)\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}(x){1}_{u}(x,t)+\overline{\delta}\overline{\alpha}(t)\,.

On
\overline{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, one has
\Phi_{{\infty},\overline{\delta}}(x,t)=1_{u}(x,t)+\overline{\delta}\overline{\alpha}(t)
.
For every
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
, the map
t\mapsto{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,t)
is non-increasing and left-continuous. Moreover,
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}})
and since
\max_{0\leq j\leq J}\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}\|_{L^{\infty}(U_{j})}\leq M^{\prime}
, for every
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and every
t\geq M^{\prime}
, we have

{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,-t)=1+\overline{\delta}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,t)=0\,.

(8.22)

The same arguments leading to
[
16
, Lemma 6.3]
imply that
D{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}=\lambda_{n}+h_{n,\overline{\delta}}{{{H}}^{N+1}}
, where

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}(x,t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}(x)D1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t)\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{n,\overline{\delta}}(x,t)\coloneqq\left(\sum_{j=0}^{J}\nabla\Psi_{j}(x)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t),\overline{\delta}\overline{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)\right)\,

and
h_{n,\overline{\delta}}(x,t)=(0,0)
for every
x\in\Omega
and
|t|>M^{\prime}
. Furthermore, for every
\overline{\delta}_{0}>0
it holds

\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}|\lambda_{n}|({\mathbb{R}^{N}}\times{\mathbb{R}})<\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},\,\overline{\delta}<\overline{\delta}_{0}}\|h_{n,\overline{\delta}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})}<\infty\,.

(8.23)

Let us establish some additional properties of
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}
.

1.

(Limits of
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}
). We observe that for every
w_{1},w_{2}\in L^{1}(\Omega)
,

\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}|1_{w_{1}}-1_{w_{2}}|{\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=\int_{\Omega}|w_{1}-w_{2}|{\,{\rm d}x}.

Using that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}-u\|_{L^{1}(U_{j})}=0
, for every
\overline{\delta}>0
, we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}-\Phi_{{\infty},\overline{\delta}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})}=0
.

2.

(Limit of
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}})
). We show that
for every
\overline{\delta}>0
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}})=E_{\Omega}(u)\,
.

By using that the measures
\lambda_{n}
and
h_{n,\overline{\delta}}{{{H}}^{N+1}}
are mutually singular, (
8.21
), and the fact that
h_{n,\overline{\delta}}
vanishes on
\Omega\times({\mathbb{R}}\setminus(-M^{\prime},M^{\prime}))
, one gets

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}

\displaystyle{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,D{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}})=\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}}\widehat{f}(x,t,\lambda_{n})+\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}(x,t,h_{n,\overline{\delta}}{{{H}}^{N+1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\,{\rm d}\widehat{f}}\left(x,t,\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}D1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}\right)+\frac{\overline{\delta}}{2M^{\prime}}\int_{-M^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}\int_{U_{j}}f\left(x,t,\frac{2M^{\prime}}{\overline{\delta}}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{J}\nabla\Psi_{j}1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}\right]\right){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{J}\int_{\Omega}\Psi_{j}f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}){\,{\rm d}x}+\frac{\overline{\delta}}{2M^{\prime}}\int_{-M^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega}f\left(x,t,\frac{2M^{\prime}}{\overline{\delta}}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{J}\nabla\Psi_{j}1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}\right]\right){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}\eqqcolon\mathcal{K}_{1,n}^{\Omega}+\mathcal{K}_{2,n}^{\Omega}\,,

(8.24)

where the last inequality relies on the convexity of
\widehat{f}
with respect to the last variable and (
5.11
).

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}-u\|_{L^{1}(U_{j})}=0
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\sum_{j=0}^{J}\nabla\Psi_{j}1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}-\sum_{j=0}^{J}\nabla\Psi_{j}1_{u}\right\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}=0.

Since
\sum_{j=0}^{J}\nabla\Psi_{j}1_{u}=1_{u}\nabla\left(\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}\right)=0
on
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}
, it follows that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\sum_{j=0}^{J}\nabla\Psi_{j}1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}=0
. By the dominated convergence theorem, we get

\mathcal{K}_{2,n}^{\Omega}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\frac{\overline{\delta}}{2M^{\prime}}\int_{-M^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega}f(x,t,0){\,{\rm d}x}{\,{\rm d}t}=0\,.

(8.25)

On the other hand, to pass to the limit in
\mathcal{K}_{1,n}^{\Omega}
, we start from the observation that for every
0\leq j\leq J
, we have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}E_{U_{j}}({\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}})=E_{U_{j}}(u)
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}-u\|_{L^{1}(U_{j})}=0
. Using that
f
is superlinear with respect to the last variable, we deduce from the Dunford-Pettis theorem that

({\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
weakly converges to
u
in
W^{1,1}(U_{j})
. By weak lower semicontinuity, it follows that for every open subset
A\subset U_{j}
, one has

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{A}f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}){\,{\rm d}x}\geq\int_{A}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}.

(8.26)

Applying (
8.26
) with
A=U_{j}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
, we get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}E_{U_{j}\cap\Omega}({\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}})=\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{U_{j}}({\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}})-\liminf_{n\to\infty}E_{U_{j}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}({\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}})\leq E_{U_{j}}(u)-E_{U_{j}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}(u)=E_{U_{j}\cap\Omega}(u).

Relying on (
8.26
) with
A=U_{j}\cap\overline{\Omega}
, this implies that
\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{U_{j}\cap\Omega}({\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}})=E_{U_{j}\cap\Omega}(u)
.

Hence, one can apply
[
2
, Proposition 1.80]
to the sequence of finite Borel measures on
U_{j}\cap\Omega
defined by

\mu_{n}(A)=\int_{A}f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}},\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}){\,{\rm d}x}
,
to conclude that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{U_{j}\cap\Omega}\Psi_{j}(x)f(x,{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}(x),\nabla{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}(x)){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{U_{j}\cap\Omega}\Psi_{j}(x)f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}.

Thus, using (
8.24
) and (
8.25
), we get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}({\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}})\leq\sum_{j=0}^{J}\int_{U_{j}\cap\Omega}\Psi_{j}(x)f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}=E_{\Omega}(u)\,,

where in the last equality we used that
\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}(x)=1
and that
\Psi_{j}
equals
0
outside
U_{j}
.
On the other hand, using that
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}
converges to
\Phi_{{\infty},\overline{\delta}}
in
L^{1}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
and the fact that
\sup_{n}|D{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}|(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})<\infty
by (
8.23
), we deduce that
D{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}
weakly-
*
converges to
D\Phi_{{\infty},\overline{\delta}}
. The Reschetnyak
lower semicontinuity theorem thus implies that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}({\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}})\geq\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{{\infty},\overline{\delta}})=E_{\Omega}(u).

It follows that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}({\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}})=E_{\Omega}(u)
.

Step 3. Construction of the final approximation.
For every
\overline{\delta}\in(0,\infty)
, property
(
v
-b)
implies that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}({\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}})=E_{\Omega}(u)
while by property
(
v
-a)
,
({\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
1_{u}+\overline{\delta}\overline{\alpha}
in
L^{1}(\Omega\times(-M^{\prime},M^{\prime}))
. Hence,

\lim_{\overline{\delta}\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}({\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}})=E_{\Omega}(u)\textrm{ and }\lim_{\overline{\delta}\to 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\|{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}-(1_{u}+\overline{\delta}\overline{\alpha})\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times(-M^{\prime},M^{\prime}))}=0.

Given any sequence
(\overline{\delta}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
tending to
0
, we can thus find a subsequence
(n_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that

{\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}})<\infty},\quad\lim_{i\to+\infty}\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}})=E_{\Omega}(u)\,,\ \textrm{ and }\ \lim_{i\to+\infty}\|\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}}-1_{u}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times(-M^{\prime},M^{\prime}))}=0\,.

(8.27)

Up to an extraction, one can assume that
\lim_{i\to+\infty}\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}}(x,t)=1_{u}(x,t)
for a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega\times(-M^{\prime},M^{\prime})
.
For every
s\in(0,1)
, we introduce the maps
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}
with the use of the generalized inverse with respect to the second variable of
\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}}
constructed in Step 2, i.e.,

{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}(x)\coloneqq\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}}^{-1}(x,s)\,.

We establish some properties of
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}
following the same steps as in Section
8.1
.

1.

(Boundedness of (
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}
)).
By (
8.22
) and (
3.2
) we infer that
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}
belongs to
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, with
\|{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq M^{\prime}
.
By the fact that
\widehat{E}_{\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}})<\infty
and (
8.2
), one gets that for a.e.
s
it holds
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
and
E_{\Omega}({{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}})<\infty
.

2.

(Limits of (
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}
)). From Lemma
8.2
with
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Omega
and (
8.27
), we deduce that for a.e.
s\in(0,1)
, there exists a subsequence (we do not relabel) such that
({{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}})_{i}\to u
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
and
E_{\Omega}({{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}})\to E_{\Omega}(u)
as
i\to\infty
.

3.

(
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}\in{\rm Lip}(\Omega)
).
We only need to prove that for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}},\ \overline{\delta}\in(0,1)
, there exists
C>0
(which may depend on
n,\overline{\delta}
) such that for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and every
x,y\in\Omega
, it holds

{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,t+C|x-y|)\leq{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(y,t)\,,

(8.28)

which in view of Lemma
8.4
ensures that each
(\Phi_{n_{i},\overline{\delta}_{i}})^{-1}(\cdot,s)={{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}
is Lipschitz continuous.

We start from the fact that for all
j=0,\dots,J
functions
{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}
are Lipschitz continuous on
U_{j}
with constant
C_{n}
. Then for every
x,y\in U_{j}
, every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
c\geq C_{n}
we have

1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t+c|x-y|)\leq 1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(y,t)\,

so that

\Psi_{j}(x)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t+c|x-y|)\leq\Psi_{j}(x)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(y,t)

and thus

\Psi_{j}(x)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t+c|x-y|)\leq\Psi_{j}(y)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(y,t)+\|\nabla\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}|x-y|\,.

(8.29)

Inequality (
8.29
) remains true when
x\not\in U_{j}
(because in that case, the left-hand side vanishes) and when
y\not\in U_{j}
(because in that case, it follows from the inequality
\Psi_{j}(x)\leq\Psi_{j}(y)+\|\nabla\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}|x-y|=\|\nabla\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}|x-y|
).

Let
C\coloneqq C_{n}+\frac{2M^{\prime}}{\overline{\delta}}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\|\nabla\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
Then for every
x,y\in\Omega
,

\sum_{j=0}^{J}\Psi_{j}(x)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(x,t+C|x-y|)\leq\sum_{j=0}^{J}\left(\Psi_{j}(y)1_{{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}}(y,t)+\|\nabla\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}|x-y|\right)\,.

Hence, using the definition of
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}
,

{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,t+C|x-y|)\leq{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(y,t)+\sum_{j=0}^{J}\|\nabla\Psi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}|x-y|+\overline{\delta}(\overline{\alpha}(t+C|x-y|)-\overline{\alpha}(t))\,.

(8.30)

We are now in position to prove (
8.28
) for we are in one of the following three cases. If
t+C|x-y|\geq M^{\prime}
, then by (
8.22
),
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,t+C|x-y|)=0\leq{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(y,t)\,.

If
t\leq-M^{\prime}
,
then by (
8.22
) again,
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(x,t+C|x-y|)\leq 1+\overline{\delta}={\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}(y,t)\,.

Otherwise
-M^{\prime}\leq t\leq t+C|x-y|\leq M^{\prime}
and in that case
\overline{\alpha}(t+C|x-y|)-\overline{\alpha}(t)=-\frac{C|x-y|}{2M^{\prime}}.

In view of (
8.30
) and the definition of
C
, this yields (
8.28
), which implies that
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}
is Lipschitz continuous on
\Omega
.

4.

(
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}={\varphi}
on
\partial\Omega
).
For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and every
1\leq j\leq J
, one has
{\overline{u}_{n}^{{\varphi},j}}={\varphi}
on
U_{j}\cap\partial\Omega
. Hence,
{\Phi_{n,\overline{\delta}}}={1}_{{\varphi}}+\overline{\delta}\overline{\alpha}\text{ on }\partial\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\,
. Thus, for every
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
s>\overline{\delta}_{i}
, it holds
{{u}_{i}^{s,{\varphi}}}={\varphi}
on
\partial\Omega
.

Step 4. Conclusion.

For a.e.
s\in(0,1)
, we have constructed a sequence
(u^{s,\varphi}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
, which is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, converges to
u
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
and satisfies
\lim_{i\to+\infty}E_{\Omega}(u^{s,\varphi}_{i})=E_{\Omega}(u)
. Moreover, for every
i
sufficiently large, one has
\overline{\delta}_{i}<s
, which implies that
u^{s,\varphi}_{i}
coincides with
\varphi
on
\partial\Omega
.

∎

9
Proofs of the main results

9.1
Proof of Theorem
3

Before giving the proof of Theorem
3
, we present a couple of auxiliary facts. In the next lemma, we extend the integrand
f
to a slightly larger domain.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
be bounded and Lipschitz, and let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function convex with respect to the last variable which satisfies (

H
conv

) on
\Omega
. Then there exists an open set
\Lambda\Supset\Omega
and a Carathéodory function
g:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
convex with respect to the last variable which satisfies (

H
conv

) on
\Lambda
and coincides with
f
on
\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.

Proof.

We first extend
f
on
\overline{\Omega}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
by setting
f(x,t,\xi)=0
for every
x\in\partial\Omega
and every
(t,\xi)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
Since
\Omega
is bounded and Lipschitz, there exists an open set
\Omega_{0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that
\partial\Omega\subset\Omega_{0}
and a biLipschitz map
\Pi_{0}:\Omega_{0}\to\Omega_{0}
which agrees with the identity on
\partial\Omega
, and such that
\Pi_{0}(\Omega_{0}\setminus\overline{\Omega})=\Omega_{0}\cap\Omega
, see e.g.
[
26
, Theorem 3.1.]
.
We then set
\Lambda\coloneqq\Omega\cup\Omega_{0}
and

\Pi:x\in\Lambda\mapsto\begin{cases}x&\textrm{ if }x\in\overline{\Omega},\\
\Pi_{0}(x)&\textrm{ if }x\in\Omega_{0}\setminus\overline{\Omega}.\end{cases}

Since
\Pi_{0}
is Lipschitz continuous on
\Omega_{0}\setminus\Omega
and coincides with the identity on
\partial\Omega
, we deduce that
\Pi
is Lispchitz continuous on
\Lambda
.
We also observe that
\Pi
and
(\Pi|_{\Lambda\setminus\overline{\Omega}})^{-1}
map negligible sets on negligible sets (here, we use that
\Pi_{0}
is a biLipschitz homeomorphism).

We then define
g(x,t,\xi)=f(\Pi(x),t,\xi)
. This is a Carathéodory function on
\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
which is convex with respect to the last variable.
Moreover, one has for every
x\in\Lambda
and every
{\varepsilon}>0
,

g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)=\essinf_{y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})\cap\Lambda}f(\Pi(y),t,\xi)=\essinf_{z\in\Pi(B(x,{\varepsilon})\cap\Lambda)}f(z,t,\xi)\,.

(9.1)

Denoting by
{\mathcal{a}}
the Lipschitz constant of
\Pi
, one has
\Pi(B(x,{\varepsilon})\cap\Lambda)\subset B(\Pi(x),{\mathcal{a}}{\varepsilon})\cap\overline{\Omega}\,.
Since
\partial\Omega
is negligible, this implies that

g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)\geq\essinf_{z\in B(\Pi(x),{\mathcal{a}}{\varepsilon})\cap\Omega}f(z,t,\xi)=f_{B(\Pi(x),{\mathcal{a}}{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)

and thus

\left(f_{B(\Pi(x),{\mathcal{a}}{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)\leq\left(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)\,.

Let
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
. If
t\in(-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1})
and
(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi){+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}
, then

\left(f_{B(\Pi(x),{\mathcal{a}}{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi){+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}}\leq{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}=\frac{\mathcal{k}_{2}{\mathcal{a}}^{N}}{({\mathcal{a}}{\varepsilon})^{N}}\,.

Since
f
satisfies (

H
conv

), there exists
C_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}>0
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2}{\mathcal{a}}^{N})
such that for a.e.
x\in\Pi^{-1}(\Omega)=\Lambda\setminus\partial\Omega
, it holds

f(\Pi(x),t,\xi)\leq C_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}\left(\left(f_{B(\Pi(x),{\mathcal{a}}{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)+1\right)\leq C_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}\left(\left(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)+1\right)\,.

Since
{|\partial\Omega|}=0
, this proves that for a.e.
x\in\Lambda
,

g(x,t,\xi)\leq C_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}\left(\left(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)+1\right)\,.

We can conclude that
g
satisfies (

H
conv

) on
\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
.
∎

In the view of the next lemma, we can assume without loss of generality that the function
u\in W^{1,p}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
to be approximated, is bounded on
\Omega
. Remember that this automatically holds true when
N=1
, due to the embedding
W^{1,1}(\Omega)\subset L^{\infty}(\Omega)
in that case.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
be a bounded open set, and let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function which is convex with respect to the last variable. We further assume that there exist
{\vartheta}\in[1,\infty]
,
a\in L^{{\vartheta}}(\Omega)
and
t_{0}>0
which satisfy (
1.8
) for some
p\geq 1
.
If
u\in{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)
,
then there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset{{E}_{\varphi}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, which converges to
u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, and such that
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)
.

Proof.

We set
u_{n}\coloneqq\min(n,\max(-n,u)).

Then,
\|u_{n}-u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\to 0
when
n\to\infty
, and for
n\geq\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}}
we have
u_{n}\in W^{1,1}_{{\varphi}}(\Omega)
.
Moreover,

E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=\int_{\Omega}f(x,u_{n},\nabla u_{n}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\{|u|<n\}}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}+\int_{\{u\geq n\}}f(x,n,0){\,{\rm d}x}+\int_{\{u\leq-n\}}f(x,-n,0){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(9.2)

By the monotone convergence theorem,

\int_{\{|u|<n\}}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

Using the assumption (
1.8
) and the Hölder inequality, for every
n\geq t_{0}
,

\int_{\{u\geq n\}}f(x,n,0){\,{\rm d}x}\leq n^{p^{*}/{\vartheta}^{\prime}}\int_{\{u\geq n\}}a(x){\,{\rm d}x}\leq n^{p^{*}/{\vartheta}^{\prime}}|\{u\geq n\}|^{1/{\vartheta}^{\prime}}\left(\int_{\{u\geq n\}}a(x)^{\vartheta}{\,{\rm d}x}\right)^{1/{\vartheta}}\,.

By the Chebyshev inequality
|\{u\geq n\}|\leq{n^{-p^{*}}}\|u\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\Omega)}^{p^{*}},
so the right-hand side is finite since
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\subset L^{p^{*}}(\Omega)
.
Hence,

\int_{\{u\geq n\}}f(x,n,0){\,{\rm d}x}\leq\|u\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\Omega)}^{p^{*}/{\vartheta}^{\prime}}\left(\int_{\{u\geq n\}}a(x)^{\vartheta}{\,{\rm d}x}\right)^{1/{\vartheta}}\,.

By the monotone convergence theorem again, we get

\int_{\{u\geq n\}}f(x,n,0){\,{\rm d}x}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0\,.

A similar analysis holds on the sublevel sets
\{u\leq-n\}
.
In conjunction with (
9.2
), this yields
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}E_{\Omega}(u_{n})=E_{\Omega}(u)
.
∎

The assumption (

H
conv

) and (

H
iso

) enjoy the following stability property.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function convex with respect to the last variable and
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
be convex. If
f
satisfies (

H
conv

), then so does
f+h
. If
f
satisfies (

H
iso

), then so does
f+h
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
and
{\varepsilon}>0
.
Since
f\leq f+h
, for every
x\in\Omega
,
t\in[-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1}]
and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,

\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)\leq\left((f+h)_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)\,.

(9.3)

Hence, if
\xi
satisfies

\left((f+h)_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}\leq\frac{\mathcal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{N}}\,,

then
(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}
. If
f
satisfies (

H
conv

), this implies that for a.e.
x\in\Omega
,

f(x,t,\xi)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}((f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+1)\,,

for some
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}\geq 1
.
It follows that

f(x,t,\xi)+h(\xi)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}((f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+1)+h(\xi)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}((f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+h(\xi)+1)\,.

We can complete the proof by observing that
(f_{B}^{-})^{**}+h\leq f_{B}^{-}+h=(f+h)_{B}^{-}
and thus

(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+h(\xi)\leq((f+h)_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)\,.

The conclusion for condition (

H
iso

) can be obtained in essentially the same way.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3
.

We first consider the case
p=1
.
Let
f
and
u
as in the statement. By Lemma
9.2
and a diagonal argument, we may assume that
u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
Using Lemma
9.1
, we introduce an extension
f_{1}
of
f
on
\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, which satisfies (

H
conv

) on
\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
Note that by
[
31
, Theorem VIII.1.3 and Lemma VIII.1.2]
for
\nabla u\in L^{1}(\Lambda)
there exists a superlinear convex function
h:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
h(\nabla u)\in L^{1}(\Lambda)
and
h(0)=0
. Let
g_{0}
and
g
be the functions defined for a.e.
x\in\Lambda
and all
(t,\xi)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
by

g_{0}(x,t,\xi)=(f_{1}(x,t,\xi)-f_{1}(x,t,0))_{+}\,,\quad g(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq g_{0}(x,t,\xi)+h(\xi)+\sqrt{1+|\xi|^{2}}-1\,.

Then
g
is a Carathéodory non-negative function which is convex with respect to the last variable. Moreover,
g(x,t,\xi)\geq h(\xi)
and
g(x,t,0)=0
for every
(x,t,\xi)\in\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
.
From Lemma
A.3
, we deduce that the function
g_{0}
satisfies (

H
conv

), while Lemma
9.3
allows to conclude that the same property holds for
g
. Hence,
g
satisfies
(

f
red

)
and (

H
conv

).
Since
E_{\Omega}(u)<\infty
, one also has
\int_{\Omega}g(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}<\infty
. By Proposition
5.3
applied to
g
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\to u
as
n\to\infty
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
,
\sup_{n}\|u_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}g(x,u_{n},\nabla u_{n}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}g(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(9.4)

Since
g(x,u_{n}(x),\nabla u_{n}(x))\geq h(\nabla u_{n}(x))
,
the Dunford–Pettis theorem implies that, up to a subsequence,
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
weakly converges to
u
in
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
. By the weak lower semicontinuity of the functionals in
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, we know that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}g_{0}(x,u_{n},\nabla u_{n}){\,{\rm d}x}\geq\int_{\Omega}g_{0}(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,,\quad\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}h(\nabla u_{n}){\,{\rm d}x}\geq\int_{\Omega}h(\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}

and

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u_{n}|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}\geq\int_{\Omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(9.5)

In view of (
9.4
), this implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}g_{0}(x,u_{n},\nabla u_{n}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}g_{0}(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,,\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u_{n}|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}{\,{\rm d}x}\,.

The last equality above and the weak convergence of
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
then imply the strong convergence in
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, see e.g.
[
35
, Section 1.3.4, Proposition 1]
. Since
g_{0}
is non-negative, it follows from the first equality that
(g_{0}(\cdot,u_{n},\nabla u_{n}))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
g_{0}(\cdot,u,\nabla u)
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
.
We note that

f(x,u_{n}(x),\nabla u_{n}(x))\leq g_{0}(x,u_{n}(x),\nabla u_{n}(x))+f(x,u_{n}(x),0)\leq g_{0}(x,u_{n}(x),\nabla u_{n}(x))+\max_{|t|\leq\sup_{n}\|u_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}}f(x,t,0)\,,

and that the last term in the right-hand side is bounded by Lemma
A.2
. The Vitali convergence theorem thus implies

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}f(x,u_{n}(x),\nabla u_{n}(x)){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

This completes the proof when
p=1
.

When
p>1
, we replace the term
h(\xi)+\sqrt{1+|\xi|^{2}}-1
by
|\xi|^{p}
; that is, we introduce the function:

\overline{g}(x,t,\xi)=f(x,t,\xi)+|\xi|^{p}\,,

and apply the above arguments to
\overline{g}
instead of
g
. Observe in particular that since
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, we still have
\int_{\Omega}\overline{g}(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}<\infty
. We thus obtain an approximating sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
which satisfies (
9.4
) with
\overline{g}
instead of
g
. By
p
-coercivity of
\overline{g}
, this implies that
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
weakly converges to
u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. By the above lower semicontinuity argument which leads to (
9.5
), we deduce that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u_{n}|^{p}{\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}{\,{\rm d}x}.

By uniform convexity of the
L^{p}
norm, it follows that
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
strongly converges to
u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
.
The end of the proof can now be repeated without changes, which completes the case
p>1
.
∎

9.2
Proofs of Theorems
2
and
5

Having proven Theorem
3
, we may now infer Theorems
2
and
5
.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

It is enough to show that our regime implies (

H
conv

). Indeed, once we show (

H
conv

) the result holds due to Theorem
3
.

Fix
L=(L_{1},L_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
and
t\in[-L_{1},L_{1}]
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that the corresponding constant
C_{L}
in (

H

Δ
2

) is not lower than
1
. Since
f(x,t,0)=0
, the convexity of
f
implies that

f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)\leq{L_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}\implies f(x,t,\tfrac{1}{C_{L}}\xi)\leq f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\xi)+1\,.

(9.6)

We apply
[
41
, Theorem 1.2]
to get

(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)\leq{L_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}\implies f(x,t,\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{C}_{L}}\xi)\leq(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+1\,,

for some
\widetilde{C}_{L}>1
. Since
f(x,t,\cdot)
satisfies the
\Delta_{2}
condition, there exists
\overline{C}_{L}>1
such that

(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)\leq{L_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}\implies f(x,t,\xi)\leq\overline{C}_{L}[(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+1]\,,

(9.7)

which implies (

H
conv

).

∎

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We simply observe that by Theorem
4
,
f
satisfies condition (

H
conv

), in the isotropic as well as in the orthotropic case. Therefore, Theorem
3
gives the conclusion.

∎

Appendix

A
Auxiliaries

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded Lipschitz open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
. Let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to[0,\infty)
be a Carathéodory function such that there exists a function
g:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to[0,\infty)
satisfying all the assumptions of Theorem
3
with some
p\geq 1
. Assume that there exist a constant
C>0
and a non-negative function
\vartheta\in L^{1}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}})
such that (
1.9
) holds true.
Let
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz continuous. Then, for every
u\in W^{1,p}_{{\varphi}}(\Omega)
such that
\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}<\infty
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\varphi}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
as
n\to\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}f(x,u_{n},\nabla u_{n}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

Proof.

Let us take any
u\in W^{1,p}_{{\varphi}}(\Omega)
such that
\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}<\infty
. Then by (
1.9
) we know that
\int_{\Omega}g(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\leq\int_{\Omega}C(f(x,u,\nabla u)+\vartheta(x)){\,{\rm d}x}<\infty
. As
g
satisfies assumptions of Theorem
3
, we infer that there exists
(u_{n})_{n}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{{\varphi}}(\Omega)
converging to
u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, and such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}g(x,u_{n},\nabla u_{n}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}g(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

(A.1)

Since
(u_{n})_{n}
converges in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, we pick its subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
such that
u_{n_{k}}\to u
and
\nabla u_{n_{k}}\to\nabla u
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
. As
g
is continuous with respect to its last two variables, we get that
g(x,u_{n_{k}}(x),\nabla u_{n_{k}}(x))\to g(x,u(x),\nabla u(x))
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
. This with (
A.1
) and the Brezis-Lieb lemma implies that
(g(\cdot,u_{n_{k}},\nabla u_{n_{k}}))_{k}
converges in
L^{1}
to
g(\cdot,u,\nabla u)
. By the Vitali convergence theorem,
(g(\cdot,u_{n_{k}},\nabla u_{n_{k}}))_{k}
is uniformly integrable, and by (
1.9
) we also get that
(f(\cdot,u_{n_{k}},\nabla u_{n_{k}}))_{k}
is uniformly integrable. As
f
is continuous with respect to its last two variables, we have that
f(x,u_{n_{k}}(x),\nabla u_{n_{k}}(x))\to f(x,u(x),\nabla u(x))
for a.e.
x
. We finish the proof by noticing that the Vitali convergence theorem implies

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}f(x,u_{n_{k}},\nabla u_{n_{k}}){\,{\rm d}x}=\int_{\Omega}f(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}\,.

∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
be a bounded open set, and let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{[0,\infty)}
be a Carathéodory function which satisfies either (

H
conv

) or (

H
iso

). Then
for every
m>0
, there exists
K_{m}>0
such that for a.e.
x\in\Omega
,

\max_{|(t,\xi)|\leq m}f(x,t,\xi)\leq K_{m}\,.

(A.2)

Proof.

For a.e.
y\in\Omega
, the function
(t,\xi)\mapsto f(y,t,\xi)
is continuous. Let
A_{m}\coloneqq\essinf_{y\in\Omega}\max_{|(t,\xi)|\leq m}f(y,t,\xi)
.
Setting
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})
with
\mathcal{k}_{1}=m
and
\mathcal{k}_{2}=(A_{m}+m^{\max(p,N)})(\diam\Omega)^{N}
, it follows either from (

H
conv

) or from (

H
iso

) with
{\varepsilon}=\diam\Omega
that there exists
C_{\mathcal{k}}>0
such that for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
|(t,\xi)|\leq m
,

f(x,t,\xi)\leq C_{\mathcal{k}}(A_{m}+1)\,.\qed

Lemma A.3
.

Let
f:\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to[0,\infty)
be a Carathéodory function convex with respect to the last variable and
g(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq(f(x,t,\xi)-f(x,t,0))_{+}
.
Then map
g
satisfies (

H
conv

), if and only if
f
satisfies (

H
conv

). Moreover,
g
satisfies (

H
iso

), if and only if
f
satisfies (

H
iso

).

Proof.

Let us prove the result for (

H
conv

). We observe that
g
is a Carathéodory non-negative function which is convex with respect to the last variable.
Assume first that (

H
conv

) holds for
f
.
Let
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,\diam\Omega]
. For a.e.
x\in\Omega
, for every
t\in(-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1})
,
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,

f(x,t,\xi)\leq g(x,t,\xi)+f(x,t,0)\leq g(x,t,\xi)+\max_{|t|\leq\mathcal{k}_{1}}f(x,t,0)\leq g(x,t,\xi)+K_{\mathcal{k}_{1}},

(A.3)

where the constant
K_{\mathcal{k}_{1}}
in the last inequality is given by Lemma
A.2
.
Hence,

(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)\leq(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+K_{\mathcal{k}_{1}}\,.

(A.4)

Assuming further that
(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}
, we can apply (

H
conv

) to
f
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2}+(\diam\Omega)^{N}K_{\mathcal{k}_{1}})
. We infer that there exists
{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}\geq 1
such that

f(x,t,\xi)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}((f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+1)\,.

Using that
g(x,t,\xi)\leq f(x,t,\xi)
in the left-hand side and (
A.4
) in the right-hand side,
we obtain

g(x,t,\xi)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{k}}}((g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi)+K_{\mathcal{k}_{1}}+1)\;,

which shows that
g
satisfies (

H
conv

).
This completes the proof of the
if
part of the statement.

Conversely, assume that (

H
conv

) holds for
g
.
Let
\mathcal{k}=(\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,\diam\Omega]
. For a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
t\in(-\mathcal{k}_{1},\mathcal{k}_{1})
, if
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
satisfies
(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot))^{**}(\xi){+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}
, then since
g\leq f
, one has

\left(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}{+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}}\leq\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}{+|\xi|^{\max(p,N)}}\leq\mathcal{k}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}\,.

Using that
g
satisfies (

H
conv

), we deduce that there exists
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}\geq 1
such that

g(x,t,\xi)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}\left(\left(g_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)+1\right)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{k}}\left(\left(f_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}^{-}(t,\cdot)\right)^{**}(\xi)+1\right).

We can conclude by (
A.3
) that
f
satisfies (

H
conv

). The proof for (

H
iso

) is essentially the same and we omit it.
∎

B
Examples

We provide an example illustrating that condition (

H
conv

) and conditions (

H
iso

), (

H
iso

0

) are essentially different when considered in the fully anisotropic setting.

Example B.1
.

Let
N=2
and let the function
f:B(0,2)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to[0,\infty)
be defined as

f(x,\xi)\coloneqq|\langle x,\xi\rangle|^{4}+|\xi|\,.

Then
f
is of at least linear growth with respect to the last variable and vanishes for
\xi=0
. Moreover, for every
L\in(0,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C_{L}>0
such that for every
{\varepsilon}>0
, for every
x\in B(0,2)
and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, it holds

|\xi|\leq L{\varepsilon}^{-1}\Longrightarrow f(x,\xi)\leq C_{L}\left(f(y,\xi)+1\right)\,.

(B.1)

However,
f
does not satisfy the condition
(H^{\text{conv}})
with
p=1
.

Proof.

Note that whenever
|\xi|\leq L{\varepsilon}^{-1}
, then for every
x,y
such that
|x-y|<{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle f(x,\xi)\leq\left(|\langle y,\xi\rangle|+|\langle x-y,\xi\rangle|\right)^{4}+|\xi|\leq\left(|\langle y,\xi\rangle|+L\right)^{4}+|\xi|\leq 8|\langle y,\xi\rangle|^{4}+8L^{4}+|\xi|\leq(8L^{4}+8)(f(y,\xi)+1)\,.

Hence, (
B.1
) holds true with
C_{L}=8L^{4}+8
. Let us now fix any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
, and take
x=(0,{\varepsilon}^{1/2})
. We consider the ball
B(x,{\varepsilon})
. Observe that for any
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

f\left((-{\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{1/2}),({\varepsilon}^{-1/2}a,a)\right)=(1+{\varepsilon}^{-1})^{1/2}|a|=f\left(({\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{1/2}),(-{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}a,a)\right)\,.

(B.2)

Let us denote
g\coloneqq(f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})})^{**}
. By convexity of
g
, we now get for any
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
that

\displaystyle g\left((0,a)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}g\left(\left({\varepsilon}^{-1/2}a,a\right)\right)+\tfrac{1}{2}g\left(\left(-{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}a,a\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}f\left((-{\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{1/2}),({\varepsilon}^{-1/2}a,a)\right)+\tfrac{1}{2}f\left(({\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{1/2}),(-{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}a,a)\right)=(1+{\varepsilon}^{-1})^{1/2}|a|\leq 2{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}|a|\,,

(B.3)

where in the equality we used (
B.2
). Let us now take
\xi=(0,{\varepsilon}^{-1})
. By (
B.3
), we get

g(\xi)+|\xi|^{2}\leq 2{\varepsilon}^{-3/2}+{\varepsilon}^{-2}\leq 3{\varepsilon}^{-2}\,.

(B.4)

On the other hand, again by (
B.3
), we get

f(x,\xi)=|\langle x,\xi\rangle|^{4}+|\xi|={\varepsilon}^{-2}+{\varepsilon}^{-1}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}\left(g(\xi)+1\right)\,.

(B.5)

The two inequalities (
B.4
) and (
B.5
) imply that
f
cannot satisfy (

H
conv

).
∎

Let us infer the absence of the Lavrentiev gap for a generalized double phase functional of Orlicz non-doubling type.

Example B.2
.

Let
\psi_{0},\psi_{1}:{[0,\infty)}\to{[0,\infty)}
be two increasing non-negative convex functions such that
\psi_{1}/\psi_{0}
is non-decreasing. Let
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
with
\omega
satisfying (
2.4
). Then, the Lavrentiev phenomenon does not occur with the integrand
f_{\text{o}}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq\psi_{0}(|\xi|)+a(x,t)\psi_{1}(|\xi|)
.

Proof.

Let us take any
\cal{k_{2}}>0
and consider
x,t,\xi,{\varepsilon}
such that
{(f_{\text{o}})}^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(t,\xi)+|\xi|^{N}\leq{\cal{k}_{2}}{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}
. We get that
|\xi|\leq\min\left(\psi_{0}^{-1}({\cal{k}}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}),{\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1}\right)
. Fix any
y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})
and consider the function
C(\cdot)
coming from the definition of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
. If
\frac{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}{\psi_{1}(\psi_{0}^{-1}({\cal{k}}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}))}\leq\frac{\psi_{0}({\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1})}{\psi_{1}({\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1})}
, we use the inequality
{|\xi|\leq{\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1}}
and condition (
2.4
) to get

\frac{f_{\text{o}}(x,t,\xi)}{f_{\text{o}}(y,t,\xi)}\leq 1+C(t)+{C(t)}\omega({\varepsilon})\frac{\psi_{1}({\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1})}{\psi_{0}({\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1})}\leq 1+C(t)+{C(t)}\widetilde{C}(\cal{k}_{2})\,.

(B.6)

On the other hand, if
\frac{{\varepsilon}^{-N}}{\psi_{1}(\psi_{0}^{-1}({\cal{k}}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}))}\geq\frac{\psi_{0}({\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1})}{\psi_{1}({\cal{k}}_{2}^{1/N}{\varepsilon}^{-1})}
, we use the inequality
|\xi|\leq\psi_{0}^{-1}({\cal{k}}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N})
and condition (
2.4
) to get

\frac{f_{\text{o}}(x,t,\xi)}{f_{\text{o}}(y,t,\xi)}\leq 1+C(t)+{C(t)}\omega({\varepsilon})\frac{\psi_{1}(\psi_{0}^{-1}({\cal{k}}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}))}{{\cal{k}}_{2}{\varepsilon}^{-N}}\leq 1+C(t)+{C(t)}\widetilde{C}(\cal{k}_{2})\,.

(B.7)

From (
B.6
) and (
B.7
), we deduce that
f_{\text{o}}
satisfies (

H
iso

) under the condition
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}
with
\omega
satisfying (
2.4
). As
0\leq f(x,t,0)\leq\psi_{0}(0)+\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\psi_{2}(0)
, Theorem
5
applies.
∎

The example below illustrates how to exclude the Lavrentiev phenomenon in case when the Lagrangian is not convex with respect to the last variable, but is comparable to a convex one.

Example B.3

(Koch, Ruf, Schäffner
[
43
]
)
.

Let us define
f_{\text{D}}\coloneqq|\xi|^{p}+a(x,t)\exp(|\xi|^{q})
for any
p\geq 1,q>0
. Assume that
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega(\cdot,\cdot)}\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}})
with
\omega(s)\leq\exp(-s^{-{\varkappa}})
,
{\varkappa}>q\min(1,N/p)
. Then, there is no Lavrentiev gap for the functional (
1.2
) with the integrand
f_{\text{D}}
.

Proof.

In view of Proposition
A.1
and Theorem
5
, we shall find a function
f_{\text{W}}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
5
, and being comparable to
f_{\text{D}}
. Let
r_{*}\coloneqq q^{-1/q}
.
We define
f_{\text{W}}
as

f_{\text{W}}(x,t,\xi)\coloneqq\begin{cases}|\xi|^{p}+a(x,t)\exp(r_{*}^{q})r_{*}^{-1}|\xi|\,,&\text{ for }|\xi|\in[0,r_{*}]\,,\\
|\xi|^{p}+a(x,t)\exp(|\xi|^{q})\,,&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

The convexity of
f_{\text{W}}
with respect to
\xi
follows from the fact that the function
r\mapsto\exp(r^{q})
is convex on
[r_{*},\infty)
and its derivative at
r=r_{*}
is equal to
\exp(r_{*}^{q})r_{*}^{-1}
. It is also clear that
f_{\text{W}}\leq f_{\text{D}}\leq f_{\text{W}}+\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\exp(r^{q}_{*})

and also that
f_{\text{W}}
satisfies (
1.8
), as
f_{\text{W}}(x,t,0)\leq\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}
. We now aim at showing that
f_{\text{W}}
satisfies (

H
iso

). By similar computations to those in Example
B.2
, we only have to prove that
\omega
satisfies (
2.4
) with

\psi_{0}(s)=s^{p}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi_{1}(s)=\begin{cases}\exp(r_{*}^{q})r_{*}^{-1}s&\textrm{ if }s\leq r_{*}\,,\\
\exp(s^{q})&\textrm{ if }s\geq r_{*}\,.\end{cases}

We note that for any
L>0
and sufficiently small
s
, we have

\omega(s)\frac{\psi_{1}(\psi_{0}^{-1}(Ls^{-N}))}{s^{-N}}{\leq}s^{N}\exp(L^{q/p}s^{-Nq/p}-s^{-{\varkappa}})\,,\quad\omega(s)\frac{\psi_{1}(L^{1/N}s^{-1})}{\psi_{0}(L^{1/N}s^{-1})}{\leq}L^{-p/N}s^{p}\exp(L^{q/N}s^{-q}-s^{-{\varkappa}})\,.

As
{\varkappa}>q\min(1,N/p)
, at least one of the expressions above tends to
0
as
s
tends to
0
, and therefore, (
2.4
) is satisfied for small
s
. For larger
s
, (
2.4
) is clear as the left-hand side is bounded from above while the right-hand side is bounded from below by positive constants independent of
s
.
Hence, the function
f_{\text{W}}
satisfies (

H
iso

). From Theorem
5
and Proposition
A.1
, we can now infer the absence of the Lavrentiev phenomenon for the functional
u\mapsto\int_{\Omega}f_{\text{D}}(x,u,\nabla u){\,{\rm d}x}
. ∎

The last example illustrates the exclusion of the Lavrentiev gap in the doubling fully anisotropic setting.

Example B.4
.

Let
\psi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be a convex and non-decreasing function such that
\psi\in\Delta_{2}
and let
\upsilon:{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
be
C^{0,\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1]
. Then, the Lavrentiev phenomenon does not occur with the integrand
f_{\text{a}}(x,\xi)\coloneqq\psi(|\langle\upsilon(x),\xi\rangle|)+|\xi|^{N/\gamma}
.

Proof.

We shall show that
f
satisfies (

H

Δ
2

). Let
C_{\psi}\geq 1
be a doubling constant of
\psi
and
C_{\upsilon}
be a Hölder constant of
\upsilon
. Note that whenever
f^{-}_{B(x,{\varepsilon})}(\xi)\leq L{\varepsilon}^{-N}
, we have
|\xi|\leq L^{\gamma/N}{\varepsilon}^{-\gamma}
. Therefore, for every
y\in B(x,{\varepsilon})
, we have

\displaystyle f_{\text{a}}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\psi(|\langle\upsilon(x),\xi\rangle|)+|\xi|^{N/\gamma}\leq C_{\psi}\left(\psi(|\langle\upsilon(y),\xi\rangle|)+\psi(|\langle\upsilon(x)-\upsilon(y),\xi\rangle|){+1}\right)+|\xi|^{N/\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\psi}\left(\psi(|\langle\upsilon(y),\xi\rangle|)+\max_{s\leq C_{\upsilon}L^{\gamma/N}}\psi(s){+1}\right)+|\xi|^{N/\gamma}\leq C_{\psi}\left(1+\max_{s\leq C_{\upsilon}L^{\gamma/N}}\psi(s)\right)(f(y,\xi)+1)\,,

which is (

H

Δ
2

) with
C_{L}=C_{\psi}\left(1+\max_{s\leq C_{\upsilon}L^{\gamma/N}}\psi(s)\right)
. As additionally
f_{\text{a}}(x,0)={\psi(0)}
, Theorem
2
applies.

∎
